Chapter 1
Grothendieck-Katz conjecture

Man sollte weniger danach streben, die Grenzen der mathematischen Wissenschaften zu erweitern, als vielmehr danach,
den bereits vorhandenen Stoff aus umfassenderen Gesichtspunkten zu betrachten,
E. Study
, (see the preface of
[
30
]
).

Abstract:
In this article we prove that linear differential equations with only algebraic solutions
have zero
m
-curvature modulo
p^{k}
for all except a finite number of primes
p
and all
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with

{\rm ord}_{p}m!\geq k
. This provides us with a reformulation of Grothendieck-Katz conjecture with stronger hypothesis.

1.1
Introduction

Let us consider a linear differential equation:

(1.1)

\frac{\partial y}{\partial z}={\sf A}(z)y,

where
{\sf A}={\sf A}(z)
is an
{\sf n}\times{\sf n}
matrix with entries which are rational functions in
z
and coefficients in
\mathbb{C}
. We are looking for holomorphic solutions of this differential equation. These are
{\sf n}\times 1
matrices
y
whose entries are holomorphic functions in an open set
U
in
\mathbb{C}
. Let
\Delta\in\mathbb{C}[z]
be the common denominator of the entries of
{\sf A}
, and so the entries of
\Delta{\sf A}
are in
\mathbb{C}[z]
. We take
{\mathfrak{R}}\subset\mathbb{C}
any finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra generated by the coefficients of
\Delta
and
\Delta{\sf A}
.
It is easy to see that
y^{(n)}={\sf A}_{n}y
, where
{\sf A}_{n}
are recursively computed by

{\sf A}_{1}={\sf A},\ \ {\sf A}_{n+1}=\frac{\partial{\sf A}_{n}}{\partial z}+{\sf A}_{n}{\sf A}.

We can easily check that
\Delta^{n}{\sf A}_{n}
has entries in
{\mathfrak{R}}[z]
.

Theorem 1.1
.

If all the entries of solutions of
\frac{\partial y}{\partial z}={\sf A}y
are algebraic functions over
\mathbb{C}(z)
, that is, they are in the algebraic closure
\overline{\mathbb{C}(z)}
of
\mathbb{C}(z)
then there is
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
such that

(1.2)

\hbox{ entries of }\frac{(N\Delta)^{m}\cdot{\sf A}_{m}}{m!}\ \in\ {\mathfrak{R}}[z],\ \ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}.

In particular, for all primes
p
and
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\rm ord}_{p}m!\geq k
we have
{\sf A}_{m}\equiv_{p^{k}}0
, that is,
{\sf A}_{m}
is zero in the ring
{\mathfrak{R}}[z,\frac{1}{N\Delta}]/p^{k}{\mathfrak{R}}[z,\frac{1}{N\Delta}]
.

If
{\mathfrak{R}}
is a ring of integers of a number field, in the above theorem we can take
N\in\mathbb{N}
, see
Theorem

1.2
.
This is natural as in the left hand side of (
1.2
) only integers are inverted and not
an element of
{\mathfrak{R}}-\mathbb{Z}
. The only reason that we assume that
{\mathfrak{R}}
is the ring of integers of a number field is
Proposition

1.5
.
The conclusion in the second part of
Theorem

1.1
is equivalent to
\Delta^{m}{\sf A}_{m}\equiv_{p^{k}}0
which means
\Delta^{m}{\sf A}_{m}=0
in the ring

{\mathfrak{R}}[z,\frac{1}{N}]/p^{k}{\mathfrak{R}}[z,\frac{1}{N}]
.
We may expect that the converse of
Theorem

1.1
holds.

Conjecture 1.1
.

For a linear differential equation (
1.1
) if there is
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
such that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
the entries of

\frac{(N\Delta)^{m}{\sf A}_{m}}{m!}
are in
{\mathfrak{R}}[z]

then its solutions are algebraic functions over
\mathbb{C}(z)
.

It is easy to verify that
{\rm ord}_{p}m!\leq\frac{m}{p-1}
and so
m
in the above conjecture satisfies
m\geq(p-1)k
.

For a given prime and
k\in\mathbb{N}
let
m_{p,k}
be the smallest
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
{\rm ord}_{p}m!\geq k
. We have
m_{p,1}=p,\ m_{p,2}=2p
and
(p-1)k\leq m_{p,k}\leq pk
and it is not hard to see that the conclusion of
Theorem

1.1
, and hence the hypothesis of
Conjecture

1.1
, is equivalent to the same statement with
m=m_{p,k}
.

For a prime number
p
, the matrix
{\sf A}_{p}

considered as a matrix with entries in
{\mathfrak{R}}[z,\frac{1}{\Delta}]/p{\mathfrak{R}}[z,\frac{1}{\Delta}]
is usually called the
p
-curvature of
{\sf A}
(there is no
N
in the denominator).
The statement in the second part of
Theorem

1.1
for
k=1
and
m=p
and with
N\in\mathbb{N}
(see
Theorem

1.2
) says that the
p
-curvature vanishes for all except a finite number of primes. Its converse, which is a stronger version of
Conjecture

1.1
, is known as Grothendieck-Katz (p-curvature) conjecture.
It was suggested by A. Grothendieck and popularized by N. Katz in
[
46
,
47
]
.
Note that there is a finite number of primes which are invertible in
{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N}]
and the second statement
in the main theorem becomes empty for these primes. Therefore, the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture is: If for all but a finite number of primes the
p
-curvature of
y^{\prime}={\sf A}y
vanishes then all its solutions are algebraic.

Proposition 1.1
.

If Grothendieck-Katz conjecture is true for
y^{\prime}={\sf A}y
then the vanishing of
p
-curvature
for all except a finite number of primes
p
implies the vanishing of
{\sf A}_{m_{p,k}}
modulo
p^{k}
for all except a finite number of primes
p
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

A random search, without no strategy, for finding a counterexample to the conclusion of
Proposition

1.1
for single prime is also not easy. That is, for for many differential equations the conclusion of (
1.1
) is true. However, we were able to find:

Proposition 1.2
.

For the Lamé differential equation

(4z^{3}-1)\frac{d^{2}y}{dz^{2}}+6z^{2}\frac{dy}{dz}-\frac{7}{36}zy=0

we have
{\sf A}_{5}\equiv_{5}0
, however,
{\sf A}_{25}\not\equiv_{5^{6}}0
.

1
1

1

For the computer code which proves this proposition see
Proposition

2.3
.

Therefore, any proof of the conclusion of
Proposition

1.1
without assuming the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture, must use a mixed prime argument.
João Pedro dos Santos took my attention to the articles
[
59
]
in which the authors right at the end of the article makes the following conjecture: Let
F
be a number field and
M
be a differential module over
F(z)
.
If for all but a finite number of primes
p
, the iterative differential module with respect to
p
exists and has a finite differential Galois group
G
, then the differential Galois group of
M
is isomorphic to
G
. The property (
1.2
) implies the existence of such an iterative differential module and so
Conjecture

1.1
implies this conjecture. A similar conjecture has been stated in
[
33
]
using the language of
D
-modules: Let
X/\mathbb{C}
be a smooth projective variety over
\mathbb{C}
and
M=(E,\nabla)
be a vector bundle with an integrable connection on
X
. If there is a dense open subscheme

U\subset{\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}})
such that for all closed points
s\in U
,
M_{s}
underlies a
D_{X_{s}}
-module then
M
has finite monodromy. Another similar conjecture has been advertised by M. Kontsevich in
[
55
]
. Hopefully the reader will be convinced that the hypothesis of conjectures in
[
59
,
33
,
55
]
are hard to check whereas (
1.2
) can be even implemented in a computer and it is characteristic zero statement. Moreover, we would like to emphasize that more divisibility properties are missing in the hypothesis of Grothendieck-Katz conjecture. Y. André in a personal communication took my attention to the works
[
8
,
28
,
5
]
. The following is written based on his comments.
The
p
-adic radius of convergence of a full set of solutions of a linear differential equation is
\geq p^{-1/(p-1)}
, see
[
8
]
, and if this is strict, for instance if the
p
-curvature is nilpotent, then
\frac{{\sf A}_{m}(z)}{m!}
becomes divisible by very high powers of
p
for large
m
.
The vanishing of
p
-curvatures for a fixed
p
implies that the generic radius
R_{p}
of convergence satisfies
R_{p}>p^{-1/(p-1)}
and the
\tau
-invariant is
0
in this case, see
[
5
, Section 5]
.
H. Esnault informed me about
[
32
, Proposition 8.1, 8.2]
in which the authors verify the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture for two partial cases. W. Zudilin has called phenomenon like (
1.2
) the cancellation of factorials, see
[
96
]
. He took my attention to
[
22
]
in which the interest in algebraic solutions of rank one systems is attributed to Abel.

1.2
Algebraic functions

The following proposition for
{\mathfrak{R}}=\mathbb{Z}
is due to G. Eisenstein, see
[
57
]
and the references therein.
In the following
y(z)
is a holomorphic function
y:U\to\mathbb{C}
defined in some connected open subset
U
of
\mathbb{C}
.

Proposition 1.3

(G. Eisenstein)
.

Let
y(z)
be an algebraic function and
z_{0}
be in its definition domain. We have

1.

The Taylor series of
y
at
z_{0}\in U
has coefficients in a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-subalgebra of
\mathbb{C}
.

2.

More precisely, if the polynomial equation
P(z,y(z))=0
is defined over a ring
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
z_{0},y(z_{0})\in{\mathfrak{R}}
then there is
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
such that the Taylor series of
y(Nz+z_{0})
at
z=0
has coefficients in
{\mathfrak{R}}
, and hence
y(z)\in{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N}][[z-z_{0}]]
.

3.

For
{\mathfrak{R}},z_{0},y(z_{0})
as in the previous item there is
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
such that

\frac{N^{m}y^{(m)}(z_{0})}{m!}\in{\mathfrak{R}},\ \ \ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

It is clear that 2 and 3 are equivalent and that 1 is a particular case of 2. Therefore, we only prove 2.
We write the Taylor series
y(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}y_{i}\cdot(z-z_{0})^{i}
of
y(z)
at
z=z_{0}
, where
y_{i}
’s are unknown coefficients and substitute in
P(z,y(z))=0
. Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be the
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra generated by coefficients of
P
,
z_{0}
and
y_{0}:=y(z_{0})
. Let also
\Delta:=\frac{\partial P}{\partial y}(z_{0},y_{0})
.
Computing the coefficient of
(z-z_{0})^{n}
we get a recursion of type

\Delta y_{n}=\hbox{ a polynomial of degree $\leq n$ in $y_{i},\ 1\leq i<n$, with coefficients in ${\mathfrak{R}}$},

for instance
\Delta y_{1}=-\frac{\partial P}{\partial z}(z_{0},y_{0})\in{\mathfrak{R}}
.
This implies that
y_{n}
lies in the finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{\Delta}]
. More precisely, by induction we can show that
y_{n}
has a pole order at most
2n-1
at
\Delta
. For
n=1
this is easy. If this is true for all
m<n
then
\Delta y_{n}
is a sum of monomials
y_{i_{1}}y_{i_{2}}\cdots y_{i_{k}}
with
i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{k}\leq n
and
1\leq i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}<n
and coefficients in
{\mathfrak{R}}
. If
k=1
then
2i_{1}-1\leq 2n-2
and we are done. If
k\geq 2
then
2i_{1}-1+2i_{2}-1\cdots+2i_{k}-1\leq 2n-k\leq 2n-2
and we are done again.
For the second part of the proposition we put
N:=\Delta^{2}
.
∎

Remark 1.1
.

If for a ring
{\mathfrak{R}}\subset\mathbb{C}
, the Taylor series
y(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}y_{i}\cdot(z-z_{0})^{i}\in{\mathfrak{R}}[[z-z_{0}]]
is algebraic over
\mathbb{C}(z)
then
there is a polynomial
P\in{\mathfrak{R}}[z,y]
such that
P(z,y(z))=0
. In order to see this we first take
P\in\mathbb{C}[z,y]
and regard the coefficients
P_{i}
of
P
as unknowns. The equalities derived from
P(z,\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}y_{i}\cdot(z-z_{0})^{i})=0
are linear equations in
P_{i}
and with coefficients in
{\mathfrak{R}}
. They have solutions in
\mathbb{C}
and so they have solutions in
{\mathfrak{R}}
.

Let us now consider the differential equation
y^{\prime}={\sf A}y
.

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
be a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-subalgebra of
\mathbb{C}
containing the coefficients

{\sf A}
,
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\frac{1}{\Delta(z_{0})}
and the entries of
y_{0}=[y_{0,1},y_{0,2},\cdots,y_{0,n}]^{{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{r}}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\sf n}
.
A solution of
y^{\prime}={\sf A}y
with the initial condition
y(z_{0})=y_{0}
is a formal power series with
coefficients in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\check{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
:

y(z)\in\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[[(z-z_{0})]].

Proof.

We
can find recursively a unique formal solution
y=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}y_{n}(z-z_{0})^{n}\in\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[[(z-z_{0})]]^{\sf n}
. The recursion is given by

ny_{n-1}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}{\sf A}_{n-1-i}y_{i},

where we have written the Taylor series
{\sf A}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{\sf A}_{i}(z-z_{0})^{i}
. Since the entries of
{\sf A}
are rational functions with coefficients in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
, the entries of
{\sf A}_{i}
are in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
. This follows from the fact that for a polynomial
P\in\check{\mathfrak{R}}[z]
we have:

\frac{P(z)}{\Delta(z)}=\frac{P(z)}{\Delta(z_{0})}\frac{1}{1-(1-\frac{\Delta(z)}{\Delta(z_{0})})}=\frac{P(z)}{\Delta(z_{0})}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{\Delta(z)}{\Delta(z_{0})}\right)^{i}.

∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be as
Section

1.1
, that is,
{\mathfrak{R}}
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-subalgebra of
\mathbb{C}
containing the coefficients of
\Delta
and the coefficients of the polynomials in
\Delta(z){\sf A}
. By
Proposition

1.4
we have a
{\sf n}\times{\sf n}
matrix
Y(z)
whose entries
are formal power series in
z-z_{0}
and with coefficients in the ring
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[z_{0},\frac{1}{\Delta(z_{0})}]
,
Y(z_{0})
is the identity matrix and

dY={\sf A}Y
. This is called the fundamental system at
z=z_{0}
.
From another side we know that the entries of
Y
are algebraic, and so by
Remark

1.1
we can assume that the corresponding polynomials are defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[z_{0},\frac{1}{\Delta(z_{0})}]
. A multiplication by a power of
\Delta(z_{0})
and possibly an integer,
we can assume that such polynomials are defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}[z_{0}]
. Now by
Proposition

1.3
we know that the coefficients of the Taylor series of
Y
are in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}:={\mathfrak{R}}[z_{0},\frac{1}{N}]
for some
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}[z_{0}]
. This
N
is the product of all
N
’s attached to the polynomial equation of each entry of
Y
.
Since for any
f=(z-z_{0})^{n}
we have

f^{(m)}=\binom{n}{m}m!(z-z_{0})^{n-m},\

we conclude that
Y^{(m)}=0
in
(\check{\mathfrak{R}}/m!\check{\mathfrak{R}})[[z-z_{0}]]
, and hence

(1.3)

{\sf A}_{m}Y=0\hbox{ in }(\check{\mathfrak{R}}/m!\check{\mathfrak{R}})[[z-z_{0}]].

From now on take
z_{0}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
and hence
\check{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N}]
.
The number
N
in the announcement of theorem is exactly the number
N
that we have obtained from Eisenstein theorem. We have
\check{\mathfrak{R}}/m!\check{\mathfrak{R}}=0
if and only if

(1.4)

m!|N^{d},\hbox{ in ${\mathfrak{R}}$ for some $d\in\mathbb{N}$}.

In this case (
1.2
) is automatic.
Therefore, we assume that
\check{\mathfrak{R}}/m!\check{\mathfrak{R}}\not=0
, and in particular
1\not=0
in this ring. In this ring
Y=I_{{\sf n}\times{\sf n}}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}y_{i}(z-z_{0})^{i}
is invertible, that is the entries of
Y^{-1}
are formal power series
in
(z-z_{0})
and coefficients in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}/m!\check{\mathfrak{R}}
. This together with (
1.3
) imply that the Taylor series of
{\sf A}_{m}(z)
, and hence
\Delta(z)^{m}{\sf A}_{m}(z)
, at
z=z_{0}
,
is zero in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}/m!\check{\mathfrak{R}}[[z-z_{0}]]
. But
R(z):=\Delta(z)^{m}{\sf A}_{m}(z)
is a matrix of polynomials, and hence, its entries are in

m!\check{\mathfrak{R}}[z]
. This finishes the proof of the fact that the entries of
\frac{\Delta^{m}\cdot{\sf A}_{m}}{m!}
are
{\mathfrak{R}}[z,\frac{1}{N}]
, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
which is slightly weaker than (
1.2
). The proof of this stronger statement is similar.
∎

1.3
Bad primes

The number
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
in
Theorem

1.1
is the main responsible for the finite number of exceptional primes in the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture. In this section we describe these primes.

2
2

2

For examples of bad primes of Lamé equations see
Chapter

2
.

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be a ring of integers of a number field and
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
.
There is a finite number of primes
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{s}
(depending only on
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
N
) such that the following property holds: for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
coprime with all
p_{i}
’s and
all
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
if
m|N^{d}M
in
{\mathfrak{R}}
then
m|M
.
In particular, such a property hold for all except a finite number of prime numbers
m
.

Proof.

If
{\mathfrak{R}}=\mathbb{Z}
then this proposition follows from the unique factorization. The bad primes are those dividing
N
. Let us now consider a number field
{\mathfrak{k}}
and its ring of integers
{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. By unique factorization theorem for ideals in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
, we know that there are only a finite number of prime ideals in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
containing
N{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. Let
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}
be the list of characteristics of the corresponding residue fields. Take an arbitrary
m\in\mathbb{Z}
coprime with
p_{i}
’s and such that
m|N^{d}M
in
{\mathfrak{R}}
.
By construction
m
and
N^{d}
are coprime, that is, there is no prime ideal containing both
m{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
and
N^{d}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. Therefore,
m{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}+N^{d}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}={\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. In particular, there is
a,b\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
such that
ma+N^{d}b=1
. Multiplying this equality with
M
we conclude that
M\in m{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
.
∎

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be a ring of integers of a number field. In
Theorem

1.1
we can take
N\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We would like to replace
N
with another one
\tilde{N}
in
\mathbb{N}
.
Let
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{s}
be the list of primes in
Proposition

1.5
attached to
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
N
and define
\tilde{N}:=p_{1}p_{2}\cdots p_{s}
. In general, the invertiblity of
N
in
{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N}]
does not imply the invertiblity of
p_{i}
’s. We claim that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
the entries of
\frac{{\sf A}_{m}}{m!}
are in

{\mathfrak{R}}[z,\frac{1}{\tilde{N}\Delta}]
.
A coefficient
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}
of
\Delta^{m}{\sf A}_{m}
satisfies
M=m!\frac{S}{N^{d}}
for some
S\in{\mathfrak{R}}
and

d\in\mathbb{N}
, and so
m!\mid MN^{n}
in
{\mathfrak{R}}
. We write
m!=N_{1}N_{2}
, where
N_{1}
contains only the primes
p_{i}
, and
N_{2}
is free of
p_{i}
’s. By

Proposition

1.5
we have
N_{2}|M
in
{\mathfrak{R}}
, and hence,
\frac{M}{m!}\in{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N_{1}}]\subset{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{\tilde{N}}]
.

∎

1.4
Algebraicity of a single solution

In the theory of holomorphic foliations one is also interested in the algebraicity of the leaf passing through a given point. In the framework of linear differential equations this is:

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume that
{\mathfrak{R}}
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-subalgebra of
\mathbb{C}
,
z_{0}\in{\mathfrak{R}},\ \Delta(z_{0})\not=0
and
y_{0}\in{\mathfrak{R}}^{n}
.
If a solution of
\frac{\partial y}{\partial z}={\sf A}y
with the initial value
y(z_{0})=y_{0}
is algebraic then there is
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
such that

(1.5)

\hbox{ entries of }\frac{N^{m}{\sf A}_{m}(z_{0})y_{0}}{m!}\ \in\ {\mathfrak{R}},\ \ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}.

In other words, for all primes
p
and
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\rm ord}_{p}m!\geq k
we have
{\sf A}_{m}(z_{0})y_{0}\equiv_{p^{k}}0
, that is,
{\sf A}_{m}(z_{0})y_{0}
is zero in the ring
{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N}]/p^{k}{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N}]
.

The proof is the same as the proof of
Theorem

1.1
. In the last step (
1.3
), instead of
Y
we use
y
itself and evaluate it in
z=z_{0}
. We have also
\Delta(z_{0})\in{\mathfrak{R}}
which is absorbed by
N
, that is, instead of
{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N\Delta(z_{0})}]
we have written
{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N}]
.
It is already evident what is the analog of
Conjecture

1.1
in this framework: If for a solution
y(z)
of

\frac{\partial y}{\partial z}={\sf A}y
with
y(z_{0})=y_{0}
we have (
1.5
) then
y(z)
must be algebraic.

3
3

3

Daniel Litt and Joshua Lam kindly reminded me that this conjecture is related to a conjecture of
Y. André and G. Christol in
[
5
, Remarque 5.3.2]
. In
[
12
, Conjecture 1.12]
this is actually attributed to them.

The analog of
Grothendieck-Katz conjecture is: if for all but a finite number of primes
p
,
{\sf A}_{p}(z_{0})y_{0}
vanishes modulo
p
then
y(z)
is algebraic.

4
4

4

This statement is false. See
Remark

3.2
.

1.5
Integral functions

Our main goal in the present section is to define integral functions, and prove that algebraic functions are integral (
Proposition

1.7
) and conjecture that this is an if and only if statement. In the following, for
a\in\mathbb{C}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we will use the notation

(a)_{n}:=a(a-1)\cdots(a-n+1),\ \ [a]_{n}:=\frac{(a)_{n}}{n!}.

Moreover, for a holomorphic function
y:U\to\mathbb{C}
defined in an open set
U
of
\mathbb{C}
with the coordinate
z
, by
y^{(n)}
we mean its
n
-th derivative with respect to
z
and

y^{[n]}:=\frac{y^{(n)}(z)}{n!}.

With this notation we have

(1.6)

\displaystyle(y_{1}y_{1})^{[n]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}y_{1}^{[i]}y_{2}^{[n-i]},

(1.7)

\displaystyle(y^{-1})^{[n]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-y^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}y^{[n-i]}(y^{-1})^{[i]},

(1.8)

\displaystyle(y^{m})^{[n]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{m}=n}y^{[i_{1}]}y^{[i_{2}]}\cdots y^{[i_{m}]},

(1.9)

\displaystyle(y^{[n]})^{[m]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\binom{n+m}{n}y^{[n+m]},

(1.10)

\displaystyle(z^{a})^{[n]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[a]_{n}z^{a-n},\ \ \hbox{In particular }(z^{-1})^{[n]}=(-1)^{n}z^{-n-1}.

Definition 1.1
.

A finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
\mathbb{Z}[x]=\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N}]
generated by holomorphic functions
x_{i}:U\to\mathbb{C}
is called integral if it is closed under

(1.11)

\mathbb{Z}[x]\to\mathbb{Z}[x],\ \ P\mapsto P^{[n]},

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. A holomorphic function
y:U\to\mathbb{C}
is called integral (of length
\leq N
) if it belongs to some integral
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
\mathbb{Z}[x]
generated by at most
N
elements. We call
\mathbb{Z}[x]
the associated
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra.

Remark 1.2
.

The operator (
1.11
) is
\mathbb{Z}
-linear, and so in order to show that
\mathbb{Z}[x]
is closed under (
1.11
), we need to show it for a monomial in
x
.
From (
1.6
) it follows that in order to verify that
\mathbb{Z}[x]
is an integral algebra it is enough to prove that
x_{i}^{[n]}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and for a set of generators
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N})
of
\mathbb{Z}[x]
.

Proposition 1.6
.

The set of integral functions is a field.

Proof.

If
\mathbb{Z}[x]
and
\mathbb{Z}[\tilde{x}]
are integral
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra, then by
Remark

1.2
,
\mathbb{Z}[x,\tilde{x}]
is also integral. This implies that if
y
and
\tilde{y}
are two integral functions with the associated
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras
\mathbb{Z}[x]
and
\mathbb{Z}[\tilde{x}]
then

y+\tilde{y},\ y\tilde{y}
are also integral with the associated
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
\mathbb{Z}[x,\tilde{x}]
.
For an integral function
y\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
,
\mathbb{Z}[y^{-1},x]
is also an integral algebra. This follows from (
1.7
) and
Remark

1.2
.
∎

Proposition 1.7
.

Algebraic functions are integrals.

Proof.

Let
y(z)
be an algebraic function, that is, there is a polynomial
P(z,y)=\sum_{i=0}^{m}p_{i}(z)y^{i}\in\mathbb{C}[z,y]
such
P(z,y(z))=0
. Let
d:={\rm max}\{\deg(p_{i}),\ i=0,1,\ldots,m\}
. We take the
k
-th derivative of
P(z,y(z))=0
with
k\geq d+1
and we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{k}p_{i}^{[j]}(y^{i})^{[k-j]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{d}p_{i}^{[j]}(y^{i})^{[k-j]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{d}\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{i}=k-j}p_{i}^{[j]}y^{[i_{1}]}y^{[i_{2}]}\cdots y^{[i_{i}]}.

The largest derivative in this equality is in the term
\frac{\partial P}{\partial y}y^{[k]}
. This means that if we invert
\frac{\partial P}{\partial y}
then we can write
y^{[k]}
as linear combination of the previous derivatives. The conclusion is

(1.12)

y^{[k]}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]:=\mathbb{Z}\left[\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial y}\right)^{-1},\ z,{\rm coef}(P),\ y^{[i]},\ \ \ i=0,1,\ldots,d\right],\ \ k\geq d+1,

where
{\rm coef}(P)
is the list of coefficients of
P
(they might be transcendendtal numbers).
By
Remark

1.2
we need to show
P^{[n]}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
for generators of
\mathbb{Z}[x]
.
For
P=\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial y}\right)^{-1}
this follow from (
1.7
). For
P=y^{[n]}
this follows from (
1.9
) and (
1.12
).
∎

Remark 1.3
.

It is clear from (
1.12
) the the length of an algebraic function
y
is less that or equal

N:=d+3+\hbox{number of monomials in $P$ with non-zero coefficients}-1.

If
P
is defined over
\mathbb{Z}
then
N=d+3
. Precise formulas can be obtained by using the ring of definition of
P
.

Remark 1.4
.

Let
y(z)
be an integral function and
z_{0}
be in its domain of definition.
The Taylor series of
y
at
z_{0}
has coefficients in a finitely generated ring
{\mathfrak{R}}
, that is,

y(z)\in{\mathfrak{R}}[[(z-z_{0})]]
. A weaker version of
Proposition

1.3
is this statement
for algebraic functions.

We believe that the converse of
Proposition

1.7
is also true.

Conjecture 1.2
.

Integral functions are algebraic.

For a linear differential equations with only algebraic solutions,
Theorem

1.1
gives us an integral algebra for its solutions of smaller length than the one given by
Proposition

1.7
. For an algebraic solution
y=[y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}]^{{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{r}}}
of
y^{\prime}={\sf A}(z)y
, we have

\frac{y^{(m)}}{m!}=\frac{{\sf A}_{m}}{m!}y
, and so

(1.13)

\hbox{ entries of }\frac{y^{(m)}}{m!}\ \in\ {\mathfrak{R}}[z,\frac{1}{N\Delta},y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}],\ \ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}.

If the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture is true then similar to
Proposition

1.1
we must have:

Conjecture 1.3
.

If the
p
-curvature of
y^{\prime}={\sf A}(z)y
is zero for almost all prime then the entries of
y
are integral.

Remark 1.5
.

Let us consider a differential equations
y^{\prime}={\sf A}y
satisfying the hypothesis of
Conjecture

1.1
. It is a straightforward computation to show that

U=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}{\sf A}_{k}(z)}{k!}(z-z_{0})^{k},

with
U(0)=
the identity matrix, satisfies the differential equation
U^{\prime}=-U{\sf A}
, and hence,
Y:=U^{-1}
is a fundamental matrix of
Y^{\prime}={\sf A}Y
, see
[
12
, Remark 3.20]
. We can consider
U
and
U^{-1}
as formal power series in
(z-z_{0})
and the coefficients are in the ring
{\mathfrak{R}}[z_{0},\frac{1}{N\Delta(z_{0})}]
. Note that we have also

U=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{{\sf A}_{k}(z_{0})}{k!}(z-z_{0})^{k},

which follows again by the hypothesis of
Conjecture

1.1
and the formula for Taylor series.

1.6
An attempt

The author’s impression is that
Conjecture

1.3
which is a consequence of Grothendieck-Katz conjecture might be false. In order to transfer this feeling to the reader, in this section, we attempt to translate the vanishing of
p
-curvatures into the
p
-integrality of its solutions. T
Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be the
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra generated by the coefficients of
{\sf A}
and
\check{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathfrak{R}}[z_{0},\frac{1}{\Delta(z_{0})},y_{0}]
for
y_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{\sf n}
,
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
which is not a pole of
{\sf A}
.

Proposition 1.8
.

If the
p
-curvature of
y^{\prime}={\sf A}y
is zero then we have a formal power series
\check{y}\in\check{\mathfrak{R}}[[z-z_{0}]]
with
\check{y}=y_{0}
such that
\check{y}^{\prime}={\sf A}\check{y}
in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}/p\check{\mathfrak{R}}
.

Proof.

We give two proofs of this statement. The first proof is well-known, see
[
12
, Remark 3.20]
, and it is less intuitive. It is a mere computation to see that if
{\sf A}_{p}=0
in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}/p\check{\mathfrak{R}}
then the sum
U=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}(z-z_{0})^{k}{\sf A}_{k}
with
U(0)=
identity matrix, satisfies the differential equation
U^{\prime}=-U{\sf A}
, and hence,
U^{{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{r}}}
is the solution of the dual differential equation
y^{\prime}=-{\sf A}^{{{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{r}}}}y
. It turns out that
U^{-1}
is a fundamental matrix of
y^{\prime}={\sf A}y
.

The second proof is more natural and it is a simplification and adaptation of
[
81
, Theorem 2]
into our context.
Let
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}:={\mathfrak{R}}/p{\mathfrak{R}}
.
We consider the larger ring
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}[z,y]
and the derivation

{\sf v}:{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}[z,y]\to{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}[z,y],\ \ {\sf v}(z)=1,\ {\sf v}(y)={\sf A}(z)y,

and let

{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v}):=\{f\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}[z,y]\ \Big|{\sf v}(f)=0\}.

We have clearly
f^{p}\subset{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})
for all
f\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}[z,y]
.
The hypothesis on
p
-curvature is equivalent to the fact that
{\sf v}^{p}(f)=0
for all
f
.
Let
{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})[z]
be the polynomial ring in
z
and coefficients in
{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})
.
Since
z^{p}\in{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})
such polynomials can be taken of degree
\leq p-1
in
z
.
We claim that

{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}[z,y]={\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})[z].

If not, there is a
f\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}[z,y]
such that
f\not\in{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})[z]
.
Since
{\sf v}^{p}f=0
, for some
1\leq e\leq p-1
we have

g={\sf v}^{e}f\not\in{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})[z]
but
{\sf v}g\in{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})[z]
.
Let us write

{\sf v}g=a_{0}+a_{1}z+a_{2}z^{2}+\cdots+a_{p-1}z^{p-1},\ \ a_{i}\in{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v}).

By our hypothesis
{\sf v}(a_{i})=0
, and so,
0={\sf v}^{p-1}{\sf v}g=(p-1)!a_{p-1}
. Now we use the fact that
p
is a prime, and hence
a_{p-1}=0
in
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}
.
Therefore, we can find
\tilde{g}=a_{0}z+\frac{a_{1}}{2}z^{2}+\cdots+\frac{a_{p-2}}{p-1}\in{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})[z]
such that
{\sf v}g={\sf v}\tilde{g}
. Note that
\frac{1}{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
is any representative for the inverse of
i
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and hence
\frac{1}{i}i-1\in p\mathbb{Z}
, and it is zero in
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}
. Once again the construction of
g
works only for
p
prime.
We conclude that that
g-\tilde{g}\in{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})[z]
, and so,
g\in{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})[z]
which is a contradiction.

Let us take
y_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{\sf n}
and
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
which is not a pole of
{\sf A}
. From now on we use the larger ring
\check{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathfrak{R}}[y_{0},z_{0},\frac{1}{\Delta(z_{0})}]
and the equalities are in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}:=\check{\mathfrak{R}}/p\check{\mathfrak{R}}
.
We write

y-y_{0}=a_{0}+a_{1}(z-z_{0})+a_{2}(z-z_{0})^{2}+\cdots+a_{p-1}(z-z_{0})^{p-1},\ \ a_{i}\in{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})^{n}.

This equality implies that the algebraic variety
a_{0}(z,y)=0
in
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}}
contains the point
(y,z)=(y_{0},z_{0})
. By definition of
{\rm ker}_{p}({\sf v})
, it is tangent to the vector field
{\sf v}
.
We get the map

Y:\mathbb{A}^{{\sf n}+1}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}}\to\mathbb{A}^{{\sf n}+1}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}},\ \ \ Y(z,y)=(z,w):=(z,a_{0}(z,y)),\ Y(z_{0},y_{0})=(z_{0},0),

whose derivative at
(z_{0},y_{0})
has the determinant
1
.
By inverse function theorem over a ring, see
Remark

1.6
,
we have power series

\check{y}\in\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}[[z-z_{0},w]]
such that

a_{0}(z,\check{y})=w
. We redefine
\check{y}
to be
\check{y}
restricted to
w=0
and get
\check{y}\in\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}[[z-z_{0}]]
. Therefore,
a_{0}(z,\check{y})=0
. This together with
{\sf v}(a_{0})=0
, imply that

\frac{\partial\check{y}}{\partial z}={\sf A}(z)\check{y},\ \ \check{y}(z_{0})=y_{0},

this is because these equalities imply
\frac{\partial a_{0}}{\partial z}+\frac{\partial a_{0}}{\partial y}\frac{\partial\check{y}}{\partial z}=0
and
\frac{\partial a_{0}}{\partial z}+\frac{\partial a_{0}}{\partial y}{\sf A}(z)\check{y}=0
.
∎

Remark 1.6
.

The inverse function theorem and implicit function theorem are true over an arbitrary ring
{\mathfrak{R}}
as follows. Let
A=\mathbb{A}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}^{n}={\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n}])
and
{t}=(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{n}),\ {t}_{i}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
A
. Let also
{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm for},{t}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm hol},{t}}
) be the ring of formal (resp. convergent for some embedding
{\mathfrak{R}}\subset\mathbb{C}
) power series
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}a_{i}(z-t)^{i}
.

1.

Let
F=(F_{1},F_{2},\ldots,F_{n})
with
F_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm hol},{t}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm for},{t}}
). If
\det[\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial z_{j}}(0)]
is invertible in
{\mathfrak{R}}
then there is
G=(G_{1},G_{2},\ldots,G_{n})
, with
G_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm hol},{t}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm for},{t}}
) such that
F\circ G=G\circ F=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n})
.

2.

Let
F=(F_{1},F_{2},\ldots,F_{m})
with
F_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm hol},{t}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm for},{t}}
). If the rows of
[\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial z_{j}}(0)]
can be completed to a basis of
{\mathfrak{R}}^{n}
then there is
G=(G_{1},G_{2},\ldots,G_{n-m})
, with
G_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{B^{\rm hol},0}
(resp.
{\mathcal{O}}_{B^{\rm for},{t}}
) and

B=\mathbb{A}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}^{n-m}={\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n-m}])

such that
[\frac{\partial G_{i}}{\partial z_{j}}(0)]
is invertible in
{\mathfrak{R}}
and

F(\check{z},G(\check{z}))=0,

where
\check{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n-m})
.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be a finitely generated subring of
\mathbb{C}
such that a primes number
p
is not invertible in
{\mathfrak{R}}
. We say that a formal power series is
p
-integral (relative to
{\mathfrak{R}}
) if all its coefficients are in
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, where
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
is the ring of rational numbers whose denominator is not divisable by
p
. Note that
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\frac{1}{p}]=\mathbb{Q}
.

The following is an immediate consequence of the definition.

Proposition 1.9
.

Consider a differential equation
y^{\prime}={\sf A}(z)y
defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. A solution of this differential equation with
y(z_{0})=y_{0},\ z_{0}\in{\mathfrak{R}},\ y_{0}\in{\mathfrak{R}}^{n}
, is
p
-integral if

\frac{{\sf A}_{m}(z_{0})y_{0}}{m!}\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}_{p},\ \ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}.

In
Theorem

1.3
we have proved that for an algebraic solution of a differential equation we have always
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
such that it is
p
-integral for all primes except a finite number and relative to the ring
{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N}]
.

Definition 1.3
.

We say that a differential equation
y^{\prime}={\sf A}(z)y
defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
is
p
-integral (relative to
{\mathfrak{R}}
) if

\frac{\Delta(z)^{m}{\sf A}_{m}(z)}{m!}\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[z],\ \ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}.

where
\Delta\in{\mathfrak{R}}[z]
is a polynomial such the entries of
\Delta{\sf A}
are also in
{\mathfrak{R}}[z]
.

If a differential equation is
p
-integral relative to
{\mathfrak{R}}
then all its solutions at
z=z_{0}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
with the initial value
y_{0}\in{\mathfrak{R}}^{n}
, are
p
-integral, relative to
{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{\Delta(z_{0})}]
, provided that
p
is not invertible in this ring. However, the converse does not seem to be true, but the author is not aware of a counterexample. Note that the integer-valued polynomials like
\frac{z(z+1)}{2}
have values in
\mathbb{Z}
for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}
, however, they are not defined over
\mathbb{Z}
.

The example in
Proposition

1.2
shows that the
p
-curvature of a differential equation might be zero but its solutions might not be
p
-integral. We can also observe this in a theoretical way as follows.
We take a representative for the coefficients of
y(p):=\check{y}
in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
and use
y(p)
to emphasize that it depends on
p
.
Therefore,
y(p)\in\check{\mathfrak{R}}[[z-z_{0}]]^{\sf n}
and
\frac{\partial y(p)}{\partial z}\equiv_{p}{\sf A}(z)y(p)
. The main difficulty in proving that the entries of
y
are integral functions is to glue the data of all
y(p)
for different
p
together. By
Proposition

1.4
we know that over
\check{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
we have a unique solution
y
with with
y(z_{0})=y_{0}
(here we must assume that
\Delta(z_{0})
is invertible in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
). We might claim that
y
is
p
-integral and

y\equiv_{p}y(p)
. The first strategy is prove this by induction on
n
for the coefficient of
(z-z_{0})^{n}
.
This is trivially true for the coefficients of
(z-z_{0})^{i},\ \ i=0,1
. Since in the larger ring
\check{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, we have inverted all
N
’s with
p\nmid N
, this is also true for
n<p
.
The coefficient of
(z-z_{0})^{n}
in
y
is computed by

(1.14)

ny_{n}=\sum_{i+j=n-1}{\sf A}_{i}y_{j}.

We have also
ny(p)_{n}\equiv_{p}\sum_{i+j=n-1}{\sf A}_{i}y(p)_{j}
.
For
n\leq p^{2}-1
with
p\mid n
, the right hand side of (
1.14
) is divisible by
p
and so
y_{n}
can be computed in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
. However, for
n=p^{2}
, we observe that
y_{n}
might have
p
in its denominator and the argument breaks. One might try to give a recursive formula for the order of
p
in the denominator of
y_{n}
. In case both
z_{0}
and the differential equation are defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, the classical lower bound
{\rm ord}_{p}(y_{n})\geq\frac{-n}{p(p-1)}
(see for instance
[
12
, Proposition 3.38]
) does not see this phenomenon, for instance, it says that for
n=p^{2}-1
, the prime
p
might divide the denominator of
y_{n}
, whereas by our recursion this does not happen.

1.7
Final conclusions

Apart from
Theorem

1.1
with
m=p
a prime, there are many other evidences for Grothendieck-Katz conjecture. It is verified for “suitable direct factors” of Gauss-Manin connections, see
[
47
]
, and connections on rank one vector bundles, see
[
17
]
. Moreover, if the
p
-curvature of a given differential equation is zero for all but a finite number of primes then its singularities are Fuchsian and all the residue matrices are diagonolizable with eigenvalues in
\mathbb{Q}
, see
[
50
, Chapter 9]
, see also
[
49
, Theorem 8.1]
. These are true if the differential equation has algebraic solutions. In other words, the local data of a differential equation with zero
p
-curvatures, looks like a local data of a linear differential equation with only algebraic solutions. The differential equations over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
which are uniquely determined by their local data (rigid differential equations) satisfy the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture, see
[
50
, Chapter 9]
. Therefore,
if there is a counterexample to this conjecture over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
we must look for it among non-rigid differential equations, that is, they depend one some auxiliary parameters.
Example of such differential equations come from Painlevé VI, Heun and Lamé equations, see
[
60
,
61
]
. Among these equations, the Lamé equation is the most simple one:

p(x)\frac{d^{2}y}{dz^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}p^{\prime}(z)\frac{dy}{dz}-(n(n+1)z+B)y=0,

where
p(z)=4z^{3}-g_{2}z-g_{3}
. It depends on the parameters
g_{2},g_{3}\in\mathbb{C},n\in\mathbb{Q},B\in\mathbb{C}
. Our search, with no specific strategy, gave us only Lamé equations with algebraic solutions. These are classified in
[
7
, Table 4]
. We also found the Lamé equation with
B=0,\ n=\frac{7}{4},g_{2}=0,g_{3}=1
which is not listed in the mentioned reference.
5
5

5

For further details see
Chapter

2
.

Theorem

1.1
can be reformulated for Gauss-Manin connections, and its converse provides us with a conjectural description of Gauss-Manin connections. This conjecture with
k=1
appears in
[
4
, Appendix of Chapter V]
and the main evidence for this comes from
[
47
]
.

1.8
Appendix: A finitely generated ring

We would like to highlight a very particular case of
Proposition

1.7
which results in the following elementary statement.

Proposition 1.10
.

Let
a
be a positive rational number. The
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra generated by
[a]_{k}:=\frac{a(a-1)\cdots(a-k+1)}{k!},\ k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is of the form
\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{N}]
, for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
which is a product of distinct primes. In particular, the number of primes appearing in the denominator of
[a]_{k},\ k\in\mathbb{N}
is finite.

Proof.

Let
a=\frac{d}{n},\ {\rm gcd}(n,d)=1
. We first prove that such a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
A
is finitely generated by
[a]_{k},\ k=0,1,2,\ldots,d
and
\frac{1}{n}
.
For
k>d
we have

\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{n}=k}[a]_{i_{1}}[a]_{i_{2}}\cdots[a]_{i_{n}}=0,

which follows from (
1.8
) applied to the equality
y^{n}-z^{d}=0
with
y:=z^{a}
.
This implies that

n[a]_{k}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of products of
[a]_{s},\ s<k
. Therefore,
A
is finitely generated.
For rational numbers
a_{i}=\frac{d_{i}}{n_{i}},\ {\rm gcd}(n_{i},d_{i})=1
we have
A=\mathbb{Z}[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{n_{1}},\frac{1}{n_{2}},\cdots]
which follows from
s_{i}n_{i}+r_{i}d_{i}=1
or equivalently
s_{i}+r_{i}a_{i}=\frac{1}{n_{i}}
for some
r_{i},s_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. In a similar way,
\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{n_{1}},\frac{1}{n_{2}}]=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{[n_{1},n_{2}]}]
and then by induction
A=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{N}]
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
. If
N=N_{1}^{2}N_{2}
is not square free then
\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{N_{1}^{2}N_{2}}]=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{N_{1}N_{2}}]
and the result follows.
∎

Proposition

1.10
implies that for any set of positive rational numbers
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathbb{Q}
there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
s
with

N^{s}\frac{(a_{1})_{n_{1}}(a_{2})_{n_{2}}\cdots(a_{k})_{n_{k}}}{n_{1}!n_{2}!\cdots n_{k}!}\in\mathbb{Z}.

The main interest in the literature is those cases in which
s=n_{1}+n_{2}+\cdots+n_{k}
, see for instance
[
97
]
.

The converse of
Proposition

1.10
must be also true, that is, if the
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra generated by
[a]_{k},\ k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is finitely generated then
a
must be a rational number. For the proof we might use:

Theorem 1.4

(Kronecker)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{k}}
be a number field and
a
be an element in the ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
of
{\mathfrak{k}}
.
The number
a
is in
\mathbb{Z}
if and only if for all except a finite number maximal prime ideals
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
, its class in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{k}}/{\mathfrak{p}}\cong\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
is in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Another useful version of this theorem is:

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
P
be an irreducible non-constant polynomial in
\mathbb{Z}[x]
of degree
d
. If for all except a finite number primes
p
,
P
has
d
roots in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
then
P
is of degree
1
, and hence, it has a unique rational root.

Theorem

1.4
is known under the name Kronecker’s density theorem, see
[
34
]
in which the author refers to M. Bauer.
6
6

6

M. Bauer, Über einen Satz von Kronecker. Arch. d. Math. u. Phys. 6, 212–222 (1904).
For the proof one mainly proves its generalizations such as Frobenius and Chebotarev density theorems. The author was not able to find an elementry proof.

1.9
Appendix: Eisenstein theorem in many variables

In the following
y(z)
is a holomorphic function
y:U\to\mathbb{C}
defined in some connected open subset
U
of
\mathbb{C}^{a}
with
z=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{a})\in U
. The following is the generalization of Eisenstein’s theorem in
Proposition

1.3
, to a multivariable case. In the following for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{a}
we write
|n|:=\sum_{j=1}^{a}n_{j}
and for two such vectors
n,m
when we write
n<m
we mean that
n_{j}\leq m_{j}
for all
j=1,2,\ldots,a
and at least one of them is a strict inequality.

Proposition 1.11
.

Let
y(z)
be an algebraic function and
z_{0}\in U
. If the polynomial equation
P(z,y(z))=0
is defined over a ring
{\mathfrak{R}}
,
z_{0}\in{\mathfrak{R}}^{a}
and
y(z_{0})\in{\mathfrak{R}}
then there is
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
such that

\frac{N^{|m|}y^{(m)}(z_{0})}{m!}\in{\mathfrak{R}},\ \ \ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{a}.

Proof.

We write the Taylor series
y(z)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{a}}y_{i}\cdot(z-z_{0})^{i}
of
y(z)
at
z=z_{0}
, where
y_{i}
’s are unknwon coefficients and substitute in
P(z,y(z))=0
. Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be the
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra generated by coefficients of
P
,
z_{0}
and
y_{0}:=y(z_{0})
. Let also
\Delta:=\frac{\partial P}{\partial y}(z_{0},y_{0})
.
Computing the coefficient of
(z-z_{0})^{n},n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{a}
we get a recursion of type

\Delta\cdot y_{n}=\hbox{ a polynomial of degree $\leq|n|$ in $y_{i},\ i<n$, with coefficients in ${\mathfrak{R}}$},

for instance

\Delta y_{(0,\cdots,\underbrace{1}_{j-th},0,\cdots,0)}=-\frac{\partial P}{\partial z_{j}}(z_{0},y_{0})\in{\mathfrak{R}}.

By induction we can show that
y_{n}
has a pole order at most
2|n|-1
at
\Delta
. For
|n|=1
this follows from the above equality. If this is true for all
m<n
then
\Delta y_{n}
is a sum of monomials
y_{i_{1}}y_{i_{2}}\cdots y_{i_{k}}
with
i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{k}\leq n
and
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}<n
and coefficients in
{\mathfrak{R}}
. If
k=1
then
2|i_{1}|-1\leq 2|n|-2
and we are done. If
k\geq 2
then
2|i_{1}|-1+2|i_{2}|-1\cdots+2|i_{k}|-1\leq 2|n|-k\leq 2|n|-2
and we are done again. It follows that
N:=\Delta^{2}
satisfy the desired property.
∎

Next, we give an example of algebraic function in many variables.
We consider the polynomial
f
of degree
d
:

(1.15)

f:=x^{d}+1+t_{1}x^{d-1}+\cdots+t_{d-1}x+t_{d},

and regard
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{d})\in{\sf T}:\mathbb{C}^{d}\backslash\{\Delta=0\}
as parameters. For
t=0
it is
x^{d}+1
, and its zeros are
d
-th roots of minus unity. Let
x_{i}(t),\ i=1,2
be two roots of
f
with
x_{i}(0)=\zeta_{i},\ \ \zeta_{i}^{d}=-1
. These are holomorphic functions in
t
and we may ask a student to write its Taylor series at
t=0
.
In this section we do this. For a non-integer positive number
r
let

\langle r\rangle:=(r-1)(r-2)\cdots(\{r\}+1)\{r\}=\frac{\Gamma(r)}{\Gamma(\{r\})}.

For
0<r<1
by definition
\langle r\rangle=1
. For an integer
a
we denote by
\bar{a}
it unique representative modulo
d
in
\{0,1,\ldots,d-1\}
. The following is a special case of
[
66
, Theorem 13.4]

with
n=0,\ k=1

Theorem 1.6
.

For
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
0\leq\beta\leq d-2
we have

(1.16)

\frac{x_{2}^{\beta}(t)}{f^{\prime}(x_{2}(t))}-\frac{x_{1}^{\beta}(t)}{f^{\prime}(x_{1}(t))}={{{\sum}}}_{a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},\ d\nmid(\beta+1-\sum_{i=1}^{d}ia_{i})}\left(\frac{1}{a_{1}!a_{1}!\cdots a_{d}!}D_{a}{\sf p}_{a}\right)t^{a},

where

\displaystyle D_{a}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{d-1-\beta+\sum_{i=1}^{d}ia_{i}}{d}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{\beta+1+\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}ja_{d-j}}{d}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle{\sf p}_{a}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{d}\left(\zeta_{2}^{\overline{\beta+1+\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}ja_{d-j}}}-\zeta_{1}^{\overline{\beta+1+\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}ja_{d-j}}}\right).

Chapter 2
Lamé equation

The trademark of Lamé’s career was moving from one topic to another in a quite logical way but he often ended up studying problems very far removed from the original […] for the French seemed to feel that he was too practical for a mathematician and yet too theoretical for an engineer, see
[
76
]
.

Abstract:

We analyse Lamé equations with zero
p
-curvature. We investigate the cases in which the
m
-curvature modulo

p^{k}
is not zero for some
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with

{\rm ord}_{p}m!\geq k
.

2.1
Introduction

There are many evidences for Grothendieck-Katz conjecture in the literature. The most important one is due to N. Katz in
[
47
]
: It is true for Gauss-Manin connections and its “suitable direct factors”. For connections on rank one vector bundles on curves it is proved by D. V. Chudnovsky and G. V. Chudnovsky in
[
17
, Theorem 8.1]
, however, the author’s impression is that the proof must be revised, see
Section

2.3
and
[
11
, footnote page 177]
. This has been generalized
when the differential Galois group of the connection has a solvable neutral component, see
[
4
, Chapter VIII, Exercises 5 and 6, Section 3]
and
[
11
]
.
An important class of linear differential equations
y^{\prime}={\sf A}y
for which the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture is not known is of the format

(2.1)

{\sf A}:=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{{\sf A}_{i}}{z-z_{i}},

where
z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}\in\mathbb{C}
are distinct complex numbers and
{\sf A}_{i}
’s are
{\sf n}\times{\sf n}
matrices with entries in
\mathbb{C}
. The rank one case
{\sf n}=1
is easy to handle and it follows from Kronecker’s criteria.
The rank two case
{\sf n}=2
with two singularities
r=2
is not so difficult as it is reduced to verify the conjecture for Gauss hypergeometric equation. This and the case of one (finite) singularity, that is,
r=1
follows from N. Katz’s work below.
If the
p
-curvature of a given differential equation is zero for all but a finite number of primes then its singularities are Fuchsian and all the residue matrices are diagonolizable with eigenvalues in
\mathbb{Q}
, see
[
50
, Chapter 9]
, see also
[
49
, Theorem 8.1]
. This implies that in (
2.1
) we have to consider diagonolizable matrices
{\sf A}_{i}
’s with rational eigenvalues.
These are true if the differential equation has algebraic solutions. In other words, the local data of a differential equation with zero
p
-curvatures, looks like a local data of a linear differential equation with only algebraic solutions.
The differential equations over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
which are uniquely determined by their local data (rigid differential equations) satisfy the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture, see
[
50
, Chapter 9]
.
Therefore, if there is a counterexample to this conjecture we must look for it among non-rigid differential equations, that is, they depend on some accessory parameters and they are not pull-back of rigid ones.
An important class of differential equations (
2.1
) for which the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture is still open and depend on some accessory parameters is the case
r=3
and
{\sf n}\geq 2
for which by a linear transformation we can assume that
z_{1}=0,\ z_{2}=1
and
z_{3}=t
:

(2.2)

{\sf A}=\frac{{\sf A}_{0}}{z}+\frac{{\sf A}_{1}}{z-1}+\frac{{\sf A}_{t}}{z-t}.

There are three main examples of linear differential equations of this format: Lamé, Heun and Painlevé VI. It turns out that Lamé equation is a particular case of Heun equation, and this in turn is a particular case of the linear differential equation underlying Painlevé VI. All these three differential equations are of the format (
2.2
), see
[
60
,
61
]
. In the present text we consider the Lamé equation
which is traditionally written as a linear differential equation:

(2.3)

P(z)\frac{d^{2}y}{dz^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}P^{\prime}(z)\frac{dy}{dz}-(n(n+1)z+B)y=0,

where
P(z)=4z^{3}-g_{2}z-g_{3}
with
27g_{3}^{2}-g_{2}^{3}\not=0
. It depends on the parameters
g_{2},g_{3}\in\mathbb{C},n\in\mathbb{Q},B\in\mathbb{C}
(
B
is called the accessory parameter).
Our main reference for Lamé equation is
[
7
]
and the references therein.
In a personal communication with S. Reiter, he sent me the article
[
39
]
in which the J. Hofmann computes Lamé equations with the monodromy group of type
(1;e)
.

2.2
Lamé equation as a connection on elliptic curves

We denote by

E_{g_{2},g_{3}}:y^{2}=4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3},

the corresponding Weierstrass family of elliptic curves. For this we assume that
\Delta:=27g_{3}^{2}-g_{2}^{3}\not=0
, otherwise, the curve
E_{g_{2},g_{3}}
is singular and the Heun equation is a pull-back of the Gauss hypergeometric equation, see
Proposition

2.1
.
The algebraic group
\mathbb{C}^{*}
acts on
{\rm Spec}(\mathbb{C}[g_{2},g_{3},B])
by

k\bullet(g_{2},g_{3},B):=(g_{2}k^{2},g_{3}k^{3},Bk),\ k\in\mathbb{C}^{*}

and it is easy to show that if
f(z)
satisfies the Lamé equation with parameters
g_{2},g_{3},B,n
then
f(k^{-1}z),\ \ k\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
satisfies
the Lamé equation with parameters
g_{2}k^{2},g_{3}k^{3},Bk,n
(
n
is unchanged).
It is proved that any two ‘equivalent’ Lamé equations are necessarily obtained by the above
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action, see
[
7
]
, and so, the moduli space of Lamé equations for fixed
n
is the weighted projective space
\mathbb{P}^{1,2,3}\backslash\{\Delta=0\}
, which is the projectivization of the homogeneous ring
\mathbb{C}[g_{2},g_{3},B]
with
\deg(g_{2})=2,\ \deg(g_{3})=3,\ \deg(B)=1
. The Lamé equation does not change under
n\mapsto-1-n
and so we can assume that
n\geq-\frac{1}{2}
.

Let
\Lambda
be a lattice in
\mathbb{C}
and
\wp(z,\Lambda)
be the Weierstrass
\wp
function. If
f(z)
is a solution of the the Lamé equation then
f(z):=f(\wp(z))
is a solution of

\frac{d^{2}f}{dz^{2}}-(n(n+1)\wp(z)+B)f=0.

which is called the elliptic function form of the Lamé equation (we also call (
2.3
) the algebraic Lamé equation).
Let
z
be the coordinate system on
\mathbb{C}
and hence on the torus
\mathbb{C}/\Lambda
. This gives us the vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
on
\mathbb{C}/\Lambda
as it is invariant under the translation by elements of
\Lambda
.
We remark that under Weierstrass uniformization the vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}

is mapped to the vector field

(2.4)

{\sf v}:=y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\frac{1}{2}P^{\prime}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial y}.

This follows from
\frac{\partial\wp}{\partial z}=\wp^{\prime}
and

\frac{\partial\wp^{\prime}}{\partial z}=\frac{1}{2}P^{\prime}(\wp(z))
. Note that
{\sf v}
is tangent to
E_{g_{2},g_{3}}
:

{\sf v}(y^{2}-P(x))=0

and so it is the correct vector field to consider (in the literature sometimes one uses
y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
).
Therefore, the elliptic function form of the Lamé equation is also an algebraic differential equation, not over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, but over the elliptic curve
E_{g_{2},g_{3}}
:

(2.5)

\left(y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+(6x^{2}-\frac{1}{2}g_{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)^{2}f=(n(n+1)x+B)f.

We can also write this as a system.
On the elliptic curve
E_{g_{2},g_{3}}
we consider the following linear differential equation

(2.6)

dY={\sf A}Y,\ \ \ {\sf A}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&\frac{dx}{y}\\
n(n+1)\frac{xdx}{y}+B\frac{dx}{y}&0\end{bmatrix}.

If we set
Y=[f,{\sf v}f]^{{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{r}}}
then
{\sf v}Y={\sf A}({\sf v})Y
. The local exponents of (
2.6
) at the point at infinity
\infty=[0:1:0]
of
E_{g_{2},g_{3}}
are

-n
and
n+1
and so if it has only algebraic solutions then
n\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Note that the exponents of Lamé equation itself at infinity is

-\frac{n}{2}
and
\frac{n+1}{2}
, and the difference is due to the fact that the projection
E_{g_{2},g_{3}}\to\mathbb{P}^{1},\ (x,y)\to x
is two to one map ramified at infinity.

2.3
Some special cases

Theorem 2.1
.

The Grothendieck-Katz conjecture holds for Lamé equations with
n+\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proof.

By Frobenius basis of linear differential equations, we know that if
n+\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, there might be a logarithmic solution of the Lamé equation at infinity. By a theorem of Brioschi and Halphen we know that for

n+\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exists a weighted homogeneous polynomial
p_{n}(B,g_{2},g_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}[B,\frac{g_{2}}{4},\frac{g_{3}}{4}]
of degree
n+\frac{1}{2}
and monic in
B
such that the Lamé equation has no logarithmic
solutions at
\infty
if and only if
p_{n}=0
. It turns out that this is also equivalent to the Lamé equation having finite monodromy, for details and references see
[
7
, Theorem 2.2 and 2.3]
. If the
p
-curvature of the Lamé equation is zero for all but a finite number of primes then its local solution at infinity cannot be logarithmic.
∎

In
[
17
, Theorem 7.2 page 91]
, brothers Chudnovsky claim the following:
For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the Lamé equation with
27g_{3}^{2}-g_{2}^{3}\not=0
never satisfies the assumptions of the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture, that is, its
p
-curvature is non-zero for infinitely many
p
.
Unfortunately, this statement is false. We have many Lamé equations with
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
finite monodromy, and hence, they have zero
p
-curvature for all except a finite number of primes.
It has been proved in the literature that if the Lamé equation with
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
has finite monodromy, then its monodromy group is a dihedral
group
D_{N}
of order
2N
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, see
[
7
, Corollary 3.3]
.
In this reference, and also Waal’s Ph.D. thesis
[
89
, Remark 6.7.10]
, we can find many examples, such as

(2.7)

n=2,\ B=21,\ g_{2}=327,\ g_{3}=1727,

of such Lamé equations with finite monodromy, see also
Section

2.4
. It is strange that in an earlier work
[
16
, page 2773]
the author, which mentions
[
17
]
in the introduction, has even computed the underlying (smooth) elliptic curve
E_{g_{2},g_{3}}
(unfortunately without computing
B
) but has not complained about the results in
[
17
, Theorem 7.2 page 91]
.
After a consult with F. Beukers he sent me the article
[
19
]
in which the author even counts the number of such Lamé equations.
In
[
17
, Theorem 7.2 page 91]
, it has been also claimed the following. For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
there exists a weighted homogeneous polynomial
l_{n}(B,g_{2},g_{3})\in\mathbb{Q}[B,g_{2},g_{3}]
of degree
2n+1
and monic in
B
with the following property. The
p
-curvature of Lamé equation is nilpotent for all but a finite number of primes if and only if
l_{n}=0
.
The polynomial
l_{n}
seems to be the polynomial described in
[
7
, Theorem 3.2]
. In any case, the examples of Lamé equations mentioned in the above references, and also
Section

2.4
, provide counterexamples to this statement too.

A special class of Lamé equations, which is usually excluded from discussions is:

Proposition 2.1
.

The Lamé equation with
27g_{3}^{2}-g_{2}^{3}=0
is a pull-back of Gauss hypergeometric equation, and hence, the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture is true for these differential equations.

Proof.

The curve
E_{g_{2},g_{3}}
is singular and hence after a blow-up it is a rational curve. More explicitly, let us take
g_{2}=3a^{2},\ g_{3}=a^{3}
and so
p(x)=4(x+\frac{a}{2})^{2}(x-a)
. Under the desingularization map

\mathbb{P}^{1}\to E_{g_{2},g_{3}},\ \ \ z\mapsto(z^{2}+a,2z(z+\frac{3}{2}a))

the vector field
(z^{2}+\frac{3}{2}a)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
is mapped to
{\sf v}
in
(
6.9
) and we get the differential equation:

\left((z^{2}+\frac{3}{2}a)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right)^{2}=(n(n+1)(z^{2}+a)+B).

which can be clearly transformed into a Gauss hypergemetric equation.
∎

2.4
p
-curvature of Lamé equation

The section is based on the author’s search for a possible counterexample to Grothendieck-Katz conjecture
among Lamé equations. Of course, the author has not been successful. Instead, we have produced many examples such
that the number of primes for which the
p
-curvature vanishes seems to be much more than the number of non-vanishing cases. The author’s hope is that the experiments presented in this section motivate the reader to systematically study the density of primes associated to differential equations. Another starting point for this is
[
17
, Proposition 6.2, Corollary 6.3]
. As one can feel it by reading the mentioned reference, Cheobotarev density theorem seems to be the tip of an iceberg in the framework of differential equations.

We first write the Lamé equation (
2.3
) as a system in a canonical way.
The matrix
Y:=[f,\frac{\partial f}{\partial z}]^{{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{r}}}
satisfies

Y^{\prime}={\sf A}Y,\ \ {\sf A}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-\frac{1}{2}\frac{P^{\prime}(z)}{P(z)}&\frac{n(n+1)z+B}{P(z)}\end{bmatrix}.

We consider only the cases
g_{2},g_{3},B,n\in\mathbb{Q}
, and hence, the entries of
P(z){\sf A}
are polynomials in
z
with
rational coefficients. Let
N
be the common denominator of all these parameters.
It is easy to see that
y^{(n)}={\sf A}_{n}y
, where
{\sf A}_{n}
are computed recursively by

{\sf A}_{1}={\sf A},\ \ {\sf A}_{n+1}=\frac{\partial{\sf A}_{n}}{\partial z}+{\sf A}_{n}{\sf A}
, and all
{\sf A}_{n}
’s have entries in

\mathbb{Z}[z,\frac{1}{NP(z)}]
.
For a prime number
p
, the matrix
{\sf A}_{p}

considered as a matrix with entries in
\mathbb{Z}[z,\frac{1}{NP(z)}]/p{\mathfrak{R}}[z,\frac{1}{NP(z)}]
is usually called the
p
-curvature of the Lamé equation. We have proved:

1
1

1

This is
Theorem

1.1
for Lamé equations.

Theorem 2.2
.

If all the entries of solutions of the Lamé equation are algebraic functions then
for all but a finite number of primes
p
and
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\rm ord}_{p}m!\geq k

we have
{\sf A}_{m}\equiv_{p^{k}}0
, that is,
{\sf A}_{m}
is zero in the ring
\mathbb{Z}[z,\frac{1}{NP(z)}]/p^{k}\mathbb{Z}[z,\frac{1}{NP(z)}]
.

For our purpose, we have written the procedure
BadPrD
in
foliation.lib
.
It computes the bad (
p
-curvature non-zero) and good (
p
-curvature zero) primes of a linear differential defined over
\mathbb{Q}
for primes less than or equal to a given number. If the differential equation is not written as a system then the procedure transforms it into a system in a canonical way. The output consists of three list of primes 1. primes in the denominator of the system. For the Lamé equation these are primes 2, and those in the denominator of
n
and
B
. We completely ignore these primes. 2. Bad primes: primes such that the

p
-curvature is not zero 3. Good primes: primes that the
p
-curvature is zero.
The fourth entry is optional. If it is given and it is a positive integer
k
then the procedure verifies whether the
m_{p,k}
-curvature is zero or not, where for a given prime and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
m_{p,k}
is the smallest
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
{\rm ord}_{p}m!\geq k
.
Only the result of the last two lists might change.

We first check that algebraic Lamé equations (those with only algebraic solutions=finite monodromy) in
[
7
, Table 4]
have zero
p
-curvature. We only consider examples in this table which are defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

LIB "foliation.lib";
ring r=(0,z),x,dp;
matrix lde[1][3];
list L=list(1/4,0,0,1), list(3/4,3/8,-168,622), list(1/6,0,1,0), list(5/6,0,1,0),list(1/6,1/6,60,90),
list(1/10,0,0,1), list(3/10, 3/100,3,5/4), list(7/10,0,0,1), list(7/4,0,0,1);
number B; number n; number g2; number g3; int ub=100;
number P;
for (int i=1;i<=size(L);i=i+1)
{
n=L[i][1]; B=L[i][2]; g2=L[i][3]; g3=L[i][4];
P=4*z^3-g2*z-g3; lde=-n*(n+1)*z-B, 1/2*diffpar(P,z), P;
BadPrD(lde, z, ub)[2]; " ";
}

Apart from the the primes that appear in the expression of the
8
Lamé equations above, we observe that we have the following list of bad primes for each of them:

\{3\},\{5\},\{\},\{5\},\{5\},\{3\},\{2\},\{3,7\},\{3,7\}

respectively. This data might be useful for the study of bad primes of differential equations. The last entry
(n,B,g_{2},g_{3})=(7/4,0,0,1)
is not listed in
[
7
, Table 4]
and we found it by our random search of differential equations with zero
p
-curvature. We can verify experimentally that it has finite monodromy.

2
2

2

Therefore, it is not a counterexample to the conclusion of
Proposition

1.1
, and hence the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture.

Below, we check that for this example
m_{p,k}
-curvature vanishes for all good primes
p\leq 23,\ p\not=2,3,7
and all
k\leq 6
:

LIB "foliation.lib";
ring r=(0,z),x,dp;
matrix lde[1][3]; number n=7/4; number B=0; number g2=0; number g3=1; int ub=23;
number P=4*z^3-g2*z-g3;
lde=-n*(n+1)*z-B, 1/2*diffpar(P,z), P;
for (int i=1;i<=6;i=i+1){BadPrD(lde, z, ub,i);}

Therefore, this examples must have only algebraic solutions. According to
[
7
]
its monodromy group must be
G_{12}
. The way that we have found this example is explained in the next paragraph.

The main difficulty in finding Lamé equations with vanishing
p
-curvature for all except a finite number of primes is that there are Lamé equations which the first non-vanishing
p
-curvature is obtained for large
p
’s.
For example, for

(n,B,g_{2},g_{3})=(\frac{12}{89},0,0,1)
, the first bad primes are
83,107,113,

127,149,...
. For
(n,B,g_{2},g_{3})=(\frac{5}{87},0,0,1)
the bad primes below
150
are

17,97,107,109,113,127,131,137
.
For
(n,B,g_{2},g_{3})=(\frac{4}{65},0,0,1)
, the bad primes below
150
are

71,73,89,97,101,103,107,109,113,127,137,139
. For all these we have used:

LIB "foliation.lib";
ring r=(0,z),x,dp;
matrix lde[1][3];
list L=list(12/89,0,0,1), list(5/87,0,0,1), list(4/65,0,0,1);
number B; number n; number g2; number g3; int ub=150; number P;
for (int i=1;i<=size(L);i=i+1)
{
n=L[i][1]; B=L[i][2]; g2=L[i][3]; g3=L[i][4];
P=4*z^3-g2*z-g3; lde=-n*(n+1)*z-B, 1/2*diffpar(P,z), P;
BadPrD(lde, z, ub)[2]; " ";
}

In order to search for Lamé equations with only few bad primes, let us say
\leq m
, in a large range of primes we have written the procedure

BadPrDLess
which computes
p
-curvature until the number of bad primes exceed
m
. Using this we can for instance observe that

Proposition 2.2
.

Among the Lamé equations with
n=\frac{j}{i},\ 1\leq j,i\leq 100
and

(B,g_{2},g_{3})=(\frac{3}{8},-168,622),(\frac{1}{60},60,90),(\frac{3}{100},3,\frac{5}{4})

only the three cases
n=\frac{3}{4},\frac{1}{6},\frac{3}{10}
, respectively attached to each case above, have at most three bad primes in the range
2\leq p\leq 100
.

Note that these three Lamé equations have finite monodromy
[
7
, Table 4]
. The situation for Lamé equations with the underlying elliptic curves
y^{2}=4x^{3}-1

is different.
We take the Lamé equation with
B=0,n=\frac{j}{i},\ 1\leq j,i\leq 100,\ \ g_{2}=0,\ g_{3}=1
and compute all
n
with at most three bad primes
\leq 100
. Here, we have listed
n
together with its bad primes.
