94/45 ; 37 73 97 96/91 ; 41 73 97 99/4 ; 41 53 89 100/77 ; 37 83

The cases
n=\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{10},\frac{7}{10}
correspond to the algebraic Lamé equations found in
[
7
, Table 4]
. It turns out that
n=\frac{7}{4}
is also among this class. For

n=1,4,7,10,16,

the number of bad primes does not increase in the range
p\leq 150
. They seem to be algebraic Lamé equations, and the proof must not be so hard.
Using techniques used in
[
7
]
it is quite accessible to prove that these provides counterexamples to
[
17
, Theorem 7.2 page 91]
. The example (
2.7
) has only the bad prime
5
in the range
p\leq 150
.
The case
n=81
is exceptional as its bad primes become
13,29,97,103,109,127,139
in this range.
The cases with
n+\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
must fit in
Theorem

2.1
. In the other cases the number of bad primes increases as one increases the range
p\leq 100
of tested primes (we have just tested this for random choice of
n
). For instance for
n=16/99
we have only the bad prime
29
in the range
p\leq 100
but it increases to

29,103,109,127,131,137,149
in the range
p\leq 150
.
A similar search with the same data for
g_{2}=1
, and
g_{3}=0
has produced only the list

1/2 ;
1/6 ;
5/2 ; 3 5
5/6 ; 5
9/2 ; 5 7
13/6 ; 7 13
17/6 ; 5 11 17
47/87 ; 23 41 79

For
n=\frac{1}{6},\frac{5}{6}
we get the algebraic Lamé equations which are listed in
[
7
, Table 4]
.
For our computations we have used the code:

LIB "foliation.lib";
ring r=(0,z),x,dp;
matrix lde[1][3]; number B=0; number n; number g2=1; number g3=0; int ub=100; int nBdPr=3;
number P=4*z^3-g2*z-g3; int i; int j; list final; intvec prli=primes(1,ub);
for (j=1;j<=100;j=j+1)
{
for (i=1;i<=100;i=i+1)
{
if (lcm(intvec(i,j))==i*j)
{
n=number(j)/number(i);
lde=-n*(n+1)*z-B, 1/2*diffpar(P,z), P;
final=BadPrDLess(lde, z, ub,nBdPr);
if (size(final[2])+size(final[1])+size(final[3])==size(prli))
{final=final[2]; n, ";", final[1..size(final)];}
}
}
}

Finally, we would like to highlight the following consequence of Grothendieck-Katz conjecture, classification of finite groups for Lamé equations and
[
7
, Theorem 2.3, Corollary 3.4, Theorem 4.4]
.

Conjecture 2.1
.

If the
p
-curvature of a Lamé equation is zero for all but a finite number of primes then

n\in\{0,\pm\frac{1}{2},\pm\frac{1}{6},\pm\frac{1}{4},\pm\frac{3}{10},\pm\frac{1}{10}\}+\mathbb{Z}.

Our initial goal was to find a possible counterexample to the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture.
But, we were only able to find examples like:

3
3

3

We wanted to find a counterexample to the conclusion of

Proposition

1.1
. This proposition is the same as
Proposition

1.2
.

Proposition 2.3
.

For the Lamé differential equation

(4z^{3}-1)\frac{d^{2}y}{dz^{2}}+6z^{2}\frac{dy}{dz}-\frac{7}{36}zy=0

we have
{\sf A}_{5}\equiv_{5}0
, however,
{\sf A}_{25}\not\equiv_{5^{6}}0
.

Proof.

The computer code of this can be found here:

LIB "foliation.lib";
ring r=(0,z),x,dp;
matrix lde[1][3]; number B=0; number n=1/6; int g2=0; int g3=1; int ub=20;
number P=4*z^3-g2*z-g3;
lde=-n*(n+1)*z-B, 1/2*diffpar(P,z), P;
BadPrD(lde, z, ub);
BadPrD(lde, z, ub,6);

∎

2.5
Pull-back Lamé equations

This section is based on many e-mail exchanges with S. Reiter who did most of the computation. I told him about a list of
Lamé equations

g_{2}=0,g_{3}=1,B=0
with at most one bad prime among all primes
\leq 200

and a similar list for

g_{2}=1,g_{3}=0,B=0
with at most two bad primes among all primes
\leq 100
.
For example, the Lamé equation with
g_{2}=0,g_{3}=1,B=0,n=347/480
, has only the bad prime
197
among all primes
<=200
. The primes
p=2,3,5
are not considered as they are factors of
480
.

Proposition 2.4
.

The Lamé equation with
g_{2}=0,g_{3}=1
, that is over the elliptic curve
y^{2}=4x^{3}-1
with
B=0
, is the pull-back by the map

z\mapsto z^{3}
of the linear differential equation

(2.8)

(36z^{2}-9z)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}+(42z-6)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}-n(n+1),

with the Riemann scheme
0,\ [0,\frac{1}{3}]
,
\frac{1}{4},\ [0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
\infty,\ [-\frac{n}{6},\frac{n}{6}+\frac{1}{6}]
. In a similar way, the Lamé equation with
g_{2}=1,g_{3}=0
, that is over the elliptic curve
y^{2}=4x^{3}-x
with
B=0
, is the pull-back by the map

z\mapsto z^{2}
of the linear differential equation

(2.9)

L:=(16z^{2}-4z)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}+(20z-3)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}-n(n+1)

with the Riemann scheme
0,\ [0,\frac{1}{4}]
,
\frac{1}{4},\ [0,\frac{1}{2}]
,
\infty,\ [-\frac{n}{4},\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{4}]
.

By a linear transormation in
z
, one can see easily that the differential equation (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) can be transformed into the Gauss hypergeometric equation with
(a,b,c)=(\frac{-n}{6},\frac{n}{6}+\frac{1}{6},\frac{2}{3})
and
(\frac{-n}{4},\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4})
, respectively.
S. Reiter also informed me of the classification of Pull-back Lamé equations with non Dihedral monodromy group

by M. van Hoeij and R. Vidunas
.
Finally, the following question is rised by S. Reiter which is natural in view of computations done in
[
7
]
. For any Lamé equation with parameters
(n,B,g_{2},g_{3})
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
there is a
B_{k}
such that Lamé equations with parameters
(n,B,g_{2},g_{3})
and
(n+k,B_{k},g_{2},g_{3})
are equivalent. For a suggestion on what an equivalence relation should mean see
[
7
, page 3]
.

We finish this section with some experiments with the Gauss hypergeometric equation:

(2.10)

z(1-z)y^{\prime\prime}+(c-(a+b+1)z)y^{\prime}-aby=0,\ \ a,b,c\in\mathbb{C}.

For a random choice of parameters
a,b,c
in the Gauss hypergeometric equation it seems that the
p
-curvature is always non-zero except for a finite number of primes
p
. For example, for
(a,b,c)=(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},1)
and
p\not=2
, the
p
-curvature seems to never vanish. We have checked this for primes
\leq 150
.

LIB "foliation.lib";
ring r=(0,z),x,dp; matrix lde[1][3]; int i; int j;
number a=1/2; number b=1/2; number c=1;
int ub=150; number parm=z;
lde=-a*b, c-(a+b+1)*z, z*(1-z);
BadPrD(lde, parm, ub);

From another side in
Proposition

2.4
we have got the Gauss hypergeometric equations with
(a,b,c)=(\frac{-n}{6},\frac{n}{6}+\frac{1}{6},\frac{2}{3})
and
(\frac{-n}{4},\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4})
, for which we can experimentally observe that the number of both vanishing and non-vanishing curvature is infinite.

LIB "foliation.lib";
ring r=(0,z),x,dp;matrix lde[1][3]; int i; int j;
number a; number b; number c=2/3;
int ub=50; number parm=z; number N=100;
for (i=1; i<=N; i=i+1)
{
for (j=1; j<=N; j=j+1)
{
a=-i/number(6*j); b=-i/number(6*j)+1/6;
lde=-a*b, c-(a+b+1)*z, z*(1-z);
BadPrD(lde, parm, ub);
}
}

2.6
Differential equations and density of primes

The discussion in this section motivates us to define the density of primes for which the
p
-curvature vanishes. Recall that we have two notions of density. For a subset
S
of primes, we say that
S
has the natural, respectively analytic or Dirichlet, density
\delta
if the following limit exists and it equals to
\delta
:

\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\#\{p\in S\ |\ p\leq x\}}{\#\{p\hbox{ prime}\ |\ p\leq x\}}\ \ \hbox{ respectively }\ \ \lim_{s\to 1}\frac{\sum_{p\in S}\frac{1}{p^{s}}}{\sum_{p\hbox{ prime}}\frac{1}{p^{s}}}.

If a set of primes has a natural density, then it has also an analytic density and the two densities are equal, but the converse is false, see
[
84
]
.
For a linear differential equation
\frac{\partial y}{\partial z}={\sf A}(z)y
, we define its curvature denisty (natural or analytic), the density of primes
p
such that its
p
-curvature vanishes. The density of a finite number of primes (resp. all primes except for a finite number) is zero (resp. one). The converse is not necessarily true, see
the mathoverflow link
. One might try to formulate a stronger version of the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture speculating that if the curvature density of a differential equation is one then all its solutions are algebraic. The only well-known result about curvature density is the following:

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
a
be an algebraic number. The natural and analytic curvature density of
y^{\prime}=\frac{a}{z}y,\ a\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
is

\frac{1}{\#{\rm Gal}({\sf k}/\mathbb{Q})},

where
{\sf k}
is the splitting field of the minimal polynomial of
a
.

Proof.

Let
f(x)
be the minimal polynomial of
a
. If the
p
-curvature of
y^{\prime}=\frac{a}{z}y
is zero then
a^{p}-a=0
in
\mathbb{Z}[a]/p\mathbb{Z}[a]
. This implies that
f
divides
x^{p}-x
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[x]
. In particular,
f
modulo
p
is a product of linear equations.
The proposition is now a direct consequence of the Frobenius density theorem:
Let
f\in\mathbb{Q}[x]
be a polynomial. The density of primes
p
such that

f\ {\rm mod}\ p=f_{1}f_{2}\cdots f_{t},\ f_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}[x]\hbox{ irreducible },\ \deg(f_{i})=n_{i},\

is equal to

(2.11)

\frac{\#\{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}({\sf k}/\mathbb{Q})\ |\sigma\hbox{ with cycle pattern }n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{t}\ \}}{\#{\rm Gal}({\sf k}/\mathbb{Q})}.

see
[
84
]
.
For the application of this theorem we consider the case
n_{1}=n_{2}=\cdots=n_{t}=1

∎

Frobenius’ density theorem is a particular case of Chebotarev’s density theorem. The author tried to put these theorems in terms of curvature density of differential equations but there was no succces beyond
Proposition

2.5
. Even for the Gauss hypergeometric function obtained in
Proposition

2.4
, the author is not aware of any result regarding the density of vanishing
p
-curvatures.

Each computation of the present chapter is done in less than an hour, and it turns out that if we let our codes run for a day or more we can get more accurate density statements, for instance, for the Lamé equation with
(n,B,g_{2},g_{3})=(\frac{5}{87},0,0,1)
after a day of computation, the author was able to compute all good (
p
-curvature zero) and bad primes
\leq 797
. After the first
50
primes the first digit of the density of good primes seems to stabilize in
0.7
, however, the second digit does not seem to stabilize. The density of good primes
p\leq 797
is
0.71428
. Similar statements seem to be true for the Lamé equation with
(n,B,g_{2},g_{3})=(\frac{12}{89},0,0,1)
. For the computer codes and data
see the author’s webpage
.

Chapter 3
Local-global principle for leaf schemes

One may ask whether imposing a certain Hodge class upon a generic member of an algebraic family
of polarized algebraic varieties amounts to an algebraic condition upon the parameters,
A. Weil in
[
1
]
.

Abstract:
We study Hodge loci as leaf schemes of foliations.
The main ingredient is the Gauss-Manin connection matrix of families of projective varieties.
We also aim to investigate a conjecture on the ring of definition of leaf schemes and its consequences such as the algebraicity of leaf schemes (Cattani-Deligne-Kaplan theorem in the case of Hodge loci). This conjecture is a consequence of a local-global principle for leaf schemes.

3.1
Introduction

The present text arose from an attempt to combine Grothendieck-Katz
p
-curvature conjecture on linear differential equations with only algebraic solutions, and the Cattani-Deligne-Kaplan theorem on the algebraicity of Hodge loci.
For our purpose we generalize Hodge loci into leaf schemes of foliations. This has first appeared in
[
65
]
. Foliations in the present text are given over finitely generated subrings of the field of complex numbers, and it makes sense to manipulate them modulo primes. We introduce conjectural criterions,
Conjecture

3.1
and
Conjecture

3.2
, involving modulo
p
manipulations of foliations which guarantee the algebraicity of leaf schemes.

For a holomorphic function
f
in several complex variables, the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture provides a modulo primes criterion for algebraiciy of
f
provided that
f
satisfies a linear differential equation. Our main local-global conjecture,
Conjecture

3.2
, provides a modulo primes criterion for the algebraicity of the zero locus
f=0
of
f
, and in general complex analytic ideals, provided that
f=0
is part of the ideal of a leaf scheme. A simple, but yet non-trivial example of this situation, can be constructed from the Gauss hypergeometric function
F(z):=F(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},1|t)
.
For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the holomorphic function
F(1-t_{1})F(t_{2})-NF(1-t_{2})F(t_{1})
is not algebraic, however, we know that its zero locus in
(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
is an algebraic curve which is a singular model of a covering of the modular curve
X_{0}(N)
.
After adding more three variables, and constructing an ideal with four generators in
5
dimension, it can be seen as a leaf scheme, for further details see
Section

3.7
. Another example is the following series in
\binom{12}{4}=495
variables
t_{\alpha}
:

(3.1)

{{{\sum}}}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a:I{}\to\mathbb{N}_{0},\ \beta:=\frac{1}{4}\left(\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}\cdot\alpha+(1,1,1,1)\right),}}{{\beta_{i}\not\in\mathbb{Z},\ \beta_{0}+\beta_{1},\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}}}\frac{(-1)^{[\beta_{0}]+[\beta_{2}]}\langle\beta_{0}\rangle\langle\beta_{1}\rangle\langle\beta_{2}\rangle\langle\beta_{3}\rangle}{\prod_{\alpha\in I{}}a_{\alpha}!}\cdot\prod_{\alpha\in I{}}t_{\alpha}^{a_{\alpha}},

where
I:=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}\ |\sum_{i=0}^{3}\alpha_{i}=4\}
,
for a rational number
r
,
[r]
is the integer part of
r
, that is
[r]\leq r<[r]+1
,
\{r\}=r-[r]
and

\langle r\rangle=(r-1)(r-2)\cdots(\{r\})
. This function is a period of a holomorphic
2
-form on smooth surfaces of degree
4
in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
which is not algebraic. However, its zero locus is algebraic and parameterizes such surfaces with a line, for more details see
Section

3.10
.

Summary of the text:

In
Section

3.2
we explain what we mean by our local-global principle for Hodge loci.
Hopefully, this will motivate the reader for the definition of leaf scheme in
Section

3.3
.
The main results in
Section

3.2
, namely
Corollary

3.1
and
Corollary

3.2
, are proved much later in
Section

3.9
after elaborating the concept of leaf scheme.

Conjecture

3.1
in
Section

3.3
is not suitable for experimental purposes and so in
Section

3.4
we introduce a stronger conjecture whose hypothesis is implementable in a computer. This involves constructing many vector field in the ambient space and we discuss this in
Section

3.5
.
In
Section

3.6
we explain natural leaf schemes which arise in the framework of linear differential equations (local systems), and in particular Gauss-Manin connections. We construct the Hodge loci as leaf schemes in
Section

3.7
and discuss its ring of definition in
Section

3.8
. In
Section

3.10
we experimentally observe that natural generators of the ideal of a Hodge locus have all primes inverted in their expressions. Finally, in
Section

3.11
we prove
Theorem

3.7
using two results of N. Katz. This is a consequence of the Hodge conjecture for Hodge-Tate varieties.

Notations:

Throughout the text,
{\mathfrak{R}}
is a finitely generated subring of
\mathbb{C}
and
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}:={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}={\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{5},\cdots]
which is an infinitely generated subring of
\mathbb{C}
. We also take a finitely generated ring extension
{\mathfrak{R}}\subset{\mathsf{R}}
and set
{\sf T}:={\rm Spec}({\mathsf{R}})
. By an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme we simply mean the affine
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme
{\sf T}
. We consider an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
which is nothing but an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-linear morphism
{t}:{\mathsf{R}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
and set
\mathfrak{m}_{{\sf T},{t}}:={\rm ker}({t})
.
By definition,
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
is just the ring
{\mathsf{R}}
,
\Omega^{1}_{\sf T}
is the
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
-module of Kähler differentials (differential
1
-forms) in
{\sf T}
. For
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
,
f({t}):={t}(f)
is just the evaluation at
{t}
.
The
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
-module
\Theta_{\sf T}
of vector fields/derivations in
{\sf T}
is the dual of the
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
-module
\Omega^{1}_{\sf T}
.
We also look at a vector field
{\sf v}\in\Theta_{\sf T}

as a derivation
{\sf v}:{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T},\ \ f\mapsto{\sf v}(f):={\sf v}(df)
. For an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point
{t}
, we have also the well-defined
{\mathfrak{R}}
-linear map
{\sf v}(t):={t}\circ{\sf v}:\mathfrak{m}_{{\sf T},{t}}/\mathfrak{m}_{{\sf T},{t}}^{2}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
which is called the evaluation of
{\sf v}
at
{t}
. The dual
{{\bf T}}_{t}{\sf T}:=(\mathfrak{m}_{{\sf T},{t}}/\mathfrak{m}_{{\sf T},{t}}^{2})^{\vee}
is the tangent space of
{\sf T}
at
{t}
, and so, we have the evaluation map
\Theta_{\sf T}\to{{\bf T}}_{t}{\sf T}
which is
{\mathfrak{R}}
-linear.
As we do not need the language of sheaves, a sheaf on
{\sf T}
is identified with the set of its global sections. We take a submodule
\Omega
of
\Omega_{\sf T}
and denote its dual by
\Theta:=\{{\sf v}\in\Theta_{\sf T}\ |\ {\sf v}(\Omega)=0\}
which is a submodule of
\Theta_{\sf T}
.

We will assume that
\Omega
is integrable in the strongest format, that is,
d\Omega\subset\Omega\wedge\Omega_{\sf T}
.
Even though, we rarely use the integrability of
\Omega
,
we will frequently use the notation
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
to denote the foliation induced by
\Omega
. From an algebraic point of view
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
is just
\Omega
and nothing more.
For any other subring
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
of
\mathbb{C}
, we denote by

{\sf T}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}}:={\sf T}\times_{\mathfrak{R}}{\rm Spec}(\check{\mathfrak{R}})
. The ring
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
might be infinitely generated and the main example of this in this text is
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. We denote by
({\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t})
(resp.
({\sf T}^{\rm for}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t})
) the analytic (resp. formal) scheme underlying
{\sf T}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm for}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t}}
) is the ring of holomorphic functions in a neighborhood of
t
(resp. formal power series) and with coefficients in
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
. We say that an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point
{t}
of
{\sf T}
is smooth if there are
z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}},{t}}
which generate it freely as
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebra. We call
z=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n})
a holomorphic coordinate system.

We use the letter
L
to denote a subscheme of
({\sf T}^{\rm hol},{t})
, that is, we have an ideal
{{\mathcal{I}}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},{t}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}={\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},{t}}/{{\mathcal{I}}}
(this might have zero divisors or nilpotent elements).
Let
\Theta
be a submodule of the
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
-module
\Theta_{\sf T}
. Its rank is the number
a\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\wedge^{a+1}\Theta
is a torsion sheaf and
\wedge^{a}\Theta
is not. For
{\sf v}\in\Theta_{\sf T}
we define the scheme
{\rm Sch}({\sf v}\in\Theta)
given by the ideal generated by

\left|\begin{matrix}{\sf v}(P_{1})&{\sf w}_{1}(P_{1})&{\sf w}_{2}(P_{1})&\cdots&{\sf w}_{a}(P_{1})\\
{\sf v}(P_{2})&{\sf w}_{1}(P_{2})&{\sf w}_{2}(P_{2})&\cdots&{\sf w}_{a}(P_{2})\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\sf v}(P_{a})&{\sf w}_{1}(P_{a})&{\sf w}_{2}(P_{a})&\cdots&{\sf w}_{a}(P_{a})\end{matrix}\right|,\ \

P_{1},P_{2},\cdots,P_{a}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T},\ \ {\sf w}_{1},{\sf w}_{2},\ldots,{\sf w}_{a}\in\Theta.

In geometric terms if
{\mathfrak{R}}
is an algebraically closed field,
{\rm Sch}({\sf v}\in\Theta)
is the loci of points
{t}
in
{\sf T}
such that the vector

{\sf v}({t})
is in the
{\mathfrak{R}}
-vector space generated by
{\sf w}({t}),\ {\sf w}\in\Theta
. For two vector fields
{\sf v}
and
{\sf w}
the collinearness scheme
{\sf v}||{\sf w}
is just
{\rm Sch}({\sf v}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}{\sf w})
.
For a primes
p
which in not invertible in
{\mathfrak{R}}
, the ring
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}:={\mathfrak{R}}/p{\mathfrak{R}}
is non-zero, and we define
{\sf T}_{p}:={\sf T}\times_{\mathfrak{R}}{\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}}_{p})
which is modulo
p
reduction of
{\sf T}
. For a vector field
{\sf v}
in
{\sf T}_{p}
, it is known that
{\sf v}^{p}
is also a vector field, and this phenomena does not exist in
{\sf T}
.

Section

3.2
. Thanks go to Daniel Litt and Joshua Lam for comments on
Remark

3.2
.

3.2
Hodge loci

In order to motivate experts in Hodge theory, we explain a foliation free statement of our main local-global principle for Hodge loci. This has been the main motivation for writing the present text.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
Y
be a smooth projective variety. A Hodge cycle is any element in the intersection of the integral
cohomology
H^{{m}}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\subset H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y)
(
H^{{m}}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
is considered modulo torsion) and
F^{\frac{{m}}{2}}\subset H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y)
, where

F^{\frac{{m}}{2}}=F^{\frac{{m}}{2}}H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y)
is the
\frac{{m}}{2}
-th piece of the Hodge filtration of
H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y)
.

Therefore, the
\mathbb{Z}
-module of Hodge cycles is simply the intersection

H^{{m}}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\cap H^{\frac{{m}}{2},\frac{{m}}{2}}=H^{{m}}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\cap F^{\frac{{m}}{2}}
.
We have the intersection pairing/polarization
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:H^{{m}}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\times H^{{m}}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}
and so it makes sense to talk about the self-intersection or norm

\langle\delta,\delta\rangle
of a Hodge cycle
\delta
.
Now, let
Y\to V
be a family of smooth complex projective varieties (
Y\subset\mathbb{P}^{N}\times V
and
Y\to V

is obtained by projection on the second coordinate).

Definition 3.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\sf T}:=F^{\frac{{m}}{2}}H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y/V)
be the total space of the vector bundle of
F^{\frac{{m}}{2}}
pieces of the Hodge
filtration of
H^{{m}}_{\rm dR}(Y_{t}),\ \ {t}\in V
.
The locus
L_{k}
of Hodge cycles (or simply Hodge locus) of self-intersection equal to
k
is the subset of

F^{\frac{{m}}{2}}H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y/V)
containing such Hodge cycles.

Note that
{\sf T}:=F^{\frac{{m}}{2}}H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y/V)
is an algebraic bundle, however, the Hodge locus is a union
of local analytic varieties
L
. Moreover, such an
L
has a natural analytic scheme structure, that is, the natural ideal defining
L
might not be reduced, and hence,
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
might have zero divisors or nilpotent elements, see
Section

3.7
.

Theorem 3.1
.

(Cattani-Deligne-Kaplan,
[
15
, Theorem 1.1]
)
The locus
L_{k}
of Hodge cycles of self-intersection equal to
k
is an algebraic subset of

F^{\frac{{m}}{2}}H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y/V)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be a finitely generated subring of
\mathbb{C}
such that
Y\to V
,
{\sf T}
and
Y_{t}
have smooth models over
{\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}})
(we avoid introducing new notation for these models).
For an explicit construction of
{\sf T}
as an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme see
Section

3.7
.
Let
L
be a local analytic Hodge locus as above. It is given by an ideal
{{\mathcal{I}}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{{\rm hol}}_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}},{t}}
. We define

(3.2)

\Theta_{{\sf T},L}:=\{{\sf v}\in\Theta_{\sf T}\ |\ {\sf v}({{\mathcal{I}}})\subset{{\mathcal{I}}}\},

and we call it the
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
-module of vector fields in
{\sf T}
tangent to
L
.
One of our main motivations in the present text is the author’s not yet successful attempt to prove the following corollary of
Theorem

3.1
without using it.

Corollary 3.1
.

Let
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
be the
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
-module of vector fields in
{\sf T}
tangent to
L
. For all except a finite number of primes and all
{\sf v}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
, the vector field
{\sf v}^{p}
is also in
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}
(that is modulo
p
).

The above statement is the main inspiration for one of the main conjectures of the present text, see
Conjecture

3.2
. This conjecture says that
Corollary

3.1
implies
Theorem

3.1
, and so they are equivalent.

Remark 3.1
.

Note that even though
L
modulo
p
might not make sense, as it is defined over complex numbers (actually
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
), see
Theorem

3.4
and
Section

3.10
, the module of vector fields
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\subset\Theta_{{\sf T}}
is algebraic, so it makes sense to talk about modulo
p
of this module and
{\sf v}^{p}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}
.
It says that there is a lift
{\sf w}\in\Theta_{\sf T}
of

{\sf v}^{p}\in\Theta_{{\sf T}_{p}}
such that it lies in

\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
.
Note that a direct definition of
\Theta_{{\sf T}_{p},L}
as in (
3.2
) does not make sense.

We can write down a weaker version of
Corollary

3.1
as follows.
Let

(3.3)

\alpha:{{\bf T}}_{t}{\sf T}\times H^{\frac{m}{2}}(Y_{t},\Omega_{Y_{t}}^{\frac{m}{2}})\to H^{\frac{m}{2}+1}(Y_{t},\Omega_{Y_{t}}^{\frac{m}{2}-1}),\ t\in{\sf T}

be the IVHS map (infinitesimal variation of Hodge structures in Griffiths and his coauthor’s terminology, see
[
14
]
). Here,
{{\bf T}}_{t}{\sf T}
is the dual of the
{\mathfrak{R}}
-module
\mathfrak{m}_{{\sf T},{t}}/\mathfrak{m}_{{\sf T},{t}}^{2}
and it is the tangent space of
{\sf T}
at
{t}
. If
{t}
is an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
lying in a Hodge locus, by definition of
{\sf T}
, it comes together with a a Hodge cycle
\delta_{t}\in H^{m}(Y_{t},\mathbb{Z})
and hence an element
\bar{t}\in H^{\frac{m}{2}}(Y_{t},\Omega_{Y_{t}}^{\frac{m}{2}})
. We denote by
L
the Hodge locus corresponding to variations of
\delta_{t}
. The Hodge cycle
\delta_{t}
is called general if
\alpha(\cdot,\bar{t})
has maximal rank among all Hodge cycles.
This is equivalent to say that the Hodge locus
L
is typical in the sense of
[
6
]
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Assume that
\delta_{t}
is a general Hodge cycle and take any
v\in{{\bf T}}_{t}{\sf T}
with
\alpha(v,\bar{t})=0
. There is a vector field
{\sf v}
in
{\sf T}
such that
{\sf v}({t})=v
and for all except a finite number of primes
p
we have
\alpha({\sf v}^{p}({t}),\bar{t})=0
modulo
p
, where in the last equality we have considered smooth models of
Y,V,{\sf T},Y_{t}
over a finitely generated subring
{\mathfrak{R}}
of
\mathbb{C}
and then reduction modulo
p
is performed.

We have intentionally not used the language of reduction modulo a closed point of
{\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}})
with residue field of characteristic
p
, in order to highlight the classical modulo
p
manipulations. Note that since
Y_{t}
is a projective smooth scheme over
{\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}})
, the cohomology groups
H^{i}(Y_{t},\Omega_{Y_{t}}^{j})
are free
{\mathfrak{R}}
-mdoules.
In
Corollary

3.1
we might ask how big is
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
. We have discussed this in
Section

3.5
in the framework of leaf schemes.
Since
L
has a natural structure of an analytic scheme, our definition of
{\sf v}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
is stronger than the geometric definition:
{\sf v}({t})\in{{\bf T}}_{t}L
for any complex point
{t}
of
{\sf T}
. The following example might clarify the situation better. Let
{\sf T}:=\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\mathfrak{k}}
with the coordinate system
(x,y)
and
L
be the subscheme of
{\sf T}
given by

y^{2}=0
. In this case the Zariski tangent space of
L
at each closed point is the whole tangent space of
{\sf T}
at that point, and so, any vector field in
{\sf T}
is tangent to
L
in the geometric framework, but not necessarily in a scheme theoretic framework, that is,
y^{2}\mid{\sf v}(y^{2})
is not valid in general.
If
{\sf v}
is a vector field in
{\sf T}
and tangent to
L
in the geometric sense then we have

\alpha({\sf v}({t}),\bar{t})=0
for
t\in L
but there is no reason to believe that
\alpha({\sf v}^{p}({t}),\bar{t})=0
after taking reduction modulo
p
. This is the main reason why in
Corollary

3.2
we assume that
\delta_{t}
is a general Hodge cycle and hence
L
is smooth. In this case both geometric and scheme theoretical definitions of tangency to
L
are equivalent.

3.3
Leaf scheme

As in this text we care about the field or ring of definition of leaf schemes, we rewrite the definition of leaf scheme in
[
67
, Section 5.4]
in the algebraic framework.
It might be better for the reader to read first the definition over complex numbers before reading its algebraic/arithmetic version which involves some heavy notations due to the fact that we have to distinguish algebraic and holomorphic objects, and we have to insert the ring of definition into our notations. However, if there is no confusion, we will use simplified notations as in
[
67
, Chapter 5]
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme,
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
be a foliation in
{\sf T}
and
{t}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
.
A subscheme
L
of
({\sf T}^{{\rm hol}}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t})
with
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}:={\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}}}/{{\mathcal{I}}}
is called a leaf scheme of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
defined over
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
if

\Omega\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{{\rm hol}}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{{\rm hol}}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t}}\cdot d{{\mathcal{I}}}

projected to
(\Omega^{1}_{{\sf T}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{{\rm hol}}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t}})/{{\mathcal{I}}}\Omega^{1}_{{\sf T}}
and regarded as

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t}}/{{\mathcal{I}}}
-modules are equal. In other words,
\Omega\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{{\rm hol}}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t}}d{{\mathcal{I}}}
are equal modulo
{{\mathcal{I}}}\Omega^{1}_{{\sf T}}
.

Definition 3.4
.

If in the above definition
{{\mathcal{I}}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t}}
(resp.
{{\mathcal{I}}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm for}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}},{t}}
or
{{\mathcal{I}}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}}}
) then we say that
L
is a holomorphic leaf (resp. formal leaf or algebraic leaf) of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
defined over
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
and write it
L^{\rm hol}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}}
(resp.
L^{\rm for}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}}
or
L^{\rm alg}_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}}
) if it is necessary to emphasize its property of being holomorphic, formal or algebraic, and its ring of definition.

Let
{t}
be a smooth point of
{\sf T}
, that is, there are
z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}},{t}}
which generate it freely as
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebra. We call
z=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n})
a holomorphic coordinate system. By Frobenius theorem, see
[
67
, Theorem 5.8]
, we can choose such a coordinate system such that
\Omega\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}},{t}}
is generated freely by
dz_{i},\ i=1,2,\ldots,k
. It turns out that the analytic scheme given by the ideal
{{\mathcal{I}}}=\langle z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{k}\rangle
is a leaf scheme of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
and this is the classical notion of a leaf in the literature. We call it a general leaf. A general leaf is smooth (scheme theoretically) in the sense of
[
67
, Definition 5.8]
.

As the reader might have noticed from the definition of a general leaf, for a general definition of leaf scheme we assume that integers are invertible in the underlying ring and that is why we must use
\check{\mathfrak{R}}:={\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Definition 3.5
.

We say that a leaf scheme
L
is algebraic if
{{\mathcal{I}}}\otimes_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathbb{C}
is generated by elements in
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{\mathbb{C}}}
.
In other words, there is a subscheme of
V
of
{\sf T}_{\mathbb{C}}
containing the point
{t}
such that
L_{\mathbb{C}}
is just
(V^{\rm hol},{t})
.

Proposition 3.1
.

If a leaf scheme
L
defined over a ring
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is algebraic then there is
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
such that
L
is defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}[\frac{1}{N}]
.

Proof.

We have an ideal
J\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{\mathbb{C}}}
such that it is basically
{{\mathcal{I}}}
:

(3.4)

{{\mathcal{I}}}\otimes_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathbb{C}=J\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{\mathbb{C}}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm hol},{t}}.

Let
J=\langle P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{s}\rangle
. In particular, we have regular algebraic functions
P_{i}
defined in
{\sf T}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that they define the germ of the analytic scheme
L
. After adding finitely many coefficients used in the expression of
P_{i}
’s to the quotient field
{\mathfrak{k}}
of
{\mathfrak{R}}
, it follows that we have a finitely generated field extension
{\mathfrak{k}}\subset\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}
such that
J
is defined over
\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}
, and hence by abuse of notation, we write
J\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}}}
.
In (
3.4
) we can replace all
\mathbb{C}
’s with
\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}
and this implies that the algebraic ideal
J
is invariant under the Galois group
{\rm Gal}(\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}/{\mathfrak{k}})
. From this we can deduce that it is generated by elements defined over
{\mathfrak{k}}
, see
[
95
, page 19 Lemma 2]
and so we can assume that there are new generators
P_{i}
’s of
J
defined over
{\mathfrak{k}}
and in (
3.4
) we can replace all
\mathbb{C}
’s with
{\mathfrak{k}}
. After multiplication of
P_{i}
’s by some elements of
{\mathfrak{R}}
we get
P_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
.
The number
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
is the product of
N_{i}
’s attached to a set of generators
f_{i}
of
{{\mathcal{I}}}
such that
N_{i}f_{i}
is in the ideal
\langle P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{s}\rangle\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
.
∎

We believe that converse of
Proposition

3.1
is true.
The statement that a leaf scheme
L
is still defined over a finitely generated subring of
\mathbb{C}
has strong consequences.

Conjecture 3.1
.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be a finitely generated subring of
\mathbb{C}
and
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme.
Let also
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
be a foliation on
{\sf T}
. If a leaf scheme is defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
then it is algebraic.

3.4
Leaf scheme modulo prime

In this section we explain a generalization of Grothendieck-Katz conjecture for leaf schemes in the sense of
Definition

3.3
. We call it a local-global principle for leaf schemes.
Recall that the leaves of foliations in our context have scheme structure and their structural sheaf might have nilpotent elements. They are also defined over rings, for instance by Frobenius theorem if we start with a foliation defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
, the general leaves are defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
They might also have different codimensions for a given foliation.

Definition 3.6
.

We define

(3.5)

\Theta_{{\sf T},L}:=\left\{{\sf v}\in\Theta_{{\sf T}}\ |\ {\sf v}({{\mathcal{I}}})\subset{{\mathcal{I}}}\right\},

and call it the module of vector fields in
{\sf T}
tangent to
L
.

Conjecture 3.2

(Main local-global conjecture)
.

Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme,
{t}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
,
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
be a foliation on
{\sf T}
and
L
be a leaf scheme of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
through
{t}
.
If for all vector fields in
{\sf T}
tangent to
L
, that is
{\sf v}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
, and all but a finite number of primes
p
, the vector field
{\sf v}^{p}
in
{\sf T}_{p}
is also tangent to
L
modulo
p
, that is,
{\sf v}^{p}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}
,
then the leaf
L
is algebraic.

One may formulate a stronger conjecture with weaker hypothesis:

Conjecture 3.3
.

Conjecture

3.2
is true if we replace in its hypothesis
{\sf v}^{p}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}
with the weaker
hypothesis
{\rm Sch}({\sf v}^{p}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}{\mathfrak{R}}_{p})
contains the point
t
, that is, the vector field
{\sf v}^{p}
is tangent to
L
at
t
.

It is not so hard to see that
\Theta\subset\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
and even if we start with
{\sf v}\in\Theta
then
{\sf v}^{p}
might be in
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
and not
\Theta
.

Conjecture 3.4
.

(Ekedahl, Shepherd-Barron, Taylor and Luntz see
[
11
, Page 165]
and
[
55
]
)

Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme and
{\mathcal{F}}
be a non-singular foliation on
{\sf T}
.
If for all vector fields
{\sf v}
in
{\sf T}
tangent to the leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}
and all but a finite number of primes
p
,
{\sf v}^{p}
is also tangent to the leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}
then all the leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}
are algebraic.

Note that this is a particular case of
Conjecture

3.2
.
The main evidence to this conjecture (and the next one) is due to Bost in
[
11
]
in which he proves this with an extra hypothesis on the leaves (Liouville property).

Proposition 3.2
.

Conjecture

3.2
implies
Conjecture

3.1
.

Proof.

Let us take the leaf scheme
L
defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. If
{\sf v}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
then
we have
{\sf v}({{\mathcal{I}}})\subset{{\mathcal{I}}}
and hence
{\sf v}^{p}({{\mathcal{I}}})\subset{{\mathcal{I}}}
. Since the leaf
L
is also defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
, it makes sense to talk about its reduction
L_{p}
modulo
p
. In particular, a direct definition
\Theta_{{\sf T}_{p},L_{p}}
as in (
3.2
) is possible and

\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}=\Theta_{{\sf T}_{p},L_{p}}
.
This implies that
{\sf v}^{p}
is tangent to the leaf
L_{p}
in
{\sf T}_{p}
. In particular,

{\rm Sch}({\sf v}^{p}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}{\mathfrak{R}}_{p})\subset{\sf T}_{p}
contains the point
{t}
. This is exactly the hypothesis of
Conjecture

3.2
.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

Conjecture

3.2
can be rewritten for foliations given by a single vector field as follows:
Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme,
{t}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued smooth point of
{\sf T}
, and
{\sf v}
be a vector field in
{\sf T}
with
{\sf v}({t})\not=0
.
If for all but a finite number of primes
p
,
{\sf v}^{p}
is tangent to
L
(the collinearness scheme
{\sf v}||{\sf v}^{p}
contains
L
), then
L
is algebraic.
In a similar way
Conjecture

3.3
in this case is the following: If we have
{\sf v}
as before
such that for all but a finite number of primes
p
,
{\sf v}
is collinear with
{\sf v}^{p}
at the point
{t}
(the collinearness scheme
{\sf v}||{\sf v}^{p}
contains the point
{t}
) then the solution
L
of
{\sf v}
through
{t}
is algebraic.
In a private communication with Daniel Litt and Joshua Lam, they explained the author that the second conjecture is wrong. Their counterexample is
{\sf v}:=zy\frac{\partial}{\partial y}+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
in
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. One can easily check that

{\sf v}^{n}y=P_{n}(z)y
, where
P_{n}
can be computed recursively
P_{n+1}=P_{n}^{\prime}+zP_{n}
. For
n
even and odd,
P_{n}
is respectively even and odd polynomial. This implies that for
n
odd we have
P_{n}(0)=0
which implies that
{\sf v}^{p}(0,1)=0
for all primes
p\not=2
. The solution through
(0,1)
is given by
(z,e^{\frac{1}{2}z^{2}})
which is not algebraic.

1
1

1

Apart from
Conjecture

3.2
we have also another correction to this conjecture which is the converse of
Theorem

1.3
.

3.5
Vector fields tangent to leaf schemes

The formulation of
Conjecture

3.2
with the module
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
arises the question how big this module is? For instance, if
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}=0
then the hypothesis of
Conjecture

3.2
is automatically satisfied and one might doubt this conjecture. From another side, if
{t}
is a smooth point of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
then
L
is a general leaf and
\Theta\subset\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
.
In this section we gather some statements about
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
be a foliation in an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme
{\sf T}
,
L
be a leaf scheme of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
defined over a larger ring
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
) and through an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point
{t}
of
{\sf T}
.

Definition 3.7
.

We define
\bar{L}
to be the Zarsiki closure of
L
in
{\sf T}
, that is,

(3.6)

\bar{L}:={\rm Zero}\left(\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}}\right),\ \ \bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}\cap{{\mathcal{I}}}.

We say that
L
is Zariski dense in
{\sf T}
if
\bar{L}={\sf T}
. In other words,

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}\cap{{\mathcal{I}}}=\{0\},

which means that
{{\mathcal{I}}}
has not algebraic elements.

Note that the concept of Zariski closure depends on the underlying ring
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
. For instance
L
might be Zariski dense for
{\mathfrak{R}}
but not for an extension
\check{\mathfrak{R}}
of
{\mathfrak{R}}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme and
{t}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
, not necessarily smooth, and
L
be a leaf scheme of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
through
{t}
. We have

(3.7)

\Theta_{{\sf T},L}=\{{\sf v}\in\Theta_{{\sf T}}\ |\ {\sf v}(\Omega)\subset\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}}\}.

We may call the right hand side of (
3.7
) the dual of
\Omega
along the Zariski closure of
L
.
It is a kind of surprising that we do not need to insert
{\sf v}(\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}})\subset\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}}
in (
3.7
) as it will be clear in the proof.

Proof.

Proof of
\subset
: If
{\sf v}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
then
{\sf v}({{\mathcal{I}}})\subset{{\mathcal{I}}}
, and since
{\sf v}
is algebraic, that is

{\sf v}({\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T})\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
), we get

{\sf v}(\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}})\subset\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}}
. Moreover, by definition of a leaf scheme we have
\Omega={\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}d{{\mathcal{I}}}
modulo
{{\mathcal{I}}}\Omega_{\sf T}
(and after tensoring with holomorphic functions). Taking
{\sf v}
from both sides we have
{\sf v}(\Omega)\subset{{\mathcal{I}}}
, and since
{\sf v}(\Omega)\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
, we get

{\sf v}(\Omega)\subset\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}}
.

Proof of
\supset
: Let
{\sf v}\in\Theta_{{\sf T}}
with
{\sf v}(\Omega)\subset\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}}
.
By definition of a leaf scheme
d{{\mathcal{I}}}
is in
\Omega\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},{t}}+{{\mathcal{I}}}\Omega_{\sf T}
.
Therefore,
{\sf v}({{\mathcal{I}}})
is in
{\sf v}(\Omega){\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},{t}}+{{\mathcal{I}}}
. Since we have
{\sf v}(\Omega)\subset\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}}\subset{{\mathcal{I}}}
we get the result.

∎

Proposition 3.4
.

If there is an element of
\Theta_{\sf T}(\Omega)
which is not a zero divisor (in particular if
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
has no zero divisors and
\Theta\subsetneq\Theta_{\sf T}
)
then the following are equivalent:

1.

L
is Zariski dense in
{\sf T}
.

2.

\Theta=\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
.

Proof.

1. implies 2.: If
L
is Zariski dense then
\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}}=0
and this follows from (
3.7
).
2. implies 1.:
Assume that
L
is not Zariski dense. The algebraic ideal

\bar{}{{\mathcal{I}}}
has a non-zero element
f
, and
f\Theta_{{\sf T}}\subset\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
. If
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}=\Theta
then

f\Theta_{{\sf T}}(\Omega)=0
. But by our assumption,
\Theta_{{\sf T}}(\Omega)
has an element which is not a zero divisor and we get a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 3.5
.

We have a natural
{\mathfrak{R}}
-bilinear pairing
\Omega_{\sf T}\times{{\bf T}}_{t}{\sf T}\to{\mathfrak{R}},\ (\omega,v)\mapsto\omega(v)
such that

{{\bf T}}_{t}L=\{v\in{{\bf T}}_{t}{\sf T}\ \mid\ \Omega(v)=0\}.

Proof.

First, let us define the pairing. For
f,g\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
and
\omega=fdg
we define
\omega(v):=f_{0}v(g-g_{0})
, where
g_{0}=g({t}),\ f_{0}=f({t})
. Since
\Omega_{\sf T}
as
{\mathfrak{R}}
-module is generated by
fdg
’s, the definition extend to
\Omega^{1}_{\sf T}
. It is well-defined because

\displaystyle(d(fg)-fdg-gdf)(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v(d((f-f_{0}+f_{0})(g-g_{0}+g_{0})-f_{0}g_{0}))-f_{0}v(g-g_{0})-g_{0}v(f-f_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v((f-f_{0})(g-g_{0}))=0.

If
v\in{{\bf T}}_{t}L
then we know that
{{\bf T}}_{t}L=\left(\frac{{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},t}}{{{\mathcal{I}}}+{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},{t}}^{2}}\right)^{\vee}
, and so we get a map
v:{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},t}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
with
v({{\mathcal{I}}})=0
. We use the definition of a leaf scheme and we have
\Omega\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},t}d{{\mathcal{I}}}+{{\mathcal{I}}}\Omega_{\sf T}
. Applying
v
to both sides we get
\Omega(v)=0
. Conversely if we have
v\in{{\bf T}}_{t}{\sf T}
with
\Omega(v)=0
then we use again the definition of leaf scheme and we have
d{{\mathcal{I}}}\subset\Omega\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},t}+{{\mathcal{I}}}\Omega_{\sf T}
. Applying
v
to both sides we conclude that
v({{\mathcal{I}}})=0

∎

Proposition 3.6
.

The evaluation at
{t}
induces a map
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\to{{\bf T}}_{t}L
and if
L
is a general leaf then it is surjective.

Proof.

Let
{\sf v}\in\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
and so
{\sf v}({{\mathcal{I}}})\subset{{\mathcal{I}}}
. This implies that
{\sf v}({{\mathcal{I}}})({t})=0
and so

{\sf v}({t})\in{{\bf T}}_{t}L
. Therefore, the evaluation at
{t}
induces a map
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\to{{\bf T}}_{t}L
. We have

\Theta\subset\Theta_{{\sf T},L}
, and if
L
is a general leaf we claim that
\Theta\to{{\bf T}}_{t}L
is surjective. In order to see this, we first define
\Theta^{\rm hol}:=\{{\sf v}\in\Theta_{{\sf T}^{{\rm hol}},{t}}\ \mid\ {\sf v}(\Omega)=0\}
. Since
{t}
is a smooth point of
{\sf T}
, we have a holomorphic coordinate system given by the Frobenius theorem and in this coordinate system
the surjectivity of
\Theta^{{\rm hol}}\to{{\bf T}}_{t}L
can be checked easily. The proof finishes with

\Theta\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}}{{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},{t}}}=\Theta^{\rm hol}
which is just a linear algebra.

∎

Remark 3.3
.

Note that the kernel of
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\to{{\bf T}}_{t}L
contains vector fields
{\sf v}
such that

{\sf v}({t})=0
. In this case
{t}
is called the singularity of
{\sf v}
.
Moreover, for an arbitrary leaf scheme
L
, the map
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\to{{\bf T}}_{t}L
may not be surjective. For instance, let

{\sf T}:=\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\mathfrak{R}}
with the coordinates
(x,y)
,
L:xy=0
and
\Omega={\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}(xdy+ydx)
. It can be easily checked that
L
is a leaf scheme of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
,
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}=\{xf\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+yg\frac{\partial}{\partial y},\ \ f,g\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}\}
,
{{\bf T}}_{0}L={{\bf T}}_{0}{\sf T}
and
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\to{{\bf T}}_{t}L
is the zero map.

3.6
Foliations of linear differential equations

Let
{V}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme and
{\sf B}
be a
{\sf h}\times{\sf h}
matrix with entries which are global sections of

\Omega^{1}_{V}
. Let also
0
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued smooth point of
{V}
.
Recall that
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}:={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
and if
{\mathfrak{R}}\subset\mathbb{C}
then this is the smallest subring of
\mathbb{C}
containing both
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
\mathbb{Q}
. The first fundamental theorem of linear differential equations is:

Theorem 3.2
.

For any
y_{0}\in{\mathfrak{R}}^{\sf h}
, the linear differential equation
dy={\sf B}y
has a unique solution

y\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\rm hol},0}^{\sf h}
with
y(0)=y_{0}
if and only if
{\sf B}
satisfies the integrability condition
d{\sf B}={\sf B}\wedge{\sf B}
.
Moreover, if
{\sf B}
is defined over a ring
{\mathfrak{R}}\subset\mathbb{C}
then
y_{i}
’s are convergent.

Proof.

The direction
\Rightarrow
is easy and we only prove this.
We take a basis
e_{i},\ i=1,2,\ldots,{\sf h}
of
{\mathfrak{R}}^{\sf h}
and find
{\sf h}
linearly independent solutions
y_{i},\ y_{i}(0)=e_{i}\ i=1,2,3,\ldots,{\sf h}
.
We put all
{\sf h}
solutions
y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{{\sf h}}
inside a
{\sf h}\times{\sf h}
matrix
Y=[y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{\sf h}]
, and by our hypothesis on the initial values of
y_{i}
’s, we have
\det(Y(0))\not=0
and so
{\sf B}=dY\cdot Y^{-1}
. Therefore,

d{\sf B}=-dY\cdot d(Y^{-1})=dY\cdot Y^{-1}\cdot dY\cdot Y^{-1}={\sf B}\wedge{\sf B}.

∎

Let us consider new variables
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{{\sf h}}
, and define

O={\rm Spec}\left({\mathfrak{R}}[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{{\sf h}}]\right).

The entries of
dx-{\sf B}x
are differential forms in
{\sf T}:={V}\times_{\mathfrak{R}}O
.
If
{\sf B}
is integrable in the sense of
Theorem

3.2
then the
{\mathcal{O}}_{V}
-module generated by the entries of
dx-{\sf B}x
is integrable. This follows from

d(dx-{\sf B}x)=-d({\sf B}x)={\sf B}\wedge dx-(d{\sf B})x={\sf B}\wedge(dx-{\sf B}x)+({\sf B}\wedge{\sf B}-d{\sf B})x.

We denote the corresponding foliation in
{\sf T}
by
{\mathcal{F}}(dx-{\sf B}x)
.

Proposition 3.7
.

All the leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}(dx-{\sf B}x)
are general and hence smooth.

Proof.

First note that for a point
(0,y_{0})\in{\sf T}:={V}\times O
, there is a unique solution
y({t})
of
dy={\sf B}y,\ y(0)=y_{0}
. This gives us the leaf
L
whose ideal is generated by the entries of
x-y(t)
.
The linear part of the entries of
x-y(t)
is
x
minus the linear part of
y(t)
at
0
.
These are trivially
{\sf h}
linearly independent functions due to the presence of
x
.
∎

Let
W
be a subvariety of
V
. It turns out that the foliation

{\mathcal{F}}(dx-{\sf B}x)\cap W:={\mathcal{F}}((dx-{\sf B}x)|_{W})

might have non-smooth leaves and one of the main goals of the present text is to study such foliations. The main example for
W
is
x_{1}=x_{2}=\cdots=x_{k}=0
for some
k<n
.

Gauss-Manin connection

Let
Y\to{V}
be a family of smooth complex projective varieties over the field
{\mathfrak{k}}
and let
{V}
be irreducible, smooth and affine. Around any point of
{V}
we can find global
sections
\omega
of the
{m}
-th relative de Rham cohomology sheaf of
Y/{V}
such that
\omega_{i},i=1,2,\ldots,{\sf h}
at each fiber

H_{\rm dR}^{m}(Y_{t}),\ t\in{V}
form a basis compatible with the Hodge filtration. If it is necessary we may
replace
{V}
with a Zariski open subset of
{V}
. We write the Gauss-Manin connection of
Y/{V}
in the basis
\omega=[\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\ldots,\omega_{{\sf h}}]^{{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{r}}}
:
\nabla\omega={\sf B}\otimes\omega
, where
{\sf B}
is
{{\sf h}}\times{{\sf h}}
matrix with entries which are differential forms in
{V}
. As all our objects
Y\to{V},\ {t},\ \omega_{i}
etc. use a finite number of coefficients in
{\mathfrak{k}}
, we can take a model of all these over a finitely generated ring
{\mathfrak{R}}
so that
{\mathfrak{k}}
is the quotient field of
{\mathfrak{R}}
. For simplicity, we use the same notations for these objects defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}\subset\mathbb{C}
.
The linear differential equation
dy={\sf B}y
for an unknown
{{\sf h}}\times 1
matrix
y
with entries which are holomorphic functions in
({V},{t})
, has a basis of solutions given by
\int_{\delta_{t}}\omega
, where
\delta_{t}
ranges in a basis of
H_{m}(Y_{t},\mathbb{Z})
.

This is classical statement in Hodge theory, see for instance or
[
70
, Theorem 9.3]
.

3.7
Foliations attached to Hodge loci

In this section, we write the expression of a foliation in
{\sf T}:=F^{\frac{{m}}{2}}H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y/V)
with Hodge loci as leaf schemes.
A version of these foliations developed in
[
67
, Chapter 5,6]
is not suitable for our purpose and we mainly use the version in
[
65
]
.

Let
{\sf h}:={\sf h}^{{m},0}+\cdots+{\sf h}^{\frac{{m}}{2},\frac{{m}}{2}}+\cdots+{\sf h}^{0,{m}}
be the decomposition of
{\sf h}
into Hodge numbers of
H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y_{t})
and
{\sf h}^{i}:={\sf h}^{{m},0}+\cdots+{\sf h}^{i,{m}-i}
.
We take variables
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{{\sf h}^{\frac{{m}}{2}}}
and put them in a
{\sf h}\times 1
matrix
x
as below.
The first
\frac{{m}}{2}
Hodge blocks of
x
are zero and
x_{i}
’s are listed in the next blocks:

(3.8)

x=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
x^{\frac{{m}}{2}}\\
\vdots\\
x^{{m}}\end{pmatrix}.

Here,
x^{i}
is a
{\sf h}^{{m}-i,i}\times 1
matrix.
We take
{\sf C}
the constant matrix which is obtained by replacing
x_{i}
with
0
in
x
except for
x_{1}
, which is replaced with
1
(this is the first coordinate of
x^{\frac{{m}}{2}}
).
Let
{\sf S}
be a Hodge block lower triangular
{\sf h}\times{\sf h}

matrix which is obtained from the identity matrix by replacing the
{\sf h}^{\frac{{m}}{2}+1}+1
column with
x
. It is defined in this way to have the equality

(3.9)

{\sf S}\cdot{\sf C}=x.

In this way
{\sf S}^{-1}
is obtained from
{\sf S}
by
replacing
x_{1}
with
x_{1}^{-1}
and
x_{i},\ i\geq 2
with

-x_{i}x_{1}^{-1}
. Note that
\det({\sf S})=x_{1}
. Define

O:={\rm Spec}\left({\mathfrak{R}}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{{\sf h}^{\frac{{m}}{2}}},\frac{1}{x_{1}}\right]\right).

We consider the family
{\sf X}\to{\sf T}
, where
{\sf X}:=Y\times O,\ {\sf T}:={V}\times O
. It is obtained from

Y\to{V}
and the identity map
O\to O
. We also define
\alpha
by

(3.10)

\alpha:={\sf S}^{-1}\cdot\omega.

Let
\nabla:H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y/{V})\to\Omega_{V}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y/{V})
be the algebraic Gauss-Manin connection. We can write
\nabla

in the basis
\omega
and define the
{\sf h}\times{\sf h}
matrix
{\sf B}
by the equality:

\nabla\omega={\sf B}\otimes\omega.

The entries of
{\sf B}
are differential 1-forms in
{V}
. In a similar way we can compute the Gauss-Manin connection of
{\sf X}/{\sf T}
in the basis

\alpha
:

(3.11)

\nabla\alpha={\sf A}\otimes\alpha,\ \ {\sf A}=-{\sf S}^{-1}d{\sf S}+{\sf S}^{-1}\cdot{\sf B}\cdot{\sf S}.

This follows from the construction of the global sections
\alpha
in (
3.10
) and the Leibniz rule.
We call
{\sf B}
(resp.
{\sf A}
) the Gauss-Manin connection matrix of the pair
(Y/{V},\omega)
(resp.
({\sf X}/{\sf T},\alpha)
).
From the integrability of the Gauss-Manin connection it follows that

(3.12)

d{\sf A}={\sf A}\wedge{\sf A}.

Definition 3.8
.

The entries of
{\sf A}{\sf C}
induce a holomorphic foliation
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
in
{\sf T}
. The integrability
follows from
(
3.12
):

d({\sf A}\cdot{\sf C})=d{\sf A}\cdot{\sf C}={\sf A}\wedge({\sf A}\cdot{\sf C}).

Proposition 3.8
.

The foliation
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
in
{\sf T}
is given by

(3.13)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sf B}^{\frac{{m}}{2}-1,\frac{{m}}{2}}x^{\frac{{m}}{2}}

(3.14)

\displaystyle dx^{\frac{{m}}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sf B}^{\frac{{m}}{2},\frac{{m}}{2}}x^{\frac{{m}}{2}}+{\sf B}^{\frac{{m}}{2},\frac{{m}}{2}+1}x^{\frac{{m}}{2}+1},

(3.15)

\displaystyle dx^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=\frac{{m}}{2}}^{m}{\sf B}^{i,j}x^{j},\ \ \ \ \ i=\frac{{m}}{2}+1,\ldots,{m}.

Proof.

For this we use (
3.11
) and we conclude that
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
is given by

(-{\sf S}^{-1}d{\sf S}+{\sf S}^{-1}\cdot{\sf B}\cdot{\sf S}){\sf C}
. Since
{\sf C}

is a constant vector and
{\sf S}
is an invertible matrix and we have (
3.9
),
we conclude that
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
is given by the entries of

dx-{\sf B}x=0.

Opening this equality and
using the zero blocks of
x
in (
3.9
) we
get (
3.13
), (
3.14
) and (
3.15
).
Note that by Griffiths transversality
{\sf B}^{i,j}=0
for
j-i\geq 2
.
∎

Let
\delta_{t}\in H_{m}({\sf X}_{t},\mathbb{Q})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C},\ t\in({\sf T},0)

be a continuous family of cycles, that is, the Poincaré dual of
\delta_{t}
is a flat section
of the Gauss-Manin connection:
\nabla\delta_{t}=0
. Here,
({\sf T},0)
is a small neighborhood of
0
in
{\sf T}
in
the usual topology.

Proposition 3.9
.

The following

(3.16)

L_{\delta_{t}}:=\left\{\ \ \ t\in({\sf T},0)\ \ \ |\ \ \ \int_{\delta_{t}}\alpha={\sf C}.\right\},

is a leaf scheme of
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
. In other words, the ideal

{{\mathcal{I}}}_{\delta_{t}}
generated by the entries of
\int_{\delta_{t}}\alpha-{\sf C}
gives a leaf scheme of
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
. Moreover,
\delta_{t}
is a general Hodge cycle if and only if
0
is a smooth point of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, and hence,
L_{\delta_{t}}
is general.

Proof.

We have the holomorphic function

f:({\sf T},0)\to\mathbb{C}^{{\sf h}},\ \ f(t):=\int_{\delta_{t}}\alpha-{\sf C}

which satisfies

(3.17)

df=\int_{\delta_{t}}\nabla\alpha={\sf A}\cdot\int_{\delta_{t}}\alpha={\sf A}\cdot{\sf C}+{\sf A}\cdot f.

This implies that

L_{\delta_{t}}
is a
leaf scheme of
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
. For the second part, note that

{\sf B}^{\frac{{m}}{2}-1,\frac{{m}}{2}}x^{\frac{{m}}{2}}
is just the matrix format of
\alpha
in (
3.3
), see

[
65
, Proposition 5]
. The singular locus of
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
corresponds to those
t\in{\sf T}
such that the kernel of the entries of
{\sf B}^{\frac{{m}}{2}-1,\frac{{m}}{2}}x^{\frac{{m}}{2}}
is not minimal, and the statement follows.
∎

One might conjecture that all the leaf schemes of
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
are of the form
L_{\delta_{t}}
.
As we do not need this kind of statements, we leave it to the reader.

Definition 3.9
.

The Hodge locus with constant periods
{\sf C}
is defined to be
L_{\delta_{t}}
in (
3.16
)
with
\delta_{t}\in H^{m}({\sf X}_{t},\mathbb{Q})
. Its ideal is given by

{{\mathcal{I}}}_{\delta_{t}}:=\left\langle\int_{\delta_{t}}\alpha-{\sf C}\right\rangle=\left\langle\int_{\delta_{t}}\omega-x\right\rangle\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol},0}.

From the zero blocks of
{\sf C}
, it follows that the Poincaré dual
\delta_{t}^{\sf pd}
of
\delta_{t}
is in
H^{\frac{{m}}{2},\frac{{m}}{2}}\cap H^{{m}}({\sf X}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
and so
\delta_{t}
is a Hodge cycle in the classical sense.

Remark 3.4
.

For the example mentioned in the Introduction, we take
E_{z}:y^{2}=x(x-1)(x-t)
the Legendre family of elliptic curves, and
Y_{t_{1},t_{2}}:=E_{t_{1}}\times E_{t_{2}}
. The de Rham cohomology
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(Y_{t_{1},t_{2}})
modulo the cohomology class of fibers of projections in each factor, is four dimensional with Hodge numbers
1,2,1
. The loci of isogenies of degree
N
between
E_{t_{1}}
and
E_{t_{2}}
is an algebraic curve and we can construct the corresponding leaf scheme and foliation in
(t_{1},t_{2},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{C}^{5}
. For a more conceptual treatment of this example see
[
67
, Chapter 10]
.

3.8
The definition ring of Hodge loci

Let
Y\to{V}
be a family of smooth projective varieties over
\mathbb{C}
and take a smooth model of this over
{\mathfrak{R}}\subset\mathbb{C}
.
Let also
0
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{V}
. We take a topological cycle

\delta_{0}\in H_{m}(Y_{0},\mathbb{Z})
and enlarge
{\mathfrak{R}}
to

{\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0}):={\mathfrak{R}}\left[\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{\frac{m}{2}}}\int_{\delta_{0}}\omega,\omega\in H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y_{0}/{\mathfrak{R}})\right],

that is,
{\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0})
is the ring of polynomials in the periods
(2\pi i)^{-\frac{m}{2}}\int_{\delta_{0}}\omega,\omega\in H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y_{0}/{\mathfrak{R}})
. Note that these numbers are conjecturally in
\bar{\mathfrak{k}}
, where
{\mathfrak{k}}
is the quotient field of
{\mathfrak{R}}
. This follows from the Hodge conjecture or if we assume that
\delta_{0}
is an absolute Hodge cycle.
We will not need this for our investigation, and hence,
{\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0})

might contain new transcendental numbers.
Consider the monodromy
\delta_{t}\in H_{m}(Y_{t},\mathbb{Z})
of
\delta_{0}
to nearby fibers
t\in({V},0)
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
0
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{V}
and
\omega\in H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y/{V})
. Then the Taylor series of
\int_{\delta_{t}}\omega
at
0
has coefficients in
{\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0})_{\mathbb{Q}}:={\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
, that is,

\int_{\delta_{t}}\omega\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{V}^{\rm hol}_{{\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0})_{\mathbb{Q}}},0}.

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem

3.3
and
Theorem

3.2
. The main ingredient is that
the Gauss-Manin connection matrix of of
Y/{V}
is defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
.
∎

It might be too naive to believe that if
\delta_{0}
is a Hodge cycle then it has coefficients in
{\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0})
itself, that is,
\int_{\delta_{t}}\omega\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{V}^{\rm hol}_{{\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0})},0}
. In
Section

3.10
we experimentally observe that this is false.

Theorem 3.5
.

The Hodge locus
L_{\delta_{0}}
defined in
Definition

3.9
is defined over the ring
{\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0})[\frac{1}{N}]
for some
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0})
.

Using
Proposition

3.9
this theorem is a particular case of
Conjecture

3.1
.
Actually
Conjecture

3.1
is inspired by
Theorem

3.5
.

Proof.

The Cattani-Deligne-Kaplan theorem, see
[
15
, Theorem 1.1]
, implies that there is an algebraic subvariety
L
of
{\sf T}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
L_{\delta_{t}}
is just an analytic germ/small open subset of
L
. By monodromy of
\delta_{t}
along any path in
L
, we can see that
L
is covered by such open sets. Therefore,
L
has the structure of an analytic scheme given by analytic ideals
{{\mathcal{I}}}_{\delta_{t}}
. We have to consider a compactification
\bar{\sf T}_{\mathbb{C}}
and see that the analytic scheme structure of
L
extends to
\bar{L}
. This is not explicitly mentioned in
[
15
]
, but their proof implies this. Now by GAGA for analytic subschemes of projective varieties we conclude that
{{\mathcal{I}}}_{\delta_{t}}
is algebraic.
Proposition

3.1
finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{k}}
be the quotient field of
{\mathfrak{R}}
. If
\delta_{0}
is an absolute Hodge cycle then all its periods
\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{\frac{m}{2}}}\int_{\delta_{0}}\omega,\omega\in H^{m}_{\rm dR}(Y_{0})
are in the algebraic closure
\bar{\mathfrak{k}}
of
{\mathfrak{k}}
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
is defined over
{\mathfrak{k}}
, by taking the action of the Galois group
{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathfrak{k}}/{\mathfrak{k}})
on the coefficients of
L
and the base point
(0,x_{0})
, we get finitely many leaf schemes of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
which come from Hodge cycles too. If
\delta_{0}
is not absolute then some of the period of
\delta_{0}
are transcendental numbers, that it they do not belong to
\bar{\mathfrak{k}}
. In this case by some standard arguments, see for instance
[
67
, Theorem 5.18]
, we can transform such transcendental numbers into variables and get continuous families of algebraic leaf schemes for
{\mathcal{F}}({\sf C})
. In general it is open whether such leaf schemes come from Hodge cycles or not. For some related results see
[
67
, Section 7.5]
.

3.9
Proofs

Proof.

(of
Corollary

3.1
)
The proof starts with
Theorem

3.1
which together with
Proposition

3.1
implies
Theorem

3.5
. We conclude that a Hodge locus is defined over a finitely generated subring of
\mathbb{C}
and in the same way as in the proof of
Proposition

3.2
, we conclude that for all but a finite number of primes
reduction modulo
p
of
L
makes sense and the result follows.
∎

Proof.

(of
Corollary

3.2
)
It is well-known that

{{\bf T}}_{t}L=\{v\in{{\bf T}}_{t}{\sf T}|\alpha(v,\bar{t})=0\},

see for instance,
[
14
]
. In other words,
v\in{{\bf T}}_{t}L
is characterised by the fact that
the infinitesimal monodromy of
\bar{t}
along the vector
v
is still in
{\sf T}
. If the Hodge cycle is general then by
Proposition

3.9
its Hodge locus is a general leaf scheme and by
Proposition

3.6
, the map
\Theta_{{\sf T},L}\to{{\bf T}}_{t}L
is surjective. This together with
Corollary

3.1
finishes the proof.
∎

3.10
Taylor series of periods over Hodge cycles

In order to prove
Corollary

3.1
and
Corollary

3.2
and verify the hypothesis of
Conjecture

3.1
by a local analysis, we have to investigate the defining ideal of Hodge loci. These are given explicitly in terms of periods, and the most general fact about their ring of definition is
Theorem

3.4
which is not enough.
In this section we give closed formulas for such coefficients for families of hypersurfaces near the Fermat variety and experimentally observe that all primes might be inverted in the Taylor series of periods, and hence, an strategy is needed to modify them and obtain new generators of the defining ideal of Hodge loci.

Let us consider the hypersurface
X_{t}
in the projective space

\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
given by the homogeneous polynomial:

(3.18)

f_{t}:=-x_{0}^{d}+x_{1}^{d}-x_{2}^{d}+x_{3}^{d}+\cdots-x_{n}^{d}+x_{n+1}^{d}-\sum_{\alpha}t_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}=0,\ \

t=(t_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in I}\in({\sf T},0),

where
\alpha
runs through a finite subset
I
of

\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n+2}
with
\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}\alpha_{i}=d
.
For a rational number
r

let
[r]
be the integer part of
r
, that is
[r]\leq r<[r]+1
, and
\{r\}:=r-[r]
. Let
also
(x)_{y}:=x(x+1)(x+2)\cdots(x+y-1),\ (x)_{0}:=1
be the Pochhammer symbol.
We compute the Taylor series of
the integration of differential forms
over monodromies of the algebraic cycle

{\mathbb{P}}^{\frac{n}{2}}:x_{0}-x_{1}=x_{2}-x_{3}=\cdots=x_{n}-x_{n+1=0},

inside the Fermat variety
X_{0}
.
The following has been proved in
[
66
, Theorem 18.9]

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
\delta_{t}\in H_{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Z}),\ t\in({\sf T},0)
be the monodromy (parallel transport) of the cycle

\delta_{0}:=[{\mathbb{P}}^{\frac{n}{2}}]\in H_{n}(X_{0},\mathbb{Z})
along a path which
connects
0
to
t
.
For a monomial
x^{\beta}=x_{0}^{\beta_{0}}x_{1}^{\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{\beta_{2}}\cdots x_{n+1}^{\beta_{n+1}}
with

k:=\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}\frac{\beta_{i}+1}{d}\in\mathbb{N}

we have

(3.19)

\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}\cdot d^{\frac{n}{2}+1}\cdot(k-1)!}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{\frac{n}{2}}}{{\int}}_{\delta_{t}}{\rm Resi}\left(\frac{x^{\beta}\Omega}{f^{k}_{t}}\right)={{{\sum}}}_{a:I{}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{(-1)^{E_{\beta+a^{*}}}D_{\beta+a^{*}}}{a!}\cdot t^{a},

where the sum runs through all
\#I{}
-tuples
a=(a_{\alpha},\ \ \alpha\in I{})

of non-negative integers such that for
\check{\beta}:=\beta+a^{*}
we have

(3.20)

\left\{\frac{\check{\beta}_{2e}+1}{d}\right\}+\left\{\frac{\check{\beta}_{2e+1}+1}{d}\right\}=1,\ \ \ \forall e=0,1,\cdots,\frac{n}{2},

and

\displaystyle t^{a}:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in I{}}t_{\alpha}^{a_{\alpha}},\ \ \ \ a!:=\prod_{\alpha\in I{}}a_{\alpha}!,\ \ \ \ a^{*}:=\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}\cdot\alpha,

\displaystyle D_{\check{\beta}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\prod}_{i=0}^{n+1}\left(\left\{\frac{\check{\beta}_{i}+1}{d}\right\}\right)_{\left[\frac{\check{\beta}_{i}+1}{d}\right]},\ \ \ \ E_{\check{\beta}}:=\sum_{e=0}^{\frac{n}{2}}\left[\frac{\check{\beta}_{2e}+1}{d}\right].

Note that for two types of
a
the coefficient of
t^{a}
in (
3.19
) is zero.
First, when
\beta+a^{*}
does not satisfy (
3.20
).
Second, when an entry of
\beta+a^{*}
plus one is divisible by
d

(this is hidden in the definition of
D_{\beta+a^{*}}
). The coefficients of
the Taylor series are in
\mathbb{Q}
. The Taylor series (
3.1
) is just obtained from
(
3.19
) by setting
n=2,\ d=4,\ \beta=0,\ k=1
. Since in this case
H^{2,0}
is one dimensional and it is generated by
\omega:={\rm Resi}\left(\frac{\Omega}{f_{t}}\right)
, the Hodge locus in
V
corresponding to the homology class of the line
\mathbb{P}^{1}:x_{0}-x_{1}=x_{2}-x_{3}=0
is given by the zero locus of
\int_{\delta_{t}}\omega
whose Taylor series at
{t}=0
is given in (
3.1
).

In the following, we use the computer implementation of (
3.20
) and its general format in
[
66
, Theorem 18.9]
. The full family of hypersurfaces has too much parameters, and so, one has to consider lower truncation of the Taylor series.
Our main goal is to show that the natural generators of the ideal of a Hodge locus, are not necessarily defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}(\delta_{0})
and one has to invert infinitely many primes. In particular, in
Conjecture

3.1
the natural generators of the ideal might not prove this conjecture, and that is why, in this conjecture we have claimed that the ideal is defined over the ring
{\mathfrak{R}}
and not its natural generators. For example, we consider the family

X:\ \ x_{1}^{4}+x_{2}^{4}+x_{3}^{4}+x_{0}^{4}-t_{0}x_{0}x_{1}^{3}-t_{1}x_{1}x_{2}^{3}-t_{2}x_{2}x_{3}^{3}-t_{3}x_{3}x_{0}^{3}=0.

In this case all the Griffiths basis of differential forms for
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X_{t})

has Taylor series with primes appearing in their denominators, but very slowly. For truncation with degree
\leq 30
we get the following denominator:

1,2^{84}\cdot 5\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2},2^{86}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}^{2},2^{84}\cdot 11:x_{0}x_{2}x_{3}^{2},2^{8}5\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{1}^{2}x_{3}^{2},2^{86}\cdot 11\cdot 13:

x_{0}x_{1}x_{3}^{2},2^{86}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{0}^{2}x_{3}^{2},2^{86}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3},2^{85}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{0}x_{2}^{2}x_{3},2^{85}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{1}^{2}x_{2}x_{3},2^{86}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:

x_{0}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3},2^{85}\cdot 11:x_{0}^{2}x_{2}x_{3},2^{86}\cdot 11:x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{3},2^{84}\cdot 11:x_{0}^{2}x_{1}x_{3},2^{85}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2},2^{86}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:

x_{0}x_{1}x_{2}^{2},2^{86}\cdot 11:x_{0}^{2}x_{2}^{2},2^{86}\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{2},2^{85}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{0}^{2}x_{1}x_{2},2^{85}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:x_{0}^{2}x_{1}^{2},2^{86}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:

x_{0}^{2}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2},2^{88}\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13:

where we have written the monomial
x^{\beta}
in

\frac{x^{\beta}\Omega}{f^{k}_{t}}
and then the denominator of its period, separated by two points. For the computer code used for this computation, see

the author’s webpage here
or the latex text of the present paper in arxiv.

3.11
Hodge-Tate varieties

One of the main goals of the present text has been to elaborate a generalization of Grothendieck-Katz conjecture which implies the algebraicity of the Hodge loci. This is also a consequence of the Hodge conjecture. It turns out that we can formulate another statement on the algebraicity of periods which is a common consequence of both Hodge and the original Grothendieck-Katz conjecture.

Definition 3.10
.

The
m
-th cohomology of a smooth projective variety
X
is called of Hodge-Tate type if

H^{n}_{\rm dR}(X)=H^{\frac{m}{2},\frac{m}{2}}
. In other words,

H^{p}(X,\Omega_{X}^{q})=0,\ \forall p+q=m,\ \ p\not=q.

As the Hodge conjecture can be reduced to middle cohomology, we say that a variety is of Hodge-Tate type if its middle cohomology is of Hodge-Tate type. The following very particular case of the Hodge conjecture is still open.

Conjecture 3.5
.

Hodge conjecture is true for Hodge-Tate varieties
X
, that is, there are algebraic cycles
Z_{i},\ i=1,2,\ldots,a
in
X
of dimension
\frac{m}{2}
such that
[Z_{i}]
’s generate the homology group
H_{m}(X,\mathbb{Q})
.

Note that for a Hodge-Tate variety all the cycles
\delta\in H_{m}(X_{z},\mathbb{Z})
are Hodge.

Conjecture 3.6
.

For a Hodge-Tate variety
X
defined over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\delta\in H_{m}(X,\mathbb{Z})
we have

\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{\frac{m}{2}}}\int_{\delta_{z}}\omega\in\bar{\mathbb{Q}},\ \forall\omega\in H^{m}_{\rm dR}(X/\bar{\mathbb{Q}}).

This is a consequence of the Hodge conjecture, see
[
25
, Proposition 1.5]
.
A complex version of the above statement is as follows.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
X_{z},\ z\in V
be a family of varieties of Hodge-Tate type and let

\omega\in H^{m}_{\rm dR}(X/V)
. The holomorphic multi-valued function
\int_{\delta_{z}}\omega
is algebraic (as a function in
z
).

Proof.

Both Hodge and Grothendieck-Katz conjectures imply
Theorem

3.7
.
Hodge conjecture is not known for varieties of Hodge-Tate type and Grothendieck-Katz conjecture for Gauss-Manin connections and its factors is known in
[
47
]
.

If the Hodge conjecture for Hodge-Tate varieties is true then
\delta_{z}=[Z_{z}]
and the result follows from a version of
[
25
, Proposition 1.5]
for families of algebraic cycles.

Let
a:=\#\{(p,q)|p+q=m,\ h^{p,q}(X_{t})\not=0\}
. N. Katz in
[
46
, Corollary 7.5, page 383]
proves that the
p
-curvature of the Gauss-Manin connection of
X/V
is nilpotent of order at most
a
. He attributes this theorem to P. Deligne. If
X/V
is of Hodge-Tate type then
a=1
and so
we know that the
p
-curvature of the Gauss-Manin connection of
X/V
is zero.
The Grothendieck-Katz conjecture for Gauss-Manin connections and its factors is known in
[
47
]
,
and so the solutions to the Gauss-Manin connection as a differential equation are algebraic.
These are exactly the periods
\int_{\delta_{z}}\omega
, see
Theorem

3.3
.
∎

As far as the author is aware of it, there is no classification of Hodge-Tate varieties, and
Conjecture

3.5
is as open as the Hodge conjecture itself. Toric varieties are Hodge-Tate varieties, and we can look for complete intersections in Toric varieties which are Hodge-Tate. For instance, let
X
be a smooth hypersurface of degree
d
in the weighted projective space

\mathbb{P}^{v_{0},v_{1},\cdots,v_{n+1}}
. By a result of J. Steenbrink in
[
83
]
we know that for
v_{0}=1
if
\frac{n}{2}\leq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}v_{i}}{d}
then
X
is of Hodge-Tate type. For an example of this, and the resulting algebraic periods see
[
66
, Section 16.9]
.

Chapter 4
Ramanujan vector field

On se propose de donner un dictionnaire heuristique entre énoncés en cohomologie

l
-adique et énoncés en théorie de Hodge. Ce dictionnaire a notamment pour sources […]
et la théorie conjecturale des motifs de Grothendieck […]. Jusqu’ici, il a surtout servi
a formuler, en théorie de Hodge, des conjectures, et il en a parfois suggéré une démonstrdtion,
(P. Deligne in
[
26
]
).

Abstract:
In this article we prove that for all primes
p\not=2,3
, the Ramanujan vector field has an invariant algebraic curve and then
we give a moduli space interpretation of this curve in terms of Cartier operator acting on the de Rham cohomology of elliptic curves. The main ingredients of our study are due to Serre, Swinnerton-Dyer and Katz in 1973.
We aim to generalize this for the theory of Calabi-Yau modular forms, which includes the generating function of genus
g
Gromov-Witten invariants.
The integrality of
q
-expansions of such modular forms is still a main conjecture which has been only established for special Calabi-Yau varieties, for instance those whose periods are hypergeometric functions. For this the main tools are Dwork’s theorem.
We present an alternative project which aims to prove such integralities using modular vector fields and Gauss-Manin connection in positive characteristic.

4.1
Introduction

We are going to study algebraic leaves of the Ramanujan vector field

(4.1)

{\sf v}:=(t_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{12}t_{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}+(4t_{1}t_{2}-6t_{3})\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{2}}+(6t_{1}t_{3}-\frac{1}{3}t_{2}^{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{3}}

in characteristic
p\not=2,3
. All the algebraic leaves of this vector field over
\mathbb{C}
are inside the hypersurface
27t_{3}^{2}-t_{2}^{3}=0
and it has a transcendental solution given by the Eisenstein series
a_{1}E_{2},a_{2}E_{4},a_{3}E_{6}
, where
a=(-\frac{1}{12},\frac{1}{12},-\frac{1}{216})
, and that is why it carries this name.
We consider it as a vector field in

\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathfrak{R}}={\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}}[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}])
, where
{\mathfrak{R}}
is a ring of characteristic zero with
2
and
3
invertible. We usually take
{\mathfrak{R}}=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{6}]
and so
{\mathfrak{R}}/p{\mathfrak{R}}=\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Let
{\mathfrak{k}}
be the quotient field of
{\mathfrak{R}}
. In
[
63
]
the author gave a moduli space interpretation of the Ramanujan vector field:

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
{\sf T}
be the moduli space of triples
(E,\alpha,\omega)
, where
E
is an elliptic curve defined over
{\mathfrak{k}}
and
\alpha,\omega
form a basis of
H^{1}_{\rm dR}(E/{\mathfrak{k}})
with

\alpha\in F^{1}H^{1}_{\rm dR}(E/{\mathfrak{k}})
and
\langle\alpha,\omega\rangle=1
.
This moduli space is the affine variety

{\sf T}={\rm Spec}\mathbb{Q}[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},\frac{1}{\Delta}],\ \Delta:=27t_{3}^{2}-t_{2}^{3},

and we have a universal family over
{\sf T}
given by
{\sf E}\to{\sf T}
, where

\displaystyle{\sf E}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle zy^{2}-4(x-t_{1}z)^{3}+t_{2}z^{2}(x-t_{1}z)+t_{3}z^{3}=0,

\displaystyle[x;y;z]\times(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\in\mathbb{P}^{2}\times{\sf T},\

\displaystyle(\alpha,\omega)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\left[\frac{dx}{y}\right],\left[\frac{xdx}{y}\right]\right)\ \ \hbox{given in the affine coordinate $z=1$.}

The natural action of the algebraic group

{\sf G}:=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}k&k^{\prime}\\
0&k^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg|k^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{k}},k\in{\mathfrak{k}}-\{0\}\right\}

by change of basis on
{\sf T}
is given by

t\bullet g:=(t_{1}k^{-2}+k^{\prime}k^{-1},t_{2}k^{-4},t_{3}k^{-6}),t=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\in{\sf T},\ \ g=\begin{bmatrix}k&k^{\prime}\\
0&k^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\in{\sf G}.

Moreover, if
\nabla:H^{1}_{\rm dR}({\sf E}/{\sf T})\to\Omega^{1}_{{\sf T}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}^{1}_{{\sf T}}}H^{1}_{\rm dR}({\sf E}/{\sf T})
is the Gauss-Manin connection of
{\sf E}/{\sf T}
then the Ramanujan vector field is the unique vector field in
{\sf T}
with the property

\nabla_{\sf v}\alpha=-\omega,\ \ \nabla_{\sf v}\omega=0.

The moduli space
{\sf T}
in
[
67
]
is called ”ibiporanga”, a word in Tupi language meaning pretty land. Once we master all the preliminaries of
Theorem

4.1
, the proof becomes an easy exercise, see also
[
43
, Appendix 1, page 158]
, and we realize that being an elliptic curve does not play a significant role in this theorem.
The author took the job of generalizing
Theorem

4.1
and wrote many papers with the title “Gauss-Manin connection in disguise”. The project has been summarized in the book
[
67
]
, and it has been so far written over fields of characteristic zero. For arithmetic applications, it seems to be necessary to consider fields of positive characteristic. We redefine
{\sf T}
to be
{\rm Spec}\mathbb{Z}[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},\frac{1}{\Delta}]
which has a similar moduli space interpretation.
In this article we prove the following:

Theorem 4.2
.

For primes
p\not=2,3
, there is a curve in
{\sf T}/\mathbb{F}_{p}
which is invariant by
{\sf v}
, and its
{\mathfrak{k}}
-rational points correspond to triples
(E,\alpha,\omega)

such that
C(\alpha)=\alpha
and
C(\omega)=0
, where
C
is the Cartier operator.

It is shown by J.V. Pereira in
[
77
]
, that foliations modulo primes might have algebraic leaves, even though in
characteristic zero they do not have such leaves.
Theorem

4.2
is a manifestation of this phenomena.
Most of the ingredients of the proof of
Theorem

4.2
comes from the articles of Swinnerton-Dyer, Serre and Katz in LNM 350 and they are the building blocks of the theory of
p
-adic modular forms. The integrality of the coefficients of
E_{2},E_{4},E_{6}
play a main role in the proof of
Theorem

4.2
. For Calabi-Yau varieties such integralities, and in particular the integrality of the mirror map, have been experimentally observed by physicists, and proved for special class of Calabi-Yau varieties, see
[
64
, Appendix C]
and the references therein, but in general it is an open problem.
In
[
64
,
3
]
we have introduced the theory of Calabi-Yau modular forms and proved a similar statement as in
Theorem

4.1
for Calabi-Yau threefolds. We strongly believe that
Theorem

4.2
for Calabi-Yau varieties is equivalent to the
p
-integrality of Calabi-Yau modular forms. In this direction we observe the following.
For a moment forget that the Ramanujan vector field has a solution given by the Eisenstein series. Instead, consider
{\sf v}
as an ordinary differential equation:

(4.2)

{\rm R}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\dot{t}_{1}=t_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{12}t_{2}\\
\dot{t}_{2}=4t_{1}t_{2}-6t_{3}\\
\dot{t}_{3}=6t_{1}t_{3}-\frac{1}{3}t_{2}^{2}\end{array}\right..

We write each
t_{i}
as a formal power series in
q
,
t_{i}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t_{i,n}q^{n},\ i=1,2,3
and substitute in the above differential
equation with
\dot{t}_{i}=-q\frac{\partial t_{i}}{\partial q}
and the initial values
t_{1}=-\frac{1}{12}(1-24q+\dots)
. It turns out that all
t_{i,n}
can be computed recursively, however, by recursion we can at most claim
t_{i,n}\in\mathbb{Q}
, for more details
see
[
63
, Section 4.3]
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
p\not=2,3
be a prime. The followings are equivalent:

1.

There is a curve
C
in
{\sf T}/\mathbb{F}_{p}
passing through
a:=(-\frac{1}{12},\frac{1}{12},-\frac{1}{216})
, smooth at
a
, tangent to
b=(2,20,\frac{7}{3})

at
a
and tangent to the Ramanujan vector field
{\sf v}
and
{\sf v}^{p}={\sf v}
restricted to
C
.

2.

The solution
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
of
{\sf v}
described above is
p
-integral, that is,
p
does not appear in the denominator of
t_{i,n}
’s.

The author strongly believes that the existence of algebraic solutions of modular vector fields as in
[
67
]
and generalizations of
Theorem

4.3
can be proved by available methods in algebraic geometry. This will give a purely geometric method for proving the integrality of Calabi-Yau modular forms without computing periods explicitly. Despite the fact that in this article we heavily use the Eisenstein series
E_{p-1},\ E_{p+1}
for
p
