Codimensions of the components of the Hodge/special loci for cubic hypersurfaces.

5.6
Artinian Gorenstein ideals attached to Hodge cycles

In order to constuct an algebraic cycle
Z
from its topological class we must compute its ideal
I_{Z}
which might be a complicated task. However, we may aim to compute at least one element
g
of
I_{Z}
which is not in the ideal
I_{X}
of the ambient space
X
. In the case of surfaces
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
this is actually almost the whole task, as we do the intersection
X\cap\mathbb{P}\{g=0\}
, and the only possiblity for
Z
comes from the irreducible components of this intersection. In general this is as difficult as the original job, and a precise formulation of this has been done in
[
86
]
.
The linear part of the Artinian-Gorenstein ideal of a Hodge cycle of a hypersurface seems to be part of the defining ideal of the underlying algebraic cycle, and in this section we aim to explain this.

Let
X=\{f=0\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
be a smooth hypersurface of degree
d\geq 3
and even dimension
n\geq 2
defined over
\mathbb{C}
, and

\sigma:=(\frac{n}{2}+1)(d-2).

Definition 5.2
.

For every Hodge cycle
\delta\in H_{n}(X,\mathbb{Z})
we define its associated Artinian Gorenstein ideal as the homogeneous ideal

I(\delta)_{a}:=\left\{Q\in\mathbb{C}[x]_{a}\Bigg|{\int}_{\delta}{\rm res}\left(\frac{QP\Omega}{F^{\frac{n}{2}+1}}\right)=0,\ \ \forall P\in\mathbb{C}[x]_{\sigma-a}\right\}.

By definition
I(\delta)_{m}=\mathbb{C}[x]_{m}
for all
m\geq\sigma+1
.

Let
{Z_{\infty}}
be the intersection of a linear
\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}+1}
with
X
and

[{Z_{\infty}}]\in H_{n}(X,\mathbb{Z})
be the induced element in homology (the polarization).
We have
I([Z])=\mathbb{C}[x]
and for an arbitrary Hodge cycle
\delta
,
I(\delta)
depends only on the equivalence class of
\delta\in H_{n}(X,\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}[{Z_{\infty}}]
.
The main purpose of the present section is to investigate the following:

Conjecture 5.5
.

Let
\delta\in H_{n}(X,\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}[{Z_{\infty}}]
be a non-torsion Hodge cycle such that
V_{\delta}
is smooth. Assume that there is a non-zero linear polynomial
g\in(I_{\delta})_{1}
. Then
\delta
is supported in the hyperplane section
Y:=\mathbb{P}\{g=0\}\cap X
.

If the Hodge conjecture is true then
Conjecture

5.5
says that the linear polynomial
g
is in the defining ideal of an algebraic cycle
Z
such that
\delta=[Z]
. We have the following statement which is stronger than the converse to
Conjecture

5.5
.
Let
\delta=[Z]\in H_{n}(X,\mathbb{Z})
be an algebraic cycle. Then the defining ideal of
Z
is inside
I_{\delta}
. The proof is the same as
[
70
, Proposition 11.3]
.

If we take a basis
g_{1},g_{2},\cdots,g_{k}
of
(I_{\delta})_{1}
and apply the above conjecture for
g=\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}g_{i}
with arbitrary
t_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
then we may conclude that
\delta
is supported in
\mathbb{P}\{(I_{\delta})_{1}=0\}\cap X
. A rigorous argument for this is needed, but it does not seem to be difficult. In particular,
{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}(I_{\delta})_{1}\leq\frac{n}{2}+1
. For
X
the Fermat variety this consequence is easy and it can be reduced to an elementary problem as
[
66
, Problem 21.3]
.

Conjecture

5.5
is mainly inspired by the following conjecture for which we have more evidences.

Conjecture 5.6
.

If
V_{\delta}
is smooth and
{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}(I_{\delta})_{1}=\frac{n}{2}+1
then
\mathbb{P}\{(I_{\delta})_{1}=0\}=\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}}
is inside
X
and modulo
\mathbb{Z}[{Z_{\infty}}]
we have
\delta=[\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}}]
.

For
d\not=3,4,6
,
X_{0}
the Fermat variety and without the smoothness condition this theorem is proved in
[
90
, Theorem 1.2]
. For
d=3,4,6
smoothness is necessary as in
[
27
]
the authors have described many non-smooth components for which the theorem is not true.

Proposition 5.2
.

If
Conjecture

5.5
is true then the hyperplane
\mathbb{P}\{g=0\}
is not transversal to
X
and hence
Y:=\mathbb{P}\{g=0\}\cap X
is not smooth.

Proof.

If
Y\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}:=\mathbb{P}\{g=0\}
is smooth then by Lefschetz’ hyperplane section theorem
H_{n}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{n}(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{Z})
and the latter is generated by any
\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
. From another side if we take any
\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}+1}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
we have
{Z_{\infty}}\subset Y\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
, and
[{Z_{\infty}}]=d[\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}}]
in
H_{n}(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{Z})
. This implies that a
d
multiple of the generator of
H_{n}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
is
[{Z_{\infty}}]
, and so
\delta
must be a torsion in
H_{n}(X,\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}[{Z_{\infty}}]
.
∎

5.7
Singular cubic hypersurfaces

If
Conjecture

5.5
is true then the Hodge cycle
\delta
is supported in a singular cubic hypersurface of dimension
n
, and our analysis of
\delta
reduces to the study of singularities of cubic hypersurfaces.
Cubic hypersurfaces have many linear subspaces and it is worth to mention the following result:

Theorem 5.4

(
[
9
]
)
.

Let
X=\{f_{1}=f_{2}=\cdots=f_{r}=0\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n+r}
be a complete intersection of dimension
n
, where
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{r},\ \ \deg(f_{i})=d_{i}
are
homogeneous polynomials in the projective coordinates of
\mathbb{P}^{n+r}
.
For a generic
X
, the variety
\Omega_{X}(k)
of
k
-planes inside
X
is non-empty and smooth of pure dimension

\delta=(k+1)(n+r-k)-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\binom{d_{j}+k}{k}
, provided
\delta\geq 0
and
X
is not a quadric. In the case
X
a quadric, we require

n\geq 2k
. Furthermore, if
\delta>0
or if in the case
X
a quadric,
n>2k
, then
\Omega_{X}(k)
is
connected (hence irreducible).

For the case of our interest
r=1,d=3
, and one dimension below linear cycles that is
k=\frac{n}{2}-1
, we have

\delta=\frac{k+1}{6}\left(6(n+1-k)-(k+3)(k+2)\right)=\frac{n}{12}(\frac{n}{2}+2)\left(5-\frac{n}{2}\right).

It follows that the number of
\mathbb{P}^{4}
’s in a generic cubic tenfold is finite. It turns out that such a number is
1812646836
, see
[
37
]
.
For
n=10
and
k=\frac{n}{2}-2=3
we have
\delta=8
, that is, the variety of
\mathbb{P}^{3}
’s inside a generic cubic tenfold is of dimension
8
.
Next, we focus on singular cubic hypersurfaces.

Proposition 5.3
.

Any line passing through two distinct points of
{\rm Sing}(X)
is inside
X
.

Proof.

If
p
and
q
are two distinct singular points of
X
then the line passing through
p
and
q
intersects
X
in more than four points (counting with multiplicity) and hence it must be inside
X
.
∎

Proposition 5.4
.

A singular cubic hypersurface
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
is either a cone over another cubic hypersurface of dimension
n-1
or it is birational to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

Proof.

Let
p\in X
be any singularity of
X
. We define
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{p}
to be the space of lines in
\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
passing through
p
and

X_{1}:=\{l\in\mathbb{P}^{n}_{p}\mid l\subset X\}.

We have the map

\alpha:\mathbb{P}^{n}_{p}\backslash X_{1}\to X,\ \ \ l\mapsto\hbox{The third intersection point of $l$ with $X$.}

If for all point
q\in X
the line passing through
p
and
q
lies in
X
then the image of
\alpha
is the point
p
. In this case
X
is a cone over another cubic hypersurface of dimension
n-1
and
p
is the vertex of the cone.
Let us assume that this is not the case. Then
\alpha
is a birational map between
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{p}
and
X
.
∎

It is useful to rewrite the above proof in a coordinate system
[x_{0}:x_{1}:\cdots:x_{n+1}]
. We take the affine chart
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
given by
x_{0}=1
and assume that the singularity
p
is at the origin
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
. The hypersurface
X
is given by
f=x_{0}f_{2}-f_{3}
, where
f_{i}
’ are homogenuous polynomials of degree
i
in
x
. If
f_{2}=0
then
X
is a cone over the cubic hypersurface
\mathbb{P}\{f_{3}=0\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
. Otherwise, we have the birational map

\alpha:\mathbb{P}^{n}\dashrightarrow X,\ \ [x]\mapsto[f_{3}(x):xf_{2}(x)].

We would like to describe
{\rm Sing}(X)
and do the desingularization of
X
. In the following we consider
\{f_{i}=0\},\ \ i=2,3
as affine subvarieties of
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
and
\mathbb{P}\{f_{i}=0\},\ \ i=2,3
as projective varieties in
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\infty}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

We have

(5.8)

\displaystyle{\rm Sing}\{f_{2}=0\}\cap{\rm Sing}\{f_{3}=0\}\subset{\rm Sing}(X)\cap\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\subset\{f_{2}=0\}\cap\{f_{3}=0\}

(5.9)

\displaystyle{\rm Sing}(X)\cap\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\infty}={\rm Sing}\mathbb{P}\{f_{3}=0\}\cap\mathbb{P}\{f_{2}=0\}.

Moreover, any line between
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
and
p\in{\rm Sing}(X)\cap\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
either lies in
{\rm Sing}(X)
for which
p\in{\rm Sing}(f_{2}=0)\cap{\rm Sing}(f_{3}=0)
or it intersects
{\rm Sing}(X)
only at
0
and
p
.

Proof.

The variety
X
is given by
x_{0}f_{2}(x)-f_{3}(x)=0
and hence
{\rm Sing}(X)
is given by
x_{0}f_{2}(x)-f_{3}(x)=f_{2}=x_{0}\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial x_{i}}-\frac{\partial f_{3}}{\partial x_{i}}=0,\ i=1,2,\ldots,n+1
.
The inclusions (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) are immediate.
∎

5.8
Computing Artinian Gorenstein ring over formal power series

The hypersurface
X_{t},\ \ t\in V_{[Z]}\backslash V_{Z}
is not given explicitly, as its existence is conjectural. Therefore, it might be difficult to study its Artinian Gorenstein ring. However, as we can write the Taylor series of the periods of
X_{t},t\in({\sf T},0)
explicitely, see
[
66
, Sections 13.9, 13.10, 18.5]
we might try to study such rings over, not only over
\mathbb{C}
, but also over formal power series.
In this section we explain this idea.

In
[
66
, Section 19.3]
, we have taken a parameter space which is transversal to
V_{Z}
at
0
and it has the complimentary dimension. Therefore, it intersects
V_{Z}
only at
0
. From now on we use
V_{Z}
and
V_{[Z]}
for this new parameter space, and hence by our construction
V_{Z}=\{0\}
.
Conjecture

5.1
is equivalent to the follwing: The Hodge locus
V_{[Z]}
is a smooth curve (
{\rm dim}(V_{[Z]})=1
). We note that
Theorem

5.3
is proved first for this new parameter space. In particular, this implies that the new parameter space is also transversal to
T_{0}V_{[Z]}
.

For a smooth hypersurface defined over the ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}
of holomorphic functions in a neighborhood of
0
, and a continuous family of cycles
\delta=\delta_{t}\in H_{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}[{Z_{\infty}}],\ t\in({\sf T},0)
, the Hodge locus
V_{\delta}
is given by the zero locus of an ideal
{\mathcal{I}}(\delta)\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\sigma:=(\frac{n}{2}+1)(d-2)
. We define the Artinian Gorenstein ideal of the Hodge locus
V_{\delta_{t}}
as the homogeneous ideal

(5.10)

I(\delta)_{a}:=\left\{Q\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}[x]_{a}\Bigg|{\int}_{\delta_{t}}{\rm res}\left(\frac{QP\Omega}{F_{t}^{\frac{n}{2}+1}}\right)\in{\mathcal{I}}(\delta),\ \ \forall P\in\mathbb{C}[x]_{\sigma-a}\right\}.

We define the Artinian Gorenstein algebra of the Hodge locus as
R(\delta):={\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}[x]/I(\delta)
. By definition
I(\delta)_{m}={\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}[x]_{m}
for all
m\geq\sigma+1
and so
R(\delta)_{a}=0
.

Note that we actually need that the integral in (
5.10
) vanishes identically over
{\rm Zero}({\mathcal{I}}(\delta))
. Since
\mathcal{I}(\delta)
might not be reduced, these two definitions might not be equivalent. Since in
Conjecture

5.1
we expect that
V_{[Z]}
is smooth, these two definitions are the same.
In a similar way we can replace
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}
with the ring
\check{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}
of formal power series, and in particular, with the truncated rings
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}^{N}:={\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}/m_{{\sf T},0}^{N+1}\cong\check{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}/\check{m}_{{\sf T},0}^{N+1}
.

Conjecture 5.7
.

For all even number
n\geq 6
the linear part
I(\delta)_{1}
of
I(\delta)
is not zero.

It seems quite possible to prove this conjecture using
[
92
, Section 3]
and
[
74
, Theorem 3, Proposition 6]
. In these reference the authors prove that if a Hodge locus
V_{\delta}
has minimal codimension then
{\rm dim}I(\delta)=\frac{n}{2}+1
. Note that the codimension of our Hodge locus as a function in
n
grows as the
minimal codimension for a Hodge loci, see
Section

5.4
.
Despite this, we want to get some evidence for
Conjecture

5.7
. The main goal of this section is to explain the computer code which verifies the following statement: The linear part
I^{N}(\delta)_{1}
of
I^{N}(\delta)
is not zero for
(n,N)=(6,7),(8,3)
.

For the computer code for experimental verification of
Conjecture

5.7
see the tex file of the present text in arxiv or the author’s webpage.

We fix the canonical basis
x^{I}
of the Jacobian ring
S_{0}:=\mathbb{C}[x]/{\rm jacob}(F_{0})
, where
F_{0}:=x_{0}^{d}+x_{1}^{d}+\cdots+x_{n+1}^{d}
is the Fermat polynomial. This is also the basis for
\mathbb{C}[x]/{\rm jacob}(F_{t})
in a Zariski neighborhood of
0\in{\sf T}
. From this basis we take out the basis for
(S_{0})_{1}
and
(S_{0})_{\sigma-1}
, where
\sigma=(d-2)(\frac{n}{2}+1)
. These are:

\displaystyle(S_{0})_{1}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n+1}

\displaystyle(S_{0})_{\sigma-1}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle x_{0}^{i_{0}}x_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots x_{n+1}^{i_{n+1}},\ \ \sum i_{j}=\sigma-1,\ 0\leq i_{j}\leq d-2.

Let
a_{1}:=n+1=\#(S_{0})_{1}
and
b_{1}:=\#(S_{0})_{\sigma-1}
. For a Hodge cycle
\delta_{0}\in H_{n}(X_{0},\mathbb{Z})
, we define the
a_{1}\times b_{1}
matrix in the following way:

A_{t}:=\left[\int_{\delta_{t}}\omega_{PQ}\right],\ \ P\in(S_{0})_{1},\ \ Q\in(S_{0})_{\sigma-1}.

For the Hodge cycle in
Conjecture

5.1
we want to compute
I(\delta)_{1}
which is equivalent to compute the kernel of
A_{t}
modulo
{\mathcal{I}}(\delta)
from the left, that is
1\times a_{1}
vectors
v
with
vA_{t}=0
modulo
{\mathcal{I}}(\delta)
. At first step we aim to compute the rank of
A_{t}
. Let
\mu
be the rank of
A_{t}
over
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T},0}/{\mathcal{I}}(\delta)
. This means that the determinant of all
(\mu+1)\times(\mu+1)
minors of

A_{t}
are in the ideal modulo
{\mathcal{I}}(\delta)
, but there is a
\mu\times\mu
minor whose determinant is not in
{\mathcal{I}}(\delta)
. Recall that
{\mathcal{I}}(\delta)
is conjecturally reduced! These statements can be experimented by computer after truncating the entries of
A_{t}
.

5.9
Kloosterman’s work

In 2023 R. Kloostermann sent the author the preprint
[
54
]
in which among many other things proves that
Conjecture

5.1
is not true for
n\geq 10
. It turns out that for a generic cubic hypersurface
X_{0}
of dimension
n\geq 10
, we have
T_{0}V_{Z}=T_{0}V_{[Z]}
, even though for Fermat variety this is not true. Actually according to
Theorem

5.3
at Fermat variety for
n=10
and
12
we have the equalities of fourth order and third order neighborhoods, respectively. The equality
T_{0}V_{Z}=T_{0}V_{[Z]}
can be easily checked by computer using Villaflor’s elegant formula in
[
91
]
. I could have done this in 2019 when Villaflor defended his thesis. However, I was too exhausted by my computer calculations in
[
66
]
.

The computer code for this verification which uses an example of hypersurface of dimension
10
in
[
54
]
can be obtained in the tex file of the present text in arxiv or the author’s webpage.

Recently in
[
6
, Corollary 1.6]
the authors have proved that the Hodge loci corresponding to all tensor product of
H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Z})
and its dual and of positive dimension in the Griffiths period domain is algebraic for
d=3
and
n\geq 10
. This Hodge loci is formulated in terms of Mumford-Tate groups and it is much larger than the Hodge loci considered in the present text. Even though
Conjecture

5.1
fails for
n\geq 10
, there might be infinite number of special components
V_{i},\ i\in\mathbb{N}
of Hodge loci in our context which might lie inside a proper algebraic subset of
V
of
{\sf T}
(in their terminology maximal element for inclusion).
It might be helpful to construct such a
V
explicitly. If we consider the variation of Hodge structures over
V
then
[
6
, Corollary 1.6]
imply that the generic period domain for
V
becomes a product of monodromy invariant factors
D_{j}
and either the level of Hodge decomposition attached to one of these factors is
\leq 2

or for all except a finite
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the Hodge locus
V_{i}
has zero dimension projected to one of
D_{j}
. A similar discussion must be also valid for
Conjecture

5.1
, that is
n=6,8
, as the Hodge locus
V_{[Z]}
is atypical in the sense of
[
6
]
. For the summary of results on Hodge loci in this general framework see
[
53
]
.

5.10
Conjecture

5.1
for tangent spaces ( By R. Villaflor)

Let
X=\{x_{0}^{3}+x_{1}^{3}+\cdots+x_{n+1}^{3}=0\}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
be the cubic Fermat variety of even dimension
n
. Let

\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}+3}:=\{x_{6}-\zeta_{2d}x_{7}=x_{8}-\zeta_{2d}x_{9}=\cdots=x_{n}-\zeta_{2d}x_{n+1}=0\},

\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}}:=\{x_{0}-\zeta_{2d}x_{1}=x_{2}-\zeta_{2d}x_{3}=x_{4}-\zeta_{2d}x_{5}=0\}\cap\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}+3},

\check{\mathbb{P}}^{\frac{n}{2}}:=\{x_{0}-\zeta_{2d}^{\alpha}x_{1}=x_{2}-\zeta_{2d}^{\alpha}x_{3}=x_{4}-\zeta_{2d}^{\alpha}x_{5}=0\}\cap\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}+3},

where
\alpha\in\{3,5,7,\ldots,2d-1\}
. Then

\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}-3}:=\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}}\cap\check{\mathbb{P}}^{\frac{n}{2}}=\{x_{0}=x_{1}=x_{2}=x_{3}=x_{4}=x_{5}=0\}\cap\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}+3}.

For
Z
as in (
5.1
), let
V_{[Z]}
,
V_{[\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}}]}
and
V_{[\check{\mathbb{P}}^{\frac{n}{2}}]}
be their corresponding Hodge loci.

Proposition 5.6
.

We have
{\rm dim}T_{0}V_{[Z]}={\rm dim}{\sf T}_{0}V_{Z}+1
.

Proof.

In fact, by
[
66
, Proposition 17.9]
we have
{\rm dim}V_{Z}={\rm dim}T_{0}V_{[\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}}]}\cap T_{0}V_{[\check{\mathbb{P}}^{\frac{n}{2}}]}
and so we are reduced to show that

{\rm dim}\frac{T_{0}V_{[Z]}}{T_{0}V_{[\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}}]}\cap T_{0}V_{[\check{\mathbb{P}}^{\frac{n}{2}}]}}=1.

By
[
91
, Corollaries 8.2 and 8.3]
this is equivalent to show that

{\rm dim}\frac{(J^{F}:P_{1}+P_{2})_{3}}{(J^{F}:P_{1})_{3}\cap(J^{F}:P_{2})_{3}}=1,

where
J^{F}=\langle x_{0}^{2},x_{1}^{2},\ldots,x_{n+1}^{2}\rangle
is the Jacobian ideal of
X
,
P_{1}:=R_{1}Q
,
P_{2}:=R_{2}Q
,

Q:=\prod_{k\geq 6\text{ even}}{(x_{k}+\zeta_{6}x_{k+1})},

R_{1}:=c_{1}\cdot{(x_{0}+\zeta_{6}x_{1})}{(x_{2}+\zeta_{6}x_{3})}{(x_{4}+\zeta_{6}x_{5})},

and

R_{2}:=c_{2}\cdot{(x_{0}+\zeta_{6}^{\alpha}x_{1})}{(x_{2}+\zeta_{6}^{\alpha}x_{3})}{(x_{4}+\zeta_{6}^{\alpha}x_{5})},

for some
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Let
I:=\langle x_{0}^{2},x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{3}^{2},x_{4}^{2},x_{5}^{2}\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{C}[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}]
. We claim that the natural inclusion

(I:R_{1}+R_{2})_{3}\hookrightarrow(J^{F}:P_{1}+P_{2})_{3}

induces an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces

(5.11)

\frac{(I:R_{1}+R_{2})_{3}}{(I:R_{1})_{3}\cap(I:R_{2})_{3}}\simeq\frac{(J^{F}:P_{1}+P_{2})_{3}}{(J^{F}:P_{1})_{3}\cap(J^{F}:P_{2})_{3}}.

Note first that

(J^{F}:Q)=\langle x_{0}^{2},x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{3}^{2},x_{4}^{2},x_{5}^{2},x_{6}-\zeta_{6}x_{7},x_{7}^{2},x_{8}-\zeta_{6}x_{9},x_{9}^{2},\ldots,x_{n}-\zeta_{6}x_{n+1},x_{n+1}^{2}\rangle

since both are Artin Gorenstein ideals of socle in degree
\frac{n}{2}+4
(here we use Macaulay theorem
[
91
, Theorem 2.1]
) and the right hand side is clearly contained in
(J^{F}:Q)
. In order to prove (
5.11
), let
r\in(I:R_{1}+R_{2})_{3}
such that
r\in(J^{F}:P_{i})_{3}=((J^{F}:Q):R_{i})_{3}
for both
i=1,2
, then
r\cdot R_{i}\in(J^{F}:Q)\cap\mathbb{C}[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}]=I
and so
r\in(I:R_{i})_{3}
for each
i=1,2
. Conversely, given
q\in(J^{F}:P_{1}+P_{2})_{3}
write it as
q=s+t+u
,
where
s\in\mathbb{C}[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}]
,
t\in\langle x_{6}-\zeta_{6}x_{7},x_{8}-\zeta_{6}x_{9},\ldots,x_{n}-\zeta_{6}x_{n+1}\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{C}[x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1}]
and
u\in\langle x_{7},x_{9},\ldots,x_{n+1}\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{C}[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[x_{7},x_{9},x_{11},\ldots,x_{n+1}]
. Since
q\cdot(R_{1}+R_{2})\in(J^{F}:Q)
, letting
x_{6}=x_{7}=\cdots=x_{n+1}=0
it follows that
s\cdot(R_{1}+R_{2})\in I
, i.e.
s\in(I:R_{1}+R_{2})
.
On the other hand is clear that
t\in(J^{F}:P_{1})\cap(J^{F}:P_{2})
, then in order to finish the claim it is enough to show that
u\in(J^{F}:P_{1})\cap(J^{F}:P_{2})
. Note that this is clearly true for all monomials appearing in the expansion of
u
divisible by some
x_{i}^{2}
for
i>6
odd. Hence we may assume that

u=\sum_{i>6\text{ odd}}p_{i}(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{5})\cdot x_{i}+\sum_{j>i>6\text{ both odd}}p_{ij}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{5})\cdot x_{i}x_{j}

+\sum_{k>j>i>6\text{ all odd}}p_{ijk}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{5})\cdot x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}.

Note also that

(J^{F}:Q)\cap\mathbb{C}[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[x_{7},x_{9},x_{11},\ldots,x_{n+1}]=

\langle x_{0}^{2},x_{1}^{2},\ldots,x_{5}^{2},x_{7}^{2},x_{9}^{2},\ldots,x_{n+1}^{2}\rangle

is a monomial ideal. From here it is clear that
u\cdot(R_{1}+R_{2})\in(J^{F}:Q)
if and only if
p_{i}\cdot(R_{1}+R_{2})\in I
,
p_{ij}\cdot(R_{1}+R_{2})\in I
and
p_{ijk}\cdot(R_{1}+R_{2})\in I
for all
k>j>i>6
odd numbers. Then
p_{i}\in(I:R_{1}+R_{2})_{2}
,
p_{ij}\in(I:R_{1}+R_{2})_{1}
and
p_{ijk}\in(I:R_{1}+R_{2})_{0}=0
. By
[
91
, Proposition 2.1]
we know
(I:R_{1}+R_{2})_{e}=(I:R_{1})_{e}\cap(I:R_{2})_{e}
for all
e\neq 3
, then
p_{i}\in(I:R_{1})_{2}\cap(I:R_{2})_{2}
and
p_{ij}\in(I:R_{1})_{1}\cap(I:R_{2})_{1}
for all
j>i>6
both odd and so
u\in(J^{F}:P_{1})\cap(J^{F}:P_{2})
as claimed. This proves (
5.11
). Finally, since
(I:R_{1}+R_{2})
,
(I:R_{1})
and
(I:R_{2})
are all Artin Gorenstein ideals of socle in degree 3 but they are not equal, we get that
(I:R_{1}+R_{2})_{3}
is a hyperplane of
\mathbb{C}[x_{0},\ldots,x_{5}]_{3}
while
(I:R_{1})_{3}\cap(I:R_{2})_{3}
is a codimension 2 linear subspace of
\mathbb{C}[x_{0},\ldots,x_{5}]_{3}
, hence

{\rm dim}\frac{(I:R_{1}+R_{2})_{3}}{(I:R_{1})_{3}\cap(I:R_{2})_{3}}=1.

∎

Remark 5.1
.

The proof of the above proposition works in general for any degree
d
such that the intersection of both linear cycles is
m
-dimensional with
(d-2)(\frac{n}{2}-m)=d
. It is easy to see that this is only possible for
(d,m)=(3,\frac{n}{2}-3)
and
(d,m)=(4,\frac{n}{2}-2)
. We expect a similar property as in
Conjecture

5.1
for the later case, see
[
66
, Section 19.8]
.

Chapter 6
Vector fields modulo primes

I recall a story (…) about a man whose canoe flipped in turbulent waters. He fought and struggled to get free from the currents but he failed, drowned and died. Shortly after, his body surfaced near by. Apparently, had he surrendered to the currents, instead of fighting them, he had a good chance of making out alive, (from the
blog iamronen.com
).

6.1
Introduction

In this chapter we aim to study a local-global principle for vector fields which generalizes the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture on linear differential equations.
Vector fields from an algebraic point of view are simple, however, the foliations induced by them might show complicated dynamical behavior. The Lorenz attractor
shows this dynamical behavior in an algebraically simple example.
We investigate modulo primes behavior of vector fields. This study is mainly for detecting algebraic solutions of vector fields and we do not know yet whether it is related to the dynamics of solutions of vector fields.

1
1

1

We use the notations in
Section

3.1
and for simplicity we do not reproduce them here.

6.2
Derivations or vector fields

For simplicity we can take
{\sf T}:=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathfrak{R}}={\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n}]/I)
, where
I
is an ideal in
{\mathfrak{R}}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n}]
. A vector field in
{\sf T}
is written in the form

{\sf v}={\sf v}_{i}(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}+{\sf v}_{2}(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{2}}+\cdots+{\sf v}_{n}(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{n}},\ \ {\sf v}_{i}\in{\mathfrak{R}}[z],

where
\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}
is the unique vector field in
{\sf T}
with
\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}(dz_{j})=1
if
i=j
and
=0
otherwise. Moreover,
{\sf v}(I)\subset I
. The
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
-module of vector fields
{\Theta}_{{\sf T}}
is isomorphic to the
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
-module
of derivations.

A map
{\sf v}:{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
is called a derivation
if it is
{\mathfrak{R}}
-linear and it satisfies the Leibniz rule

{\sf v}(fg)=f{\sf v}(g)+{\sf v}(f)g,\ \ f,g\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}.

We denote by
{\rm Der}({\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T})
the
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
-module of derivations.

Proposition 6.1
.

We have an isomorphism of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
-modules

\displaystyle{\Theta}_{{\sf T}}\cong{\rm Der}({\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}),

\displaystyle{\sf v}\mapsto(f\mapsto{\sf v}(df)).

Proof.

This isomorphism maps the vector field
{\sf v}
to the corresponding derivation
\check{\sf v}
obtained by

\check{\sf v}(f)={\sf v}(df),\ \ f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
.
This equality also defines its inverse
\check{\sf v}\mapsto{\sf v}
, that is, if
\check{\sf v}\in{\rm Der}({\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T})
then
{\sf v}\in\Theta_{\sf T}
is defined through

{\sf v}(\sum g_{i}df_{i}):=\sum g_{i}\check{\sf v}(f_{i})
. We have to show that this map is well-defined, that is, if
\sum g_{i}df_{i}=0
then
\sum g_{i}\check{\sf v}(f_{i})=0
. For this, recall that
{\sf T}:={\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}}[z]/I)
is affine, where
I\subset{\mathfrak{R}}[z]
is an ideal. In this case a derivation in
{\sf T}
is given by
\check{\sf v}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\sf v}_{i}(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}
with
\check{\sf v}(I)\subset I
. Moreover,
\Omega^{1}_{\sf T}
is the quotient of
{\mathfrak{R}}[z]dz_{1}+{\mathfrak{R}}[z]dz_{2}+\cdots+{\mathfrak{R}}[z]dz_{n}
with the
{\mathfrak{R}}[z]
-module generated by
dI,Idz_{i},d(fg)-fdg-gdf,\ f,g\in{\mathfrak{R}}[z]
.
∎

6.3
Fundamental theorems of ODE’s

In a course in ordinary differential equations one first learns the existence and uniqueness of solutions. Let
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathfrak{R}}={\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}}[z])
be the affine line over
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
be the vector field on it such that
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}(z)=1
. Let also
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}:={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
.
The next two theorems are classical in the theory of ordinary differential equations, however, they are not usually written in an algebro-geometric context, that is why we reproduce them here. We would like to point out the fact that in order to define the underlying holomorphic objects, we only need to be able to invert any natural number in the ring
{\mathfrak{R}}
, and hence the usage of
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
instead of
{\mathfrak{R}}
is justified.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
{t}
be a smooth
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
,
{\sf v}
be a vector field in
{\sf T}
with
{\sf v}({t})\not=0
and
A=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}
be the one dimensional affine scheme.
There is a unique holomorphic map

\varphi:(A^{\rm hol}_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}},0)\to({\sf T}_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}},{t})\ \ \ \varphi(0)={t}

such that
\varphi
maps the vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
to
{\sf v}
.

Proof.

Let us take an algebraic coordinate system
z=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n})
in
({\sf T},t)
and set
{\sf v}_{i}:={\sf v}(z_{i})
. We write

{\sf v}_{i}
as formal power series in
z
with coefficients in
{\mathfrak{R}}
.
We denote by
\varphi_{i}
the pull-back of
z_{i}
by
\varphi
. The fact that
\varphi
maps
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
to
{\sf v}
is translated into the following ordinary differential equation:

(6.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\frac{\partial\varphi_{1}}{\partial z}&=&{\sf v}_{1}(\varphi_{1}(z),\varphi_{1}(z),\cdots,\varphi_{1}(z))\\
\frac{\partial\varphi_{2}}{\partial z}&=&{\sf v}_{2}(\varphi_{1}(z),\varphi_{1}(z),\cdots,\varphi_{1}(z))\\
&\vdots&\\
\frac{\partial\varphi_{n}}{\partial z}&=&{\sf v}_{n}(\varphi_{1}(z),\varphi_{1}(z),\cdots,\varphi_{1}(z))\end{array}\right..

From now on we use
\varphi
for
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\ldots,\varphi_{n})
, and so, the above differential equation can be written as

\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z}={\sf v}(\varphi)
, where
{\sf v}
is identified with
({\sf v}_{1},{\sf v}_{2},\ldots,{\sf v}_{n})
. Let
{\sf v}=p_{0}+p_{1}+\cdots
, where
p_{i}
is the homogeneous piece of degree
i
of
{\sf v}
. Moreover, let us write:

\varphi=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{i}z^{i},\ \varphi_{i}\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{n},\ \varphi_{0}:=0

and substitute all these in the above differential equation. It turns out that

i\cdot\varphi_{i}
can be written in a unique way in
terms of
\varphi_{j},\ j<i
with coefficients in
{\mathfrak{R}}
. We need to invert
i
, that is why in the statement of theorem we have to use
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. This guaranties the existence of a unique
formal power series
\varphi
. Note that if
{\sf v}({t})=0
then
\varphi_{i}=0
for all
i\geq 1

and so
\varphi
is the constant map. It is not at all clear why
\varphi
must be convergent. For this we use Picard operator associated with the differential equation (
6.1
) and the contracting map principle. For more details see
[
40
, §1.4, page 4]
.
∎

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
{t}
be a smooth
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
and
{\sf v}
be a vector field in
{\sf T}
with
{\sf v}({t})\not=0
.
There is a holomorphic coordinate system
(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n})
in
({\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}},{t})

such that
{\sf v}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}
.

Proof.

Let
z
be an algebraic coordinate system in
({\sf T},t)
. We are looking for a coordinate system
F
such that
the push forward of the vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}
by
F
is
{\sf v}
. This is equivalent to

\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial z_{1}}&\frac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial z_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial z_{n}}\\
\frac{\partial F_{2}}{\partial z_{1}}&\frac{\partial F_{2}}{\partial z_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial F_{2}}{\partial z_{n}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial F_{n}}{\partial z_{1}}&\frac{\partial F_{n}}{\partial z_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial F_{n}}{\partial z_{n}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{\sf v}_{1}\circ F\\
{\sf v}_{2}\circ F\\
\vdots\\
{\sf v}_{n}\circ F\end{pmatrix},

where
F=(F_{1},F_{2},\ldots,F_{n})
.
In a similar way as in
Theorem

6.1
we have a unique solution
F

to the above differential equation with

F(0,\tilde{z})=(0,\tilde{z}),

where
\tilde{z}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{n})
.
We have

\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial z_{j}}(0)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}{\sf v}_{1}(0)&0\\
*&I_{(n-1)\times(n-1)}\end{bmatrix}

By a linear change of coordinates in
z
, we may assume that
{\sf v}_{1}(0)\not=0
,
and so
F
is the desired coordinate system.
∎

Remark 6.1
.

In the proof of
Theorem

6.2
observe that if
{\sf v}_{i}
’s does not depend on
z_{i_{j}},\ j=1,2,\ldots
then we can assume that
F_{i_{j}}=z_{i_{j}}
, that is, we do not need to change the coordinate
z_{i_{j}}
.

6.4
Algebraic solutions

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\varphi
be the solution in
Theorem

6.1
.
The Zariski closure of
\varphi
is a subscheme of
{\sf T}
given by the ideal which consists of all regular functions
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
such that the pull-back of
f
by
\varphi
is zero:

{\sf T}_{\varphi}:={\rm Zero}\left({{\mathcal{I}}}_{\varphi}\right),\ \ {{\mathcal{I}}}_{\varphi}:=\left\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}\ |\ f\circ\varphi=0\right\}.

This is an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme by definition.
We say that
\varphi
is algebraic if for all
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
,
f\circ\varphi
are algebraic functions in
z
.
We say that the image of
\varphi
is algebraic if
{\rm dim}({\sf T}_{\varphi})=1
. Equivalently,
for all
f,g\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
, there is a polynomial in
P(x,y)\in{\mathfrak{R}}[x,y]
such that
P(f,g)=0
.

Note that in the above definition it does not make any difference to use
P(x,y)\in{\mathfrak{R}}[x,y]
or
P\in\mathbb{C}[x,y]
, see
Remark

1.1
.
The
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme
{\sf T}_{\varphi}
is tangent to the vector field
{\sf v}
:
For
f
in the ideal sheaf of
{\sf T}_{\varphi}
, since
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
is mapped to
{\sf v}
under
\varphi
, we have

{\sf v}(f)\circ\varphi=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}(f\circ\varphi)=0.

We say that a solution
\varphi
of a vector field
{\sf v}
is
defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
if
\varphi=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{i}z^{i},\ \varphi_{i}\in{\mathfrak{R}}^{n}
,
that is, we do not need to invert all integers in
{\mathfrak{R}}
.
The fact that a solution of a vector field is still defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
, that is we do not need to invert integers in
{\mathfrak{R}}
, has strong consequence.

Conjecture 6.1
.

Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme,
{\sf v}
be a vector field on
{\sf T}
and
{t}
be a smooth
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
.
If the solution
\varphi
of
{\sf v}
through
{t}
is defined over a finitely generated subring
{\mathfrak{R}}
of
\mathbb{C}
and
{\sf v}({t})\not=0
then
\varphi
is algebraic.

2
2

2

If a solution
\varphi
of
{\sf v}
is defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
then the ideal
{{\mathcal{I}}}_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}},{t}}\ \Big|\ f(\varphi)=0\}

is also defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
, that is, if we define
{{\mathcal{I}}}
in a similar way, replacing
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
with
{\mathfrak{R}}
, then
{{\mathcal{I}}}_{\mathbb{Q}}={{\mathcal{I}}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
. In this way,
Conjecture

6.1
is a particular case of
Conjecture

3.1
.

The condition
{\sf v}({t})\not=0
cannot be dropped from the above conjecture. We have an abundant literature on holomorphic functions of the form
y(z):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}y_{n}z^{n}\in\mathbb{Z}[[z]]
which satisfies a linear differential equation
\sum_{i=0}^{k}P_{i}(z)y^{(i)}=0
with
P_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}[z]
and
P_{k}\not=0,\ P_{k}(0)=0
, see for instance
[
97
]
. Writing this as a vector field in

(z,y,y^{\prime},\cdots,y^{k-1})
, we observe that it is meromorphic in
z=0
which contains the point

{t}:=(0,y(0),y^{\prime}(0),\cdots,y^{k-1}(0))
. Even if we multiply this vector field with
P_{k}(z)
in order to get a polynomial expression, we observe that
{t}
is a singular point of the new vector field.

Remark 6.2
.

In
Conjecture

0.4
we have written a particular case of
Conjecture

6.1
in order to make it accessible to a general audience. We will also introduce
Conjecture

6.2
which in this case is equivalent to say that if there is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
N^{n}y_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
then
y(x)
is algebraic.

Before, introducing local-global principles for vector fields, let us first introduce the analog of
Theorem

1.3
and
Theorem

1.1
in this case. Recall that
{\mathfrak{R}}\subset\mathbb{C}
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra and
{\mathfrak{k}}
is its quotient field.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme,
{t}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued smooth point of
{\sf T}
and
{\sf v}
be a vector field in
{\sf T}
with
{\sf v}({t})\not=0
. Moreover, assume that there is a function
z\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
such that

z({t})=0,\ {\sf v}(z)=1
.
If the solution
\varphi
of
{\sf v}
through
{t}
is algebraic then there is
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
such that the composition

\frac{N^{m}{\sf v}^{m}}{m!}({t}):{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{\mathfrak{k}},{t}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{{\mathfrak{k}}},{t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{t}}}{{\to}}{\mathfrak{k}},\ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}

is defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
, that is, it sends
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
to
{\mathfrak{R}}
. If all the solutions of
{\sf v}
are algebraic then there is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\frac{N^{m}{\sf v}^{m}}{m!}:{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T},\ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}

is well-defined, that is, its image is in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
.

Proof.

The proof is almost the same as the proof of
Theorem

1.1
.
For the first part, the theorem is of local nature, and so, we can assume that
{\sf T}:={\rm Spec}({\mathsf{R}})
is affine and
{\mathsf{R}}
is generated over
{\mathfrak{R}}
by
y_{0},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},\ y_{0}=z
. We know that
\varphi(A^{\rm hol},0)
is parameterized over
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, see
Theorem

6.1
, and it is algebraic. Therefore, there are polynomials
P_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[x,y]
in two variables and coefficients in
\mathbb{C}
such that
P_{i}(z\circ\varphi,y_{i}\circ\varphi)=0
. Using a similar argument as in
Remark

1.1
we can assume that
P_{i}
has coefficients in
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and after a multiplication with an integer, it has coefficients in
{\mathfrak{R}}
.
We write the Taylor series of
\check{y}_{i}:=y_{i}\circ\varphi
in the variable
\check{z}:=z\circ\varphi
and by
Proposition

1.3
we have
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\frac{N^{m}{\sf v}^{m}}{m!}({t})=\frac{N^{m}\frac{\partial^{m}\check{y}_{i}}{\partial\check{z}^{m}}}{m!}(0)\in{\mathfrak{R}}.

By (
1.6
), we get the statement for an arbitrary element of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
. For the second part instead of
Theorem

6.1
we use
Theorem

6.2
. We get polynomials
P_{i}
in two variables with coefficients in
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
and the rest of the proof is similar.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

The condition on the existence of
z
can be achieved by taking any
z\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
with
{\sf v}(z)({t})\not=0
and replacing
{\sf v}
with
\frac{{\sf v}}{z}
. It would be interesting to formulate a similar statement without this condition.
For this we might try to divide the ideal of the collinear scheme
{\sf v}||{\sf v}^{m}
with
m!
. It seems to be necessary to find a characteristic zero version of the following identity in characteristic
p
:

(f{\sf v})^{p}=f^{p}{\sf v}^{p}+f{\sf v}^{p-1}(f^{p-1}){\sf v}\hbox{ modulo }p,\ f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}},

see
[
46
, 5.4.0]
.

The analog of
Conjecture

1.1
can be written immediately.

Conjecture 6.2
.

Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme,
{t}
be a
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued smooth point of
{\sf T}
and
{\sf v}
be a vector field in
{\sf T}
with
{\sf v}({t})\not=0
.
If for some
N\in{\mathfrak{R}}
the map

\frac{N^{m}{\sf v}^{m}}{m!}({t}):{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}\to{\mathfrak{R}},\ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}

is well-defined, then the solution of
{\sf v}
through
{t}
is algebraic. Moreover, if

\frac{N^{m}{\sf v}^{m}}{m!}:{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T},\ \forall m\in\mathbb{N}

is well-defined then all the solutions of
{\sf v}
are algebraic.

Note that in
Conjecture

6.2
we do not assume the existence of
z\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
such that

z({t})=0,\ {\sf v}(z)=1
. We think that the hypothesis of this conjecture is strong enough to imply the existence of
a function
z\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}^{\rm hol}_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathbb{Q}}},{t}}
algebraic over
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}
.
Conjecture

6.2
implies

Conjecture

6.1
, however, note that the hypothesis of

Conjecture

6.1
is weaker that the hypothesis of
Conjecture

6.2
.

Proposition 6.2
.

The first and second part of
Theorem

6.3
imply respectively
Theorem

1.3
and
Theorem

1.1
.

Proof.

We consider
{\sf T}:=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathfrak{R}}\backslash\{\Delta=0\}\ \times\ \mathbb{A}^{{\sf n}}_{\mathfrak{R}}
with the coordinate system
(z,x)
and the following vector field in
{\sf T}
:

{\sf v}(z)=1,\ \ {\sf v}(x)={\sf A}x.

We have
{\sf v}^{m}(z)=0,\ \ {\sf v}^{m}(x)={\sf A}_{m}x
.
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

Conjecture

6.2
implies
Conjecture

1.2
.

Proof.

Let
x_{1}(z)
be an integral function and
\mathbb{Z}[x]
be the associated integral
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra such that
x_{1}
is the first entry of
x
. We have
\frac{\partial x}{\partial z}={\sf v}(x)
, where
{\sf v}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]^{N}
. We consider
{\sf v}
as a vector field in
\mathbb{A}^{N}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, and hence,
\varphi(z):=x(z)
is a solution of
{\sf v}
. The entries of
{\sf v}(x(z))
are not identically zero, otherwise,
x
and in particular
x_{1}(z)
, are constant and hence algebraic. Therefore, we can choose
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
in the domain of definition of
\varphi(z)
such that
{t}:=\varphi(z_{0})\not=0
. We define
{\mathfrak{R}}:=\mathbb{Z}[t]
, and consider the vector field
{\sf v}
and the
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point
t
in
\mathbb{A}_{\mathfrak{R}}^{N}
. The fact that
\mathbb{Z}[x]
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-integral algebra implies the hypothesis of
Conjecture

6.2
.
∎

6.5
Local-Global principle for vector fields

For a prime number
p\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider the ring
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}:={\mathfrak{R}}/p{\mathfrak{R}}
which might not be a field (
p
might not be prime in the larger ring
{\mathfrak{R}}
) and the reduction modulo
p
,
{\sf T}_{p}:={\sf T}\times_{{\mathfrak{R}}}{\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}}_{p})
.
For a vector field
{\sf v}
in
{\sf T}
, we consider it as a derivation
{\sf v}:{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
and its iteration

{\sf v}^{p}:{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T},\ \ p\in\mathbb{N}.

It satisfies
{\sf v}^{p}(fg)=\sum_{i=0}^{p}\binom{n}{i}{\sf v}^{i}f{\sf v}^{p-i}g
.
For
p
a prime number this equality modulo
p
is
{\sf v}^{p}(fg)=({\sf v}^{p}f)g+f({\sf v}^{p}g)
which means that
{\sf v}^{p}
is a vector field in
{\sf T}_{p}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme,
{t}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
and
{\sf v}
be a vector field in
{\sf T}
with
{\sf v}({t})\not=0
. If the solution
\varphi
of
{\sf v}
through
{t}
is defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
then for any good prime
{\sf v}^{p}
in
{\sf T}_{p}
vanishes at
{t}
. In other words, the subscheme
\check{\sf T}
of
{\sf T}_{p}
defined by the ideal generated by
{\sf v}^{p}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{p}}
contains the point
{t}
.

3
3

3

Inspired by
Proposition

6.4
, we have analyzed the locus
{\sf v}^{p}=0
for the Ramanujan vector field and we have got
Conjecture

4.2
,
Item

1
.

Proof.

We know that
\varphi
maps
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
to
{\sf v}
. This is the same as to say that

(6.2)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf v}}}{{\to}}&{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}}\\
\downarrow&&\downarrow\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm hol},0}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}}{{\to}}&{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm hol},0}\end{array},

commutes. Making modulo
p
we have

(6.3)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{p}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf v}^{p}}}{{\to}}&{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{p}}\\
\downarrow&&\downarrow\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm hol}_{p},0}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})^{p}}}{{\to}}&{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm hol}_{p},0}\end{array}.

Note that we use the fact that
\varphi
is defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
, and so, the above commutative diagram makes sense.
It says that
\varphi
maps
(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})^{p}
to
{\sf v}^{p}
in
{\sf T}_{p}
.
But
(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})^{p}=\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial z^{p}}
is zero in
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}}
.
This implies that
{\sf v}^{p}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{p}})
is mapped to
0
under
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{p}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{A^{\rm hol}_{p},0}
. This is a morphism of
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}
-algebras, and hence,
{\sf v}^{p}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{p}})\subset{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\sf T}_{p}}
. This means that
{\sf v}^{p}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\sf T}_{p}}
vanishes at
{t}
.
∎

Definition 6.2
.

Let
{\sf v}
and
{\sf w}
be two vector fields on
{\sf T}
, all defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. The collinear or parallel scheme
{\sf v}||{\sf w}
of
{\sf v}
and
{\sf w}
is a subscheme of
{\sf T}
given by the ideal generated by

\left|\begin{matrix}{\sf v}(P)&{\sf v}(Q)\\
{\sf w}(P)&{\sf w}(Q)\end{matrix}\right|,\ \ P,Q\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme,
{t}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
and
{\sf v}
be a vector field in
{\sf T}
with
{\sf v}({t})\not=0
. If the image of the solution
\varphi
of
{\sf v}
through
{t}
is algebraic then for all but a finite number of primes
p
,
{\sf v}^{p}
and
{\sf v}
are collinear at
{t}
. In other words, the collinear scheme
{\sf v}||{\sf v}^{p}
contains the point
{t}
.

Proof.

Let
{{\mathcal{I}}}
be the ideal of
{\sf T}_{\varphi}
.
If
{\sf v}
is tangent to
{\sf T}_{\varphi}
in
{\sf T}
then by definition
{\sf v}({{\mathcal{I}}})\subset{{\mathcal{I}}}
, and hence,
{\sf v}^{p}({{\mathcal{I}}})\subset{{\mathcal{I}}}
. This implies that
{\sf v}^{p}
is tangent to
({\sf T}_{\varphi})_{p}
in
{\sf T}_{p}
. Since
{\sf T}_{\varphi}
is one dimensional we get the result.
∎

It is natural to ask whether the converse of
Proposition

6.5
is true. Namely,
let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme,
{t}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-valued point of
{\sf T}
, and
{\sf v}
be a vector field in
{\sf T}
with
{\sf v}({t})\not=0
.
If for all but a finite number of primes
p
,
{\sf v}
is collinear with
{\sf v}^{p}
at the point
{t}
, then the image of the solution
\varphi
of
{\sf v}
through
{t}
is algebraic.

4
4

4

Unfortunately this statement is not true, see
Remark

3.2
.

The following conjecture has been already treated in the literature.

Conjecture 6.3
.
5
5

5

This is a particular case of
Conjecture

3.4

.
Let
{\sf T}
be an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme and
{\sf v}\not=0
be a vector field in
{\sf T}
.
If for all but a finite number of primes
p
,
{\sf v}
is collinear with
{\sf v}^{p}
, then all the solutions of

{\sf v}
are algebraic.

6.6
p
-curvature and vector fields

In this section we discuss the
p
-curvature of connections in terms of vector fields and foliations.

Let
{V}
be a smooth
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme,
H
be a vector bundle on
{V}
and
\nabla:H\to\Omega_{V}^{1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}H
be a connection, all defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. We can regard this as

\Theta_{V}\to{\rm End}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(H),\ \ {\sf v}\mapsto\nabla_{\sf v}.

Definition 6.3
.

The
p
-curvature of
\nabla
is the map

\psi:\Theta_{V}\to{\rm End}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(H),\ \ \psi({\sf v}):=\nabla_{\sf v}^{p}-\nabla_{{\sf v}^{p}},

where we have taken reduction modulo
p
(that is over
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}:={\mathfrak{R}}/p{\mathfrak{R}}
) of the underlying objects.

It is easy to see that
\psi:\Theta_{V}\to{\rm End}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{V}}(H)
, see
[
46
, Section 5]
, that is, the image of
\psi
lies in
{\mathcal{O}}_{V}
-linear endomorphisms of
H
. It is well-known that Gauss-Manin connections have nilpotent p-curvatures.

Theorem 6.4

(P. Deligne, N. Katz,
[
46
]
Corollary 7.5, page 383)
.

Let
X\to{V}
be a family of smooth projective varieties over a field of characteristic
p
and

m=\#\left\{(p,q)|p+q=n,\ h^{p,q}(X_{t})\not=0\right\}.

Then the
m
-th power of the
p
-curvature

\psi({\sf v})^{m}:H^{n}(X/V)\to H^{n}(X/V),\ \

is identically zero for all
{\sf v}\in\Theta
.

Let
s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{n}:U\to H
be trivializer sections of
H
in an open set
U\subset{V}
,
that is, we have an isomorphism

U\times\mathbb{A}_{\mathfrak{R}}^{n}\to\pi^{-1}(U),\ \ ({v},x)\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}s_{i}({v}).

As we are using the language of
{\mathfrak{R}}
-schemes we must say what is this in the level of functions.
Let us consider the section
x\cdot s
, where
x=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}]
and
s=[s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{n}]^{{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{r}}}
and write

\nabla s=dx\otimes s+x\nabla s=(dx+x{\sf B})\otimes s,

where
{\sf B}
is computed via
\nabla s={\sf B}\otimes s
and it is called the connection matrix. It is an
n\times n
matrix with entries in
\Omega^{1}_{U}
.
Let us define the matrix
{\sf B}({\sf v})_{n}
through the equality:

\nabla_{{\sf v}}^{n}s={\sf B}({\sf v})_{n}\otimes s.

We can check easily that it is given by the recursion

{\sf B}({\sf v})_{1}={\sf B}({\sf v}),\ \ {\sf B}({\sf v})_{n+1}={\sf v}({\sf B}({\sf v})_{n})+{\sf B}({\sf v})_{n}{\sf B}({\sf v}).

The
p
-curvature can be written as

\psi({\sf v})={\sf B}({\sf v})_{p}-{\sf B}({\sf v}^{p}).

We would like to reinterpret
p
-curvature in terms of vector fields.
We have the projection
\pi:H\to{V}
which is a morphism of
{\mathfrak{R}}
-schemes and gives the inclusion

{\mathcal{O}}_{V}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{\sf T}
by pull-back of functions via
\pi
.
A section
s:U\to H
of
H
in an open subset
U\subset{V}
is a map
s^{*}:{\mathcal{O}}_{H}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
which is identity restricted to
{\mathcal{O}}_{U}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{H}
. Let
{\sf T}
be the total space of the vector bundle
H
. This is an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-scheme in a natural way and it is just
H
without its vector bundle structure.
We have a submodule
\Omega\subset\Omega^{1}_{\sf T}
which can be constructed in the following way.
In the local chart
\pi^{-1}(U)
,
\Omega
is the
{\mathcal{O}}_{\pi^{-1}(U)}
-module generated by the entries of

dx+x{\sf B}
. We can easily check that
\Omega
does not depend on the trivilizing sections
s
. If we
choose another one
\check{s}
then we write
\check{s}=Ss
, where
S
is a
n\times n
matrix with entries in
{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
. We have
\check{\sf B}=dSS^{-1}+S{\sf B}S^{-1}
,
x=yS
and

dx+x{\sf B}=d(yS)+yS{\sf B}=(dy)S+ydS+yS{\sf B}=(dy+y\check{\sf B})S.

By local description of
\Omega
, it follows that it is integrable in the strongest format, that is
d\Omega\subset\Omega\wedge\Omega^{1}_{\sf T}
. Therefore, it defines a foliation
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
in
{\sf T}
.
Let
\Theta
be the dual of
\Omega

Theorem 6.5
.

(
[
67
, Theorem 6.23]
)

We have a canonical isomorphism
f:\Theta_{V}\to\Theta
which is given by the following identities in local charts:

(6.4)

\displaystyle f({\sf v})(g)={\sf v}(g),\ g\in{\mathcal{O}}_{V},

(6.5)

\displaystyle f({\sf v})(x)=x{\sf B}({\sf v}),

We have used
f
to construct modular vector fields in
[
67
, Theorem 6.23]
.
In geometric terms, this is the lifting of vector fields from
{V}
to
{\sf T}
such that they are tangent to solutions of
dx=-x{\sf B}
.

Proposition 6.6
.

The vector field
{\sf w}:=f({\sf v})^{p}-f({\sf v}^{p})
is tangent to the fibers of
{\sf T}\to{V}
, that is,
{\sf w}(g)=0,\ g\in{\mathcal{O}}_{V}
.
Moreover, in a local chart

{\sf w}(x)=x\psi({\sf v}):=x({\sf B}({\sf v})_{p}-{\sf B}({\sf v}^{p})).

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem

6.5
. For
g\in{\mathcal{O}}_{V}
we have

\displaystyle{\sf w}(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f({\sf v})^{p}(g)-f({\sf v}^{p})(g)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{02102025yanqi-1}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle{\sf v}^{p}(g)-{\sf v}^{p}(g)=0.

In a local chart with
x
-coordinates as above we have:

\displaystyle{\sf w}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f({\sf v})^{p}(x)-f({\sf v}^{p})(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{02102025yanqi-2},\eqref{02102025yanqi-1}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle f({\sf v})^{p-1}(x{\sf B}({\sf v}))-x{\sf B}({\sf v}^{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x({\sf B}({\sf v})_{p}-{\sf B}({\sf v}^{p})).

∎

6.7
Examples of vector fields

In this section we would like to highlight that vector fields can be rich from both dynamical and arithmetic point of view, even though the intersection of these two aspects must yet to be discovered, and for an expert in dynamical system the whole theory developed in the present text might be a useless tool (specially for experts in
Example

6.1
and
Example

6.3
). We have already seen arithmetic aspects of the Ramanujan vector field in
Chapter

4
.

Example 6.1
.

Let us consider the ordinary differential equation in
(x,y,z)
and depending on three parameters
\sigma,\rho
and
\beta
:

(6.6)

{\rm R}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\dot{x}=\sigma(y-x)\\
\dot{y}=x(\rho-z)-y\\
\dot{z}=xy-\beta z\end{array}\right.,

which is called the Lorenz system and and it has a set of chaotic solutions known under the name Lorenz attractor. A simple web search will lead us to its fascinating acpects and we avoid them here. A simple plot of its trajectories in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, for instance for
\sigma=10,\ \rho=28,\ \beta=8/3
, shows its chaotic behaviour, see for instance the
wikipedia webpage for ’Lorenze system’
, and tells us that its solutions are far from being algebraic. Let us take
\sigma,\rho,\beta\in{\mathfrak{R}}
and consider (
6.6
) as a vector field
{\sf v}
in
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathfrak{R}}
which we call it Lorenz vector field.
We have verified that the Lorenz vector field, with parameters as above,
is not
p
-closed for all
p\not=3
and
p\leq 200
, that is,
{\sf v}
and
{\sf v}^{p}
are not collinear at a generic point.

LIB "foliation.lib";
ring r=0, (x,y,z),dp;
number sigma=10; number rho=28; number beta=8/3;
list vf=sigma*(y-x), x*(rho-z)-y, x*y-beta*z;
int ub=200;
BadPrV(vf, ub);

Example 6.2
.

The following vector field in
\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathfrak{R}}:={\rm Spec}({\mathfrak{R}}[x_{2},x_{3},y_{2},y_{3}])
with
{\mathfrak{R}}=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{6}]

(6.7)

\displaystyle{\sf v}:=

\displaystyle\left(2x_{2}-6x_{3}+\frac{1}{6}(x_{2}-y_{2})x_{2}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}+\left(3x_{3}-\frac{1}{3}x_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(x_{2}-y_{2})x_{3}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{3}}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left(2y_{2}-6y_{3}+\frac{1}{6}(y_{2}-x_{2})y_{2}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{2}}-\left(3y_{3}-\frac{1}{3}y_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(y_{2}-x_{2})y_{3}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{3}},

has been introduced in
[
72
]
. It has two invariant hypersurfaces
\{27x_{3}^{2}-x_{2}^{3}=0\}
and
\{27y_{3}^{2}-y_{2}^{3}=0\}
, and it is proved that outside these hypersurfaces
it has an enumerable set of algebraic leaves birational to modular curves
X_{0}(N)
and these are the only algebraic solutions outside the mentioned hypersurfaces.
We have verfied this vector field is not
p
-closed for all
p\not=2,3
and
p\leq 50
,

LIB "foliation.lib";
ring r=0, (x_2,x_3,y_2,y_3),dp;
list vf=2*x_2-6*x_3+(1/6)*(x_2-y_2)*x_2, 3*x_3-(1/3)*x_2^2+(1/4)*(x_2-y_2)*x_3,
-(2*y_2-6*y_3+(1/6)*(y_2-x_2)*y_2),-(3*y_3-(1/3)*y_2^2+(1/4)*(y_2-x_2)*y_3);
int ub=50; BadPrV(vf, ub);

Example 6.3
.

The differential equation

(6.8)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\dot{x}=2y+\frac{x^{2}}{2}\\
\dot{y}=3x^{2}-3+\frac{9}{10}y\end{array}\right.

has a limit cycle which is depicted in
Figure

6.1
. This is related to Hilbert’s 16th problem, see
[
41
,
87
]
. It turns out that only modulo
p=3
,
{\sf v}
and
{\sf v}^{p}
are collinear and in other cases different from
2,3
, these vector fields are never collinear.

LIB "foliation.lib";
ring r=0, (x, y),dp;
list vf=2*y+1/2*x^2, 3*x^2-3+9/10*y;
int ub=100; BadPrV(vf, ub);

Figure 6.1:
A limit cycle crossing
(x,y)\sim(-1.79,0)

6.8
Vector fields on elliptic curves

In this section we recall
Theorem

6.8
and
Theorem

6.6
and we prove that the first theorem is basically equivalent to
Conjecture

6.3
for certain class of vector fields. This is mainly inspired by
[
11
, Corollary 2.5]
.

Let
E:y^{2}=p(x),\ \deg(p)=3,\ p\in{\mathfrak{R}}[x],\ {\mathfrak{R}}:=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{6}]
be an elliptic curve in the Weierstrass format. The vector field

(6.9)

{\sf v}:=y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\frac{1}{2}p^{\prime}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial y}

in
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\mathfrak{R}}
is tangent to
E
, and hence, it induces a vector field on
E
. Over complex numbers, the Weierstrass uniformization
z\mapsto[\wp(z):\wp^{\prime}(z):1]
maps
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
to
{\sf v}
.
The tangent bundle of
E
is trivial and this is the trivializer section of this bundle. Therefore, we can talk about
{\sf v}
for elliptic curves, not necessarily written in the Weierstrass format. For any regular differential
1
-form
\alpha
on
E
there is a unique vector field in
E
such that
\alpha({\sf v})=1
. This is called the dual of
\alpha
. The tangent space of
E
is a trivial line bundle and
{\sf v}
is the trivializer of such a bundle.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over a perfect field of characteristic
p
and
{\sf v}
be a vector field on
E
with
\alpha({\sf v})=1
. We have

{\sf v}^{p}={\rm HW}(E,\alpha)^{\frac{1}{p}}{\sf v},

where
{\rm HW}(E,\alpha)
is the Hasse-Witt invariant. Moreover, if
E,\alpha,{\sf v}
are defined over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
then

{\rm HW}(E,\alpha)\equiv_{p}p+1-\#E(\mathbb{F}_{p}).

See
[
45
, 12.4.1.2]
,
[
44
, 3.2.1]
. Knowing
Theorem

6.6
we can formulate the first density statement for vector fields.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
\mathbb{Z}
and
{\sf v}
be a vector field on
E
without any zeros. The density of primes such that
{\sf v}^{p}=0
on
E/\mathbb{F}_{p}
is
\frac{1}{2}
for CM elliptic curves and it is zero otherwise.

By
Theorem

6.6
we know that we are looking for the density of primes such that
E/\mathbb{F}_{p}
is supersingular. For the proof see
[
31
]
and the references therein.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
E_{i},\ i=1,2
be two elliptic curves over rational numbers. The following are equivalent:

1.

E_{1}
is isogeneous to
E_{2}
over
\mathbb{Q}
.

2.

For all except a finite number of primes
\#E_{1}(\mathbb{F}_{p})=\#E_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
.

This has been proved in
[
80
, IV 2.3]
and
[
35
, §5, Korollar 2]
. Let
E_{1},E_{2}
be two elliptic curves as above and let
{\sf v}_{i},\ i=1,2
be the vector fields in
E_{i}
as in (
6.9
) all defined over
{\mathfrak{R}}:=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{N}]
, where
N
is the product of bad primes of
E_{i}
’s after choosing models over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. We consider their parallel extensions (see
[
67
, Section 2.5]
) in
{\sf T}:=E_{1}\times E_{2}
and use the same letter
{\sf v}_{i}
.

Proposition 6.7
.

Consider vector fields in
{\sf T}
of the form
{\sf v}:=r_{1}{\sf v}_{1}+r_{2}{\sf v}_{2}
with non-zero
r_{1},r_{2}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
.

Conjecture

6.3
for
{\sf v}
implies
Theorem

6.8
.

We remark that
Conjecture

6.3
for such
{\sf v}
’s follows from the main theorem of
[
11
]
.

Proof.

Using Hasse bound
|p+1-\#E(\mathbb{F}_{p})|\leq 2\sqrt{p}
, we observe that for big primes

\#E_{1}(\mathbb{F}_{p})=\#E_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
and
\#E_{1}(\mathbb{F}_{p})\equiv_{p}\#E_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
are equivalent. Therefore, we can replace the second item of
Theorem

6.8
with
\#E_{1}(\mathbb{F}_{p})\equiv_{p}\#E_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
.

Let
f:E_{1}\to E_{2}
be an isogeny defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. We take a model of
f
over
{\mathfrak{R}}:=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{N}]
, possibly with
N
bigger. Let
r\in{\mathfrak{R}},\ r\not=0
be such that
f^{*}\alpha_{2}=r\alpha_{1}
.
We have a vector field
{\sf v}:={\sf v}_{1}+r{\sf v}_{2}
which is tangent to the graph of isogeny and all its translations (for this one may take coordinate system
x
and
y
for
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
such that in these coordinates
f
is given
x\mapsto\tilde{f}(x)
). This implies that
{\sf v}^{p}
is collinear with
{\sf v}
. From another side we have also

\displaystyle{\sf v}^{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{p}\binom{p}{i}({\sf v}_{1})^{i}(r{\sf v}_{2})^{p-i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sf v}_{1}^{p}+r^{p}{\sf v}_{2}^{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{1}{\sf v}_{1}+a_{2}r{\sf v}_{2},\ \ a_{i}:=p+1-\#E_{i}(\mathbb{F}_{p}).

In the first equality we have used
[{\sf v}_{1},{\sf v}_{2}]=0
and in the third equality we have used
Theorem

6.6
and Fermat’s little theorem.
Since
{\sf v}^{p}
is collinear with
{\sf v}
and
r\not\equiv_{p}0
(we might exclude few primes which violates this property), we get
a_{1}\equiv_{p}a_{2}
.

Let us assume that
a_{1}\equiv_{p}a_{2}
for all but a finite number of primes. We take arbitrary non-zero
r_{1},r_{2}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
and our hypothesis implies that
{\sf v}^{p}=a_{1}{\sf v}
. We apply

Conjecture

6.3
and we get an algebraic curve in
{\sf T}
passing through
(O,O)
whose projection to
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
is not constant as it is the unique curve tangent to
{\sf v}
and passing through
(O,O)
. It is also defined over
\mathbb{Q}
because of the uniqueness above and the fact that
(O,O)
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. Note that we can compose the image and domain of an isogeny by multiplication by a natural number map and this produces
{\sf v}
’s with different
r_{1},r_{2}
.
∎

6.9
Poincaré linearization theorem

Proposition

4.4
can be considered as a kind of Poincaré linearization theorem in positive characteristic, see for instance
[
13
,
40
]
. This theorem is mainly stated in the complex context for singularities of holomorphic foliations, and
Proposition

4.4
motivated us to write a generalization of this theorem in positive characteristic. It turns out that in characteristic
p
the hypothesis of
Theorem

6.9
are quite different from the same theorem in characteristic zero.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{p}[[x]]
be the ring of formal power series in
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
and with coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. We define
(\mathbb{A}^{n,{\rm for}}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},0)
to be the spectrum of this ring. This is supposed to mimic a small formal neighborhood of
0
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. We are going to talk about vector fields in
(\mathbb{A}^{n,{\rm for}}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},0)
. These are given by
{\sf v}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\sf v}_{i}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}
with
{\sf v}_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}[[x]]
. The linear part of
{\sf v}
is
{\sf w}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\sf w}_{i}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}
, where
{\sf w}_{i}
is the linear part of
{\sf v}_{i}
. We write this as

(6.10)

{\sf w}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}&\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}&\ldots&\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\end{bmatrix}A\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}\end{bmatrix},\hbox{ equivalently }{\sf w}(x)=Ax,

where
A
is an
n\times n
matrix with entries in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
x=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}]^{{{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{r}}}}
.

Theorem 6.9

(Poincaré linearization theorem in characteristic
p
)
.

Let
{\sf v}
be a vector field in
(\mathbb{A}^{n,{\rm for}}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},0)
with the linear part
{\sf w}
such that
A^{p-1}=I_{n\times n}
. There is an isomorphism
f:(\mathbb{A}^{n,{\rm for}}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},0)\to(\mathbb{A}^{n,{\rm for}}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}},0)
tangent to the identity and sending
{\sf v}
to
{\sf w}
if and only if
{\sf v}^{p}={\sf v}
.

Proof.

Let us write
f
in coordinates
x
, that is,
f^{*}x_{i}=f_{i}(x)=x_{i}+\cdots\in\mathbb{F}_{p}[[x]]
. The fact that
f
maps the vector field
{\sf v}
to
{\sf w}
is translated into

\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial x_{1}}&\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial x_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial x_{n}}\\
\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial x_{1}}&\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial x_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial x_{n}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x_{1}}&\frac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x_{n}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\sf v}_{1}\\
{\sf v}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
{\sf v}_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{\sf w}_{1}(f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{n})\\
{\sf w}_{2}(f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{n})\\
\vdots\\
{\sf w}_{n}(f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{n})\end{pmatrix}.

For
{\sf w}
linear we write this as
{\sf v}f=Af
, where
f=[f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{n}]^{{{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{r}}}}
.

The direction
\Leftarrow
is easy: If there is an
f
such that
{\sf v}(f)=Af
then
{\sf v}^{p}f=A^{p}f=A^{p-1}{\sf v}(f)={\sf v}(f)
. Since
f
is tangent to the identity, that is,
f_{i}=x_{i}+\cdots
, we have
{\sf v}^{p}x={\sf v}x
, and hence,
{\sf v}^{p}={\sf v}
.

For the the direction
\Leftarrow
we set:

f:=-({\sf v}^{p-1}x+A{\sf v}^{p-2}x+\cdots+A^{p-2}{\sf v}x).

It follows from
{\sf v}^{p}x={\sf v}x
and
A^{p-1}=I_{n\times n}
that
{\sf v}(f)=Af
.
The linear part of
f
is
-(p-1)A^{p-1}=I_{n\times n}
.
∎
